Polymarket saw $600 million in election trades after the elections were over
2026-10-16

Polymarket saw $600 million in election trades after the elections were over

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
Prediction market boosters often tout the platforms as barometers of public opinion, or even “truth machines” offering a clearer picture of where things are headed than polling or other data. In 2024, for example, Kalshi beat many pollsters in forecasting the outcome of the US presidential race. But their trading activity can also tell more perplexing stories. Two global election markets on Polymarket’s offshore platform, for instance, generated over $600 million in trading volume, far more than most political markets typically attract. Most of the activity took place buying and selling contracts tied to long-shot candidates who ultimately did not win. This summer, Ethiopian Prime Minister Abiy Ahmed’s Prosperity Party cruised to victory in federal elections, winning 438 parliamentary seats and paving the way for him to serve another five-year term. The closest competitor won 13 seats. On prediction markets, trading volume—the total value of contracts bought and sold—typically tracks roughly with the odds of a given outcome; frontrunners attract far more volume than long shots. But in this case, the trading patterns deviated sharply from the norm. Just $173,000 was wagered on Ahmed winning, compared to more than $41 million traded on his chief of staff, Shimelis Abdisa, becoming the country’s next leader. Altogether, the volume traded added up to over $285 million, nearly all on losing candidates. Even odder? “Nearly all of it—about $284 million—came from pairs of accounts repeatedly buying and selling with each other,” says former Polymarket trader Chris Park, who now runs the prediction-market forensic analysis firm MSR Decode. Such a high volume of seemingly pointless back-and-forth transactions has raised eyebrows among experts. “It’s obviously some form of volume manipulation,” says Rajiv Sethi, a Barnard College economics professor who researches prediction markets. When asked about these markets, Shana Bautista, Polymarket’s head of global intelligence and investigations, told WIRED that the team “immediately flagged this activity, investigated, and blocked the accounts from trading.” Polymarket did not respond to follow-up questions about why the accounts were able to trade such a high amount of volume before it took action. The timing of these trades made things even weirder. Polymarket’s rules stipulated that the Ethiopia market would be resolved in October after the prime minister was formally appointed and sworn in, which meant that it stayed open for several months after Ahmed’s victory was all but a foregone conclusion. For 123 days after the election, bursts of seemingly irrational trading continued, prompting Polymarket users to leave comments like “THIS NOT RESOLVED?” and “fuckkkk.” Sethi calls the phenomenon a “zombie market,” where people can buy and trade on events that have already taken place or are no longer happening. Another market about a 2024 election in Romania facilitated what Sethi described as “even more dramatic” trading anomalies. The initial election was called off due to fears about foreign interference. A new race was held several months later, but instead of closing the original market in the meantime, Polymarket kept it running alongside the new option. It was during this period that Sethi first noticed something weird appeared to be happening with the older market. An algorithm Sethi and his colleagues created to flag potential wash trading on Polymarket identified nearly all of the volume as suspicious. “99.6 percent of volume in this market for certain weeks has been wash trading,” Sethi says. Wash trading is a type of market manipulation in which accounts repeatedly buy and sell assets to create the illusion of heavy trading activity, when in reality, money is simply moving back and forth. It’s banned on regulated financial exchanges in the United States and is specifically prohibited in Polymarket’s terms of service. An earlier study conducted by Sethi and colleagues found evidence of widespread wash trading on Polymarket’s offshore platform. Wash trading could explain what happened with the Ethiopian and Romanian political markets, but because the identities of the traders is unknown, uncertainty remains. “What appears to be wash trading isn’t always wash trading,” says Sethi. MSR Decode ran an analysis of the Ethiopian market for WIRED, which found that the most likely explanation for the anomalous trading is “activity farming,” which is when people try to artificially inflate their trading volumes. The firm suspects that traders may have been interested in boosting their volume because they believed that they could benefit if Polymarket launched a cryptocurrency token. In 2025, Polymarket chief marketing officer Matthew Modabber told the hosts of a crypto-themed podcast that there “would be a token” but did not provide specifics. Sethi also finds it plausible that traders could be motivated by potential “token allocation” to higher-volume accounts. This type of behavior has been observed before in the cryptocurrency industry. “These are textbook examples on how market participants farm a potential airdrop,” says Andre Guettler, the director of Ulm University’s Institute of Strategic Management and Finance, who researches the structure of prediction markets. When MSR Decode analyzed the Ethiopian election market, it found that it was highly unlikely that the trading volume could be attributed to sophisticated traders using algorithms to eke out profits. It analyzed over 38,000 “trade loops” that took place after the election (instances where individual wallets bought and sold to each other in quick succession) and found that over 98 percent resulted in neither side losing or gaining money. The median time that the accounts held onto the contracts they purchased before selling them again was nine seconds. These types of unusual trading patterns are not a new phenomenon on Polymarket. Earlier this year, Barron’s reported that much of the activity on markets predicting the 2028 US presidential election centered on unlikely candidates such as Elon Musk and New York City mayor Zohran Mamdani, neither of whom is eligible to run because they were born outside of the country. Both Polymarket and its chief rival, Kalshi, have been publicly scrutinized for unusual market activity in recent months. Furor over suspicious activity on its recently launched perpetual futures market for cryptocurrency grew so intense on X that Kalshi published a blog post denying allegations of wash trading on it. (Sethi agrees with Kalshi that the allegations were unfounded. He argues that the unusual volume could instead be explained by Kalshi’s incentives for market makers, which create opportunities for other traders to profit from small price discrepancies.) Polymarket recently raised an over $1 billion round of funding led by 1789 Capital, the venture capital firm where Donald Trump Jr. is a partner. The company is now valued at an estimated $21 billion, and earlier this summer, it announced that its annualized revenue was now over $1 billion. Polymarket’s explosive growth is often measured in volume traded, since it’s a metric that can point to how active users are. The Commodity Futures Trading Commission, which regulates prediction markets in the United States, declined to comment on whether it is looking into these election trades. The CFTC previously investigated a number of suspected instances of market manipulation on Polymarket, including several exclusively reported by WIRED, but they usually involved potential insider trading rather than wash trading or other suspicious activity. In the past, the agency has issued hefty fines for wash trading in other sectors. “It seems to me that the CFTC would want to know about weird activity in a very large market,” a former CFTC employee tells WIRED. This story originally appeared on wired.com.

Tesla turns to 50-year-old stunt technology as it tries to Roadster again
2026-10-16

Tesla turns to 50-year-old stunt technology as it tries to Roadster again

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
Nine years after first trying to resurrect the Roadster, Tesla is apparently trying again. CEO Elon Musk might have relocated its HQ to Texas, but yet again he has returned to California’s showbiz industry to hawk his wares; last time it was a Hollywood back lot for the remote-controlled Cybercab demo, last night it was decades-old stunt technology to make a show car do tricks in the air. The new Roadster, we’re told, will be unlike anything else, although the angular, wedge-shaped styling owes more than a little to Giorgetto Giugiaro’s original Lotus Esprit—Musk is known to own a James Bond prop car—as well as the more recent Lamborghini Gallardo. At the rear are four giant exhaust pipes; these do not signal Tesla’s sudden embrace of hydrocarbons beyond those emitted by private aviation. Rather, it’s where 100,000 psi jets of compressed nitrogen shoot out. It’s early in the morning and the Federal Motor Vehicle Safety Statutes are dense reading, so I am yet to find the section where it explains how and when it’s legal to use such a thing on the road. Unusually, Tesla says it stuck with rear-wheel drive for the new electric vehicle. The battery pack uses the troubled 4680 cells it has been trying to scale up in-house; customer complaints of poor performance were presumably the driving force behind developing what the company says is a special battery chemistry just for this model. Some of the performance claims are even more grandiose than nine years ago. Now, Musk claims a planned top speed of 383 mph (616 km/h), with a 0-60 mph time of 0.8 seconds. And as with the last time this happened, there are already other EVs on sale that are more capable than Tesla’s vaporware claims. And right now, that’s the McMurtry Spéirling, which looks like a miniaturized Batmobile (the good Michael Keaton one) and which is capable of generating up to 3.5 lateral Gs. The best Tesla claims for the new Roadster is 2 Gs. Oh, and the Spéirling will most definitely drive upside down. The Spéirling has also set a faster lap time around the Top Gear test track than an F1 car, blitzed the Goodwood Hill Climb record, and I’m pretty sure would demolish Volkswagen’s untouchable Pikes Peak time if it gave it a shot. McMurtry has sold at least 24 of the planned 100-car production run. Musk claims that the Roadster will go into production by 2028. But the CEO has a long and glorious history of overpromising and underdelivering; in 2017 he told attendees at the previous launch that “We are making it now.” Using the company’s referral program, some people were in line to receive two free cars. But those free cars never showed up, and neither did the ones that people put down $50,000 deposits for: last year Sam Altman discovered how hard it can be to get that money back. And it’s not like there’s actually much of a market for very expensive, very fast EVs: hypercar buyers want noise and an emotional connection, and they don’t get that from an EV. Meanwhile, Tesla’s core business remains in trouble. Even in quarters where the company has decent sales, its profits are vanishing as costs rise and selling prices drop. And most recently, sales have not been decent. Its stratospheric valuation was predicated in large part upon promises of constant growth, something that perhaps seemed possible when its marketshare was near total and there was little competition for EVs. But now Europe and China have hundreds of models to choose from—only two of which are offered by Tesla. The Models 3 and Y are both increasingly aged, and Tesla has shown little ability to innovate rapidly like the Chinese brands that have taken so much of its sales away there. In fact, it might even leave the Chinese market. But as Musk has told us time and again, Tesla is no longer a car company. Currently, he seems more interested in merging Tesla with SpaceX, claiming synergies having to do with AI despite most of his AI-derived revenues coming from renting GPU time to others. But a merger would give SpaceX a backdoor into the S&P 500 index, which rejected the rocket company in June, as it requires several consecutive profitable quarters for inclusion. Should Musk succeed, he will have access to billions of dollars from passive investment funds that invest retirement accounts in the S&P 500. Apparently that could happen before the end of the year.

Super Scam Me documentary shows what it takes to catch a romance scammer
2026-10-16

Super Scam Me documentary shows what it takes to catch a romance scammer

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
If you got a DM from an unverified Jon Bon Jovi account after commenting on one of his official Instagram posts, what would it take to make you believe it was really the rock star hoping to connect? And at what point when he starts asking you to send money—like lose-your-house kind of money—would you snap out of the fantasy and stop draining your bank account? In a documentary premiering Saturday—Super Scam Me—investigative journalist Mariana van Zeller goes deep into this toxic love-bombing terrain. In a clip that Ars is exclusively debuting below, you can see how van Zeller worked with an ethical hacker, Arman the Cyber, to use a fake bitcoin receipt and bait the fake Bon Jovi, giving the victim some much-needed closure. Broadcasting on National Geographic at 9 pm ET and available to stream on Disney+ and Hulu, the documentary takes viewers on an empathetic quest to understand how the line between “lover” and “fraud victim” gets blurred in romance scams that the Federal Trade Commission says cost Americans nearly $1.5 billion in losses each year. Often targeting older women with resources who lack community and seek out connections online, such as widows or divorcées, these scams are now a leading form of financial fraud in the US, the FTC has warned. Most victims never get their money back, and overseas scammers are only getting more sophisticated, thanks to AI, Experian forecasted in January. In Super Scam Me, viewers get a chance to go beyond a single victim’s story and see how romance scams operate so effectively to squeeze funds out of the people they target on social media apps like Instagram or dating apps like Match and Tinder. Van Zeller has spent years reporting on the scam industry, and when she saw how many victims were broken and stranded by romance scams, she devised a social experiment in which she became the target. Her plan worked quickly, hooking a scammer in Ghana who began posing as a handsome overseas four-star military general named “Mark Turner.” Relying on a wig, big glasses, and a prosthetic nose to cover her true identity as a journalist (should the scammer try to reverse-image search her profile), van Zeller posed as a middle-age antiques dealer named “Mary Zelle.” She staged her pictures to show she was a fun-time gal living her best single life. Once she hooked the scammer, she used the same tactics that outed the fake Bon Jovi in a scheme to meet her scammer face to face. To track him down, she partly relied on distributing flyers in his neighborhood that included a mashup photo of the supposedly loving couple that “Mark Turner” made to woo “Mary Zelle.” On the journey, van Zeller shared her own complicated feelings about the connection she formed with her scammer as they texted day and night. These insights, along with her work to illuminate a path to track down scammers targeting Americans, are critical. Experts largely agree that ending rampant romance scams means engaging communities to show more empathy and pay attention to the signs. That could meaningfully deter threats to victims, who are also victims of social norms that isolate older women. It’s easy to assume that romance scam victims must be gullible or uneducated, but research suggests that victims tend to be well-educated. In Super Scam Me, audiences can see how trust is slowly built in these scams, how shame prevents victims from getting help, and how simply wanting a relationship to be real can lead smart women like the victim in the Bon Jovi case to bankruptcy. “I’ve spent years reporting on scams, and the reaction I hear most is ‘How could anyone be so stupid? That would never happen to me.’ But it can, and it does happen to anyone,” said van Zeller. “So this time, I made myself the target.” Fearlessly feeling for these women and flying to Ghana to raise awareness of their struggles, van Zeller highlighted in her documentary the tactics and technologies scammers use. National Geographic touts her documentary as exposing the devastating real consequences of largely unchecked romance scams. The one-hour special notably drops shortly after a congressional investigation into dating app scams ended with a finding that scammers are getting harder to catch, NBC News reported. Watching Super Scam Me makes it feel possible to catch these predators, offering victims and society a glimmer of hope. Although van Zeller easily saw through the scammer’s stolen photos from an actual military general’s Instagram profile, her own fake online dating persona was much more convincing. As she ethically avoided sending any money and relied on her hacker friend to keep the scammer on the line, the scammer never seemed to suspect she may be baiting him, instead of the other way around.
Ars Technica - All content
The Surface Laptop Ultra delivers on most of Microsoft's promises
2026-10-16

The Surface Laptop Ultra delivers on most of Microsoft's promises

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
Over the past week, the laptop Microsoft sent me has been summarizing books and movies, generating images from my imagination, and running a 70-billion-parameter AI model that would be a stretch to fit on most laptops. It also played AAA games at a speed and fidelity I would have thought impossible on an ARM system just a year ago. Microsoft wants to sell you one computer for all of your needs, and for once that doesn’t feel like total marketing fluff. Mostly. Microsoft is throwing both arms around the new Nvidia RTX Spark platform with the Surface Laptop Ultra—and setting its sights on the would-be MacBook Pro buyer, or as the company called it, “a high performance audience.” The bet is that rather than a gaming desktop for fun and a development laptop for work, buyers will pay more for one machine that does both. The Surface Laptop Ultra arrives at a strange time in personal computing. Recent industry numbers showed a 20 percent drop in total PC shipments year over year in the third quarter of 2026. That’s not just consumer purchasing either—that’s across the board. Between the RAMpocalypse that has exploded consumer prices and an industry fixated on enterprise money, there hasn’t been much new hardware to excite PC enthusiasts. Microsoft isn’t the only one debuting an RTX-chipped laptop. Dell’s XPS 16 Creator Edition and Asus’s ProArt series (our own Ryan Whitwam‘s review should be appearing on the front page soon) are betting on the chipset as well. The question when reviewing the Surface Laptop Ultra isn’t whether it can achieve the things that it claims competently, but whether there’s finally a compelling reason for power users to switch to an ARM system, and whether their daily workloads can still be achieved without hiccups when compared to a more traditional x86 offering, or the biggest competitor within the segment, the MacBook Pro. With a starting price of $2,599, this is not a budget alternative to Apple silicon. This is bleeding-edge hardware, and you’re going to be paying a premium for it. Depending on your selected processor and memory options, MSRP can reach as high as $5,899. Prospective buyers should note that the lower-specced 24GB unified memory model ships with Windows 11 Home, while the rest of the fleet arrives with Windows 11 Pro. I received the 64GB model for testing, and I think that would probably be the sweet spot for a lot of users to align cost and performance. I really have to wonder if 24GB is enough to go around when we know that Windows 11 is memory-hungry on its own. When you have to assign a significant portion of that 24GB of unified memory to VRAM for gaming, does performance suffer? An audience question inside the Surface Laptop Ultra review information session asked about the usefulness of the base model, and didn’t get much back other than a vague “useful for smaller models locally” answer. Microsoft may have a more definitive answer to that question a few months from now when software and drivers catch up with hardware. So has the Microsoft and Nvidia team-up created the ultimate all-in-one laptop? Or is this the industry once again putting the hardware hype cart before the finished-software horse? The 15-inch mini-LED display might not be enough to get folks to run to the Ultra on its own, but it’s a compelling reason to add it to the list. The 3270×2180 native resolution looks simply stunning whether you’re watching a 4K video, playing games, or just staring at a desktop spreadsheet. I preferred the Enhanced color profile, but Vivid and sRGB modes looked fantastic as well. Blacks stayed deep against bright, vivid subjects without any sort of visible blooming. I wish I could say the same for my TV. While I can’t quantify how it stacks side by side with the latest MacBook Pro Retina display, I can say that this is a damn nice screen. Viewing angles are superb, colors are punchy, and the 2,000-nit peak brightness kept things legible even in a room flooded with natural sunlight. My only gripe is that in bright rooms, there is glare to contend with. The glossy glass looks fantastic at lower light levels but can act a bit mirror-like in direct sunlight. It hindered my ability to photograph the screen more than it did my ability to use the computer. Still, if you’re looking for a matte-screen laptop, you might want to keep that in mind. The keyboard on the Surface Laptop Ultra is exactly what you’d expect on a premium machine like this. It’s excellent, if unremarkable. The keys have a shallow but satisfying press, the spacing makes sense for my hands, and the whole package delivers a very comfortable typing experience. I don’t have a whole bunch of laptops lying around to compare it to, but unsurprisingly, the closest would be my 2020 MacBook Pro. Microsoft knows what market it’s going after here, and I don’t mind them lifting direct inspiration from Apple in this department. The trackpad is similarly competent. It’s absolutely huge, measuring 5.5 inches long by 3.5 inches tall, with excellent tracking accuracy throughout my testing time. Normally, one of the first things I do when plugging in a laptop to work from is to attach an external mouse, but over days of testing, I never reached for the mouse drawer. Microsoft made a point to emphasize the trackpad’s haptic feedback capabilities in native programs during the review information session. I’m not sure if there are still updates left to come that I haven’t received yet, but I can’t recall a time when I noticed additional mouse haptics had kicked in when I opened MS Office or anything. As it stands, the trackpad isn’t groundbreaking, but it is premium enough to be part of an “ultra” laptop. Microsoft describes the included magnetic charging connection and port as being “the world’s first built-in magnetic USB-C charging experience.” It’s difficult to get upset about the marketing department doing their jobs, but this claim struck me as more of a technical truth than engineering feat. Apple’s MagSafe charging debuted on the original MacBook Pro all the way back in 2006. The concept of a breakaway magnetic charger isn’t anything new, but technically Microsoft’s assertion holds water in that it is the first USB-C connector on a laptop with a matching magnetic port, rather than a proprietary connector. Marketing claims aside, the magnetic connection does work well. The port and charger naturally guide themselves together, as magnets do, and made plugging the laptop in to charge in a dark room hassle-free. Normally I’d be holding the cord in one hand while trying to fumble around in the dark for the phone in my pocket and remember the gesture to quickly turn on the flashlight. Now I just have to get the cable within the vicinity, and the magnets do the rest. Perfect for when all I want to do is plug the PC in and go to bed. What’s perhaps the larger selling point for magnetic charging is the cable’s ability to break away if yanked from the PC. Nothing will ruin your day faster than watching a new laptop fly off the counter. With the breakaway cable, you’re getting peace of mind that your laptop shouldn’t meet the floor should a startled cat or curious toddler come into contact with the cable. I’ve never had it happen with one of my laptops, but that doesn’t mean accidents don’t occur. I simulated a few ‘accidental disconnects’ with modest force, and the cable disconnected dutifully as it should. While I won’t say that it’s a must-have, it’s certainly a nice-to-have. And in this package it’s certainly the type of premium accessory you’d expect, but it’s not going to move units on its own—not at these price tiers. According to Nvidia, the RTX Spark N1X is a Grace CPU fused to a Blackwell GPU. Unlike chipsets like AMD’s Ryzen AI and Intel’s Core Ultra series, the N1X has no dedicated NPU—instead it leans on unified memory and the heavy-duty Blackwell GPU for AI work. Traditionally, Copilot+ certification has hinged on a 40+ TOPS NPU, but Microsoft is extending the label to this machine’s GPU-based AI performance anyway (more on that later). The graphics bench tests sing when given access to 40GB+ of usable RAM. These results certainly back up Microsoft’s claims that the Surface Laptop Ultra can handle modern AAA gaming. It just takes some different paths to performance than we might be used to. The AI benchmark was a bit of a miss. I don’t have the ability to run an NPU-accelerated benchmark in Geekbench AI, because the software presumably doesn’t currently have a compatible NPU backend for this unique architecture. Curiously, Windows does list a generic NPU under Display Adapters in Device Manager, but Geekbench AI only offered the ONNX CPU testing path. It’s unclear whether the generic NPU listed by Windows is a virtual device, some sort of driver exposure, or something else entirely. It isn’t possible to get performance metrics on hardware that Geekbench can’t properly see yet. It’s still early days, but it’s the kind of software/hardware incompatibility that you hate to run into when just trying to check out what your new computer can do. The CPU score in Geekbench AI isn’t particularly impressive, and bypassing the Blackwell sort of defeats the purpose of this hardware, so I hope that as the RTX Spark devices start getting out there, we’ll be able to get a better idea of the full potential. Microsoft recommended reviewers try its new AI Dev Gallery app (preview) as a way to test on-device AI. The software is currently in public preview, and users can pull models of their choice directly from Hugging Face for testing or workflow. The project’s GitHub carries a helpful word of warning about running models on ARM64-based Copilot+ PCs, which, according to Microsoft, this is. Or will be, eventually. Confusing, isn’t it? ⚠️ Note: On ARM64-based Copilot+ PCs, make sure to build and run the solution as ARM64 (and not as x64). This is required, especially when running the samples that communicate with models such as Phi Silica. On paper, AI Dev Gallery should have been a simple one-stop shop to test a barrage of different models against the Surface Laptop Ultra’s new RTX Spark setup. In practice, all of the available conversational Phi and Mistral models in the app are CPU-only—no GPU or NPU pathways available, despite the Blackwell sitting there idling. GPU and NPU paths are available for some image generation and embedded model options, but it again leaves me with that lingering feeling that we’re pushing cutting edge hardware just to have something to offer the market. Oh, and it’s worth noting that twice during my testing, I was browsing AI Dev Gallery’s model selection when the program abruptly froze and then crashed entirely back to desktop. There are still rough edges to smooth out. Phi Silica, Microsoft’s flagship on-device LLM and a major piece of the Copilot+ stack, cannot be accessed out of the gate. Accessing the API requires a developer registration with an associated app identity. These may seem like obvious steps for those already in the AI-know, but curious consumers might get frustrated by reaching a gate in what is advertised as a ready-to-run experience out of the box. I attempted to follow Microsoft’s instructions listed in the AI Dev Gallery to run a CUDA-accelerated Phi model, but the process failed. After resolving some command-line issues, pip downloaded the correct Python 3.13 package of onnxruntime-genai-cuda for Windows ARM64, but then failed because the required onnxruntime-gpu dependency doesn’t seem to have a compatible distribution for this environment. Logs indicated the CUDA install path wasn’t usable on this setup at this time. Whether this is a limitation of the current package, an ARM64 packaging gap, or human error, I can’t be totally sure. It was just frustrating to feel so limited in the space where Microsoft told me to play. Frustrated with the current state of Microsoft’s AI Dev Gallery app, I decided to try some testing via Ollama running locally in the command prompt. I downloaded the Llama3.3-70b model. That’s a 43GB model—10 times the size of the Mistral model we ran on the CPU in AI Dev Gallery, with 10 times the parameters (70 billion over 7). If I were Microsoft, this is the type of demo I’d want to be showing off. This massive 70B model would be unlikely to run on any discrete laptop GPU, but the Blackwell still managed 6.26 tokens/sec. The fact that it ran at all is impressive, though I will say this was one of the few times when the Blackwell really seemed to be sweating. System fans were spinning at 100 percent while those Wayne’s World synopses were being written. Credit where due, even at full tilt, the fans don’t generate a lot of sound other than the high-speed pushing of air. When Microsoft speaks of a high performance audience, none is more demanding than the modern high-end PC gamer. I’m old enough that I still find 1080p/60 Hz gaming to look great, and that’s probably for the best, given the current cost of video cards, but those who crave the zero input lag, 4K, ray-traced life are a particular bunch. Whether the package on offer with the Surface Laptop Ultra will be high performance enough for that audience will be up to the individual gamer and their wallet, but this gamer was certainly impressed that these results were coming out of an ARM machine. Microsoft claims 85 percent of its Game Pass library is currently playable on ARM, but I had difficulty trying to find if any titles have a native ARM port, or if they were running in x64 through the Prism lens of emulation. Windows Performance Fit offers a glimpse into how the game might perform on your system, but nothing more. Either way, if it’s the playing of games that you’re concerned with, there shouldn’t be much this system won’t run. The full Nvidia DLSS feature set seems to be available, and ray tracing boxes can be clicked in graphics options. Factor in an HDR-capable display, and the Surface Laptop Ultra is covering a lot of the must-haves for modern gamers. After an initial boot of Gears of War: E-Day that saw a solid 4–5 minutes of shader compiling, I was concerned that this might be an every-time occurrence, but subsequent loads were much faster. This is a game that contains anti-cheat, the latest Unreal Engine, and all the imaginable graphical bells and whistles you’d want from one of your company’s prized franchises. It’s here and running quite well on the Surface Laptop Ultra. While benchmarking, I noticed the system had reported an astonishing 46.7GiB of its 64GiB of unified memory as available VRAM for the game. To their credit, Microsoft has included a warning for consumers who might not be familiar with the new Surface Laptop Ultra architecture: “App compatibility: Surface Laptop Ultra runs Windows 11 on an Arm-based Nvidia RTX Spark platform. Many popular apps run natively on Arm. Apps that are not built for Arm can run through Prism, Windows’ built-in emulator. If you rely on specialized hardware drivers, plug-ins, or older utilities, check app and driver compatibility before purchase.” Multiplayer titles with kernel-level anti-cheat measures may fail or show warnings under Prism emulation due to signature mismatches in system files. Each time I ran Gears of War: E-Day, I was greeted with a warning message when exiting back into Windows. It appears as though the Easy Anti-Cheat was flagging the .NET cache as “modified,” though I couldn’t establish whether Prism’s translation was directly responsible. Beyond gaming, I wanted to see how video encoding would fare on the new hardware. I ran a real-world encode test via HandBrake on the 2160p 60 fps version of the famous Big Buck Bunny. The system processed the 10-minute video clip in 2:27 at 180 fps using x264 encoding via the CPU. That’s the snag. Nvidia NVENC or Nvidia HEVC encoding options are nowhere to be found, meaning the Blackwell’s encode engine still isn’t being exposed to the software. That’s not a problem with HandBrake, it’s just the continuation of a familiar pattern. Hardware decode worked just fine via Media Foundation, so it’s specifically the encode side of things running into a roadblock. According to Microsoft in the review information session I attended, the Surface Laptop Ultra will be certified as a Copilot+ device, but “full software support won’t arrive until late 2026 or early 2027.” The keyboard’s dedicated Copilot button launches the window for M365 Copilot right now, which is fine if you intended to use it for Cloud AI services, but users may be confused at first given the package they were sold. Features like Recall, Click to Do, and the new Windows search bar won’t be available until Microsoft finishes baking them for the Surface Laptop Ultra’s hardware. Participants in the Windows Insider Program will likely see early access before the general public, so if you’re itching for those Copilot+ features, you’ll have to agree to participate and share device information with Microsoft. Your call. Since the Surface Laptop Ultra is meant to be an all-day device that can handle your workload during the day and your gaming at night, there’s an unavoidable question of how much of that you can actually do on the go. With this in mind, I attempted to measure battery levels every 30 minutes throughout my testing and mapped it to a partial day of mixed use. The Surface retained 66 percent of battery life after three hours of varied use. AI-heavy application use will have you running for the magnetic plug, though. That final 30-minute sample was when I ran the llama3.3:70b model twice, and there was a significant drop-off during that period that would likely remain constant so long as you were pushing the GPU. Speakers on the Surface Laptop Ultra are impressive for such a slim device. I unwittingly found myself sitting at my desk bobbing my head along to the Forza Horizon 6 theme music while benchmarks were running. To no real surprise given the size constraints, it’s the low-end frequencies that seem most absent in the overall sound, but that’s not to say there’s anything hollow about the final mix. The package is quite serviceable given that most people are going to be using headphones with the device in an office setting or on the go. I didn’t do any dB measuring, but cranking the volume in my basement was loud enough to get my wife to ask what the heck I was doing from the floor above. Normally it takes reference speakers for that reaction, so I’ll call them sufficiently loud. At no point did I encounter any type of crackles or pops when adjusting volume on the fly, which is always a nice sign that everything is shielded as it should be. The Surface Laptop Ultra is a very capable machine, perhaps even more capable in places than I had assumed. Through clever resource allocations via unified memory and the new RTX Spark SoC, Microsoft has genuinely delivered on the hardware side of its “one device for everything” pitch. When a 70 billion-parameter model can run on this laptop with GPU acceleration and not others, that’s impressive. The Surface Laptop Ultra delivers on the gaming portion of its promises, too: AAA titles running on an ARM machine, complete with the latest bells and whistles, and displayed on one of the nicest laptop displays I’ve ever seen. From the screen to the keyboard to the trackpad, everything feels premium, because it is. The problem isn’t the laptop’s hardware, but everything currently sitting on top of it. Microsoft’s own AI Dev Gallery still feels half-baked, with a limited selection of small models mostly tied to the CPU. Phi Silica, the flagship LLM, is still gated behind developer registration. The Copilot+ features are still to come at a later date, which might be this year or next. None of these are fatal flaws on their own; this thing is still prelaunch at the time of writing after all. But taken as a whole, you start to see a pattern. PCs are being rushed to market with the new RTX Spark chipset so that manufacturers at least have something new to offer for the 2026 holiday season, with software left to play catch-up later on some non-specific date. So who is the Surface Laptop Ultra really for? If you’re a CUDA developer who favors portability and needs to test local AI models on the fly, and you happen to have an extra $2,599–$5,899 in your back pocket, this is genuinely exciting hardware. For everyone else? I’d advise patience. Take a pause to see how the software matures before jumping ship from your MacBook Pro. There’s also an element of PC philosophy to consider. I’m the type of person who enjoys purpose-built systems for specific tasks. A desktop rig for gaming, a mini-PC for office work, a laptop or Chromebook for cloud applications on the road. It’s just how I’ve become wired. “This is my PC for that,” as it were. Though with PC prices likely to stay inflated for at least another few years, maybe Microsoft and Nvidia have delivered a middle ground. If you’re the type of person who really does want everything in one package, then the Surface Laptop Ultra’s multiple personalities may actually suit you. But the math is still somewhat complicated. You’re paying a premium for bleeding-edge tech where not all features are available yet, and having to do so under the umbrella of Microsoft services, with all the live logins and subscriptions that come with it. Has Microsoft and Nvidia’s partnership delivered the ultimate all-in-one PC? Not yet, but they have delivered the first ARM laptop I’ve used that makes the question worth asking—now the horse just needs to show up.

Rocket Report: FAA eyes more Starbase traffic; India's Skyroot snares US customer
2026-10-16

Rocket Report: FAA eyes more Starbase traffic; India's Skyroot snares US customer

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
Welcome to Edition 9.15 of the Rocket Report! The launch I’m most looking forward to in the next few weeks is that of Japan’s Martian Moons Exploration (MMX) mission. MMX is one of the marquee space science missions launching this year, and it’s set to leave Earth as soon as Monday (US time) aboard a Japanese H3 rocket. The spacecraft will visit Phobos and Deimos, the two asteroid-like moons of Mars, deploying a rover and collecting specimens for return to Earth in 2031. Exploration at its finest. As always, we welcome reader submissions, and if you don’t want to miss an issue, please subscribe using the box below (the form will not appear on AMP-enabled versions of the site). Each report will include information on small-, medium-, and heavy-lift rockets as well as a quick look ahead at the next three launches on the calendar. Reflect Orbital looks to India for launch capacity. California-based Reflect Orbital, the startup attempting to offer sunlight on-demand with in-space mirrors, announced the purchase of two launches Wednesday from Indian launcher Skyroot Aerospace, Payload Space reports. One of the launches will use Skyroot’s Vikram-1 rocket, which became the first privately developed Indian rocket to reach orbit in July. The other will fly on an upgraded rocket called the Vikram-1U. Shining a light … Reflect Orbital plans to launch its first satellite on a SpaceX Falcon 9 rideshare next year. The company’s business model involves flying a constellation of some 50,000 mirrors in orbit to beam sunlight to the ground for power generation and illumination. The FCC approved Reflect’s first in-space mirror satellite earlier this year. The company’s selection of India’s Skyroot also shines a light on what satellite operators fear is a coming under-supply of launch capacity in the United States as SpaceX phases out its Falcon 9 rocket in favor of the much larger, but relatively unproven, Starship. LEAP targets smallsat launch market. A Colorado rocket startup named LEAP is aiming to enter the market for dedicated small-satellite launches in 2028 as growing demand for access to orbit creates new opportunities for smaller rockets, Space News reports. LEAP’s Bighorn rocket is designed for an initial payload capacity of 320 pounds (145 kilograms) to low-Earth orbit, with a first orbital launch slated for 2028. Bighorn is sized for dedicated launch services, with a single-engine booster stage and a kick stage named Cottontail for orbit insertion. Rockets of mass production … LEAP says its Bighorn rocket is engineered for mass production. The company’s new factory, located in a Denver suburb, will support initial production of six Bighorn vehicles per year, with capacity to scale to two per month. “We know how to build remarkable vehicles. The challenge is building enough of them fast enough,” said Chris Beckman, CEO of LEAP, in a statement last month announcing the company’s new headquarters. LEAP launched a smaller suborbital rocket, named Bullfrog, for the first time in May, reaching an altitude of approximately 130,000 feet (40 kilometers). Neutron debut delayed. Rocket Lab announced Wednesday that its Neutron launch vehicle won’t fly until 2027, citing an issue discovered during manufacturing of the rocket’s carbon composite structures, Ars reports. The announcement was public confirmation of what nearly everyone inside Rocket Lab and the wider space industry already knew. The most recent official update from Rocket Lab in August suggested the company still held hope of delivering the first Neutron rocket to its launch pad at Wallops Island, Virginia, before the end of this year, a schedule that would have almost certainly pushed the first flight into 2027. Altered carbon … Rocket Lab’s statement Wednesday about Neutron’s schedule suggests the company is still struggling with manufacturing the rocket’s large booster stage. Neutron’s reusable first stage is made of carbon-composite structures, which offer low weight, high strength, and corrosion resistance. But carbon composites in aerospace are generally harder to manufacture than metallic structures. That latest problem discovered in Rocket Lab’s factory involves small air pockets, or voids, amid carbon-fiber layers making up the forward dome of Neutron’s first-stage propellant tank. (submitted by zapman987) Stoke ships second stage for testing. The reusable upper stage for Stoke Space’s Nova rocket has left the company’s factory in Kent, Washington, for what would be the last time, Stoke wrote on X this week. Now at Stoke’s test facility in Moses Lake, the second stage will undergo cryo-proof testing and a hotfire campaign with its Andromeda engine. The milestone follows the completion of “proto-qualification” testing on Nova’s first stage earlier this year. Nova’s booster section is a rather conventional reusable first stage, but the upper stage is what sets the rocket apart. Stoke designed Nova for full reusability, with the upper stage protected by a regeneratively cooled heat shield and powered by a ring of 24 thrusters, an engine collectively named Andromeda. Next stop, Cape Canaveral … The upcoming tests at Moses Lake will be a linchpin for Stoke’s ambition to launch the Nova rocket next year. If all goes according to plan, Stoke will ship the reusable rocket stage to Cape Canaveral, Florida, where it will meet the other elements of the rocket for final launch preparations. The medium-lift Nova is the only fully reusable Western rocket other than SpaceX’s Starship in an advanced stage of development. Falcon 9 launches US military data transport satellites. A SpaceX Falcon 9 rocket carried the Space Development Agency’s fourth plane of Tranche 1 Transport Layer satellites to low-Earth orbit from Vandenberg Space Force Base, California, on Saturday, Aviation Week & Space Technology reports. The launch continues the deployment of the SDA’s network of data relay satellites to support space-based missile warning and tracking and tactical communications and targeting. The SDA has contracts with multiple companies to build the network’s data relay and missile-tracking satellites. This was the first launch of a batch of satellites from Northrop Grumman. Overcoming an abort … That mission was originally slated to launch October 5, but the Falcon 9’s automated systems halted the initial countdown just before liftoff. The launch was then pushed off two more times as SpaceX conducted extra safety checks on the vehicle and worked with the US Space Force to deconflict with other mission deployments. SpaceX completes 35th ISS cargo delivery. On Tuesday morning, SpaceX’s Falcon 9 rocket roared away from Pad 40 at Cape Canaveral Space Force Station to begin a cargo run to the International Space Station, Spaceflight Now reports. The 35th Commercial Resupply Services (CRS-35) mission carried 6,322 pounds (2,868 kilograms) of science and supplies to the orbiting outpost, where it arrived Thursday with an automated docking of the Dragon capsule to the lab’s Harmony module. Bringing the goods … SpaceX’s latest resupply mission delivered two new roll-out solar arrays to augment the ISS power system. The other payloads will support more than 40 scientific investigations in orbit, including a materials science experiment testing a more flexible and durable type of glass in the environment of space. Dragon also brought up fresh food for the lab’s seven-person crew. Let us launch more, SpaceX asks FAA. Federal aviation regulators are reviewing a plan that could allow hundreds more Starship rocket launches and landings a year at SpaceX’s Starbase in South Texas, the San Antonio Express-News reports. The proposal calls for as many as 456 launches and 912 landings a year at the complex outside Brownsville, roughly 18 times the currently allowed 25 launches and 50 landings. Starship recovery coming soon … The giant rockets have an upper stage, named Starship, and a lower stage booster, called Super Heavy. Both sections are expected to eventually land at Starbase, hence twice the number of landings. SpaceX has successfully landed boosters back at Starbase, but it has not attempted the feat with a Starship. That could happen as soon as the next Starship test flight later this year, according to Elon Musk, SpaceX’s founder and CEO. Ship 40 is back at Starbase. In other news at Starbase, SpaceX has returned the first Starship spacecraft to survive an ocean splashdown back to the South Texas launch site for inspection, Space.com reports. The rocket completed a trip halfway around the world in July before descending to a pinpoint splashdown in the Indian Ocean northwest of Australia. Surprisingly, the rocket survived splashdown without exploding or breaking apart, as SpaceX expects Starships to do after ocean landings. The company towed the rocket to calmer waters off Christmas Island, then loaded it onto a barge for the journey back to Texas. Vertical again … SpaceX teams lifted Starship upright after unloading it from its transport ship, moving it inside Starbase’s Megabay hangar alongside a trio of its successor spacecraft awaiting their opportunities to fly to space. Inspections of the flight-proven Starship will “provide first-of-its-kind data on a flown Starship heatshield, Raptor engines, avionics, and more,” SpaceX wrote in a post on X. October 19: Falcon 9 | Bandwagon-5 | Cape Canaveral Space Force Station, Florida | 10:30 UTC October 19: H3 | Martian Moons Exploration mission | Tanegashima Space Center, Japan | 19:41 UTC October 20: Falcon 9 | Starlink 15-15 | Vandenberg Space Force Base, California | 22:14 UTC
Ars Technica - All content
Blue Origin is going all in on the New Glenn rocket
2026-10-16

Blue Origin is going all in on the New Glenn rocket

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
Blue Origin announced a major expansion of its already considerable Florida launch facilities on Friday morning with the intent of eventually flying more than 100 rockets a year from there. The company said it would add two launch pads for the New Glenn rocket, in addition to two already under construction at Cape Canaveral. The company is also investing heavily in a new manufacturing facility that will produce up to 150 upper stages for a larger variant of the New Glenn rocket. The company also plans to procure two additional landing ships to catch and return the first stage of the New Glenn rocket (it currently has one, named Jacklyn). Finally, Blue Origin said it would add a new customer integration facility, built in conjunction with the US Space Force, that will feature four payload bays to process multiple missions at the same time. “These investments are centered on one goal: building the capacity to support more missions, more reliably, and more efficiently,” wrote Ian Richardson, senior vice president of operations at Blue Origin, in an update on the company’s website this morning. “As we look ahead, this infrastructure is designed to support multiple flights per week by the end of the decade. To date, Blue Origin has invested about $3 billion in facilities and infrastructure in Florida, largely to support the construction and launch of the heavy-lift New Glenn rocket. The company now has more than 5,000 employees working in Florida and 500 suppliers in the state. “Our manufacturing and operations footprint has more than doubled in the last couple of years, now spanning more than 20 sites across Brevard and Orange Counties,” Richardson wrote. “Blue Origin is the only company—and the largest employer—to manufacture, launch, and refurbish rockets on the Space Coast.” That would seem to be a reference to superseding the 800-pound gorilla on the Florida Space Coast, SpaceX, which has launched hundreds of rockets into orbit over the years from Florida. Blue Origin has launched to orbit twice. But Blue Origin is expanding its rocket facilities, while SpaceX is pulling back from the commercial launch market with its Falcon 9 and Falcon Heavy vehicles. As Ars has reported, a growing launch crisis is emerging as commercial satellite customers have struggled to purchase a Falcon launch after 2028. This has created an opening for medium- and heavy-lift vehicles that, if Blue Origin can get New Glenn flying successfully and frequently, it is poised to fill. The company’s previous investments, along with the new infrastructure, suggest Blue Origin founder Jeff Bezos is deadly serious about making this a reality. After a promising start, the New Glenn rocket program was stalled in late May, when a problem with one of the rocket’s BE-4 engines led to the catastrophic loss of the vehicle and its only launch site, Launch Complex 36A at Cape Canaveral. Since then, the company has been urgently rebuilding the site at 36A to support a return-to-flight mission for New Glenn before the end of 2026. While this is unlikely, the company appears on track to resume New Glenn launches during the first few months of 2027. Blue Origin has previously announced its plans to rebuild 36A as rapidly as possible, as well as completing the nearby Launch Complex 36B site to support a larger version of New Glenn. This 9×4 variant of the rocket has nine first-stage engines compared to the smaller 7×2 variant, with seven engines. The new launch pads announced Friday morning are likely intended to support the larger 9×4 variant. As Richardson wrote, “These pads will be rapidly sited and constructed by replicating the design and construction approach being demonstrated with the build of LC-36B.” The 9×4 rocket is designed to carry 70 metric tons to low-Earth orbit, and more than 20 tons to trans-lunar injection. The company has not confirmed this, but Blue Origin likely needs the 9×4 variant as part of its plans to launch a human lander to the Moon for NASA as part of the Artemis Program. It could debut in 2028 if development goes well.

A look inside the $52 billion hole that CoreWeave needs to climb out of

CoreWeave—and the vast amount of debt it has taken on to fund its business—dragged down the entire stock market this week. According to its Q2 earnings release, it had operating lease liabilities of $16.3 billion and total debt of $35.6 billion. The company only made $4.6 billion in revenue for the first six months, and its operating expenses exceeded its sales, leaving it with a net loss of $1.4 billion on the bottom line in the period. To put it simply, how will a company that makes no money pay back $52 billion in combined debt and lease commitments? The short answer is that the company has $103.7 billion of “remaining performance obligations” (RPOs), 41% of which is scheduled to be paid to CoreWeave through 2028, its Q2 disclosure states. Those RPOs are “take or pay” agreements that CoreWeave’s customers cannot weasel out of. Not everyone is a believer. “If CoreWeave is counting on Microsoft to honor its contracts and bail them out of extreme debt and losses I have a bridge in Brooklyn I’d like to sell them,” Francine McKenna, the accountant and investigative journalist who has taught at The Wharton School, told Fortune. Suddenly, Wall Street is worried that the U.S. economy is more ‘vulnerable’ than it looks
2026-10-15

Suddenly, Wall Street is worried that the U.S. economy is more ‘vulnerable’ than it looks

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-15

Visit Original Website
Bespoke Investment Group spotted something it did not like in the most recent National Federation of Independent Business survey this week: that small businesses increasingly report their “number one problem” is poor sales. Of all the options in the survey, “the one problem that you don’t want to be your number one problem is poor sales,” the asset manager says. “During prior economic contractions, we’ve seen this reading for poor sales tick sharply higher. As shown below, while still relatively low for now, it has begun to tick higher again recently.” The NFIB index fell to 97.9 in September, from 98.7 in August. “The other major business surveys also generally suggest that activity lost a little momentum towards the end of Q3, likely weighed down by the rebound in global energy prices,” according to Samuel Tombs and Oliver Allen at Pantheon Macroeconomics. (They also note that only 411 companies responded to the survey, down from a level of around 1,200 over a decade ago.) Mark Zandi at Moody’s Analytics thinks the U.S. economy is “vulnerable.” He is especially worried about the labor market: “Monthly payroll growth has averaged around 40,000 over the past year and has been essentially zero when excluding the health care sector. Hiring is moribund, hours worked are very low, and while the unemployment rate remains low, that is partly a reflection of declining labor force participation [from increased retirements and low immigration],” he told Goldman Sachs’ Allison Nathan recently. In addition, he’s concerned that higher rates being demanded by the Fed and the bond market could derail AI capital expenditures. “AI capex is also vulnerable as a higher cost of capital makes investment more expensive, and falling valuations make it harder to raise the capital necessary to sustain the current pace of the AI build-out,” he says. “If rates remain at these levels or higher for longer than a quarter or two, AI could become the part of the economy that breaks.” That scenario—interest rates higher for longer—appears to be shaping up. The consumer price index for September came in at 3.7%, up from 3.4% the previous month and ahead of analysts’ expectations. The Fed may feel compelled to act by raising rates further. “Inflation is cooking with gas,” Jamie Cox of Harris Financial Group told Fortune in an email. “It is abundantly clear where the inflation impulse originates, but two wars need to end before we get any relief. The Federal Reserve made the mistake of calling COVID-era inflation transitory, and now it appears they are set to make the same mistake in reverse on the energy supply shock.” Pimco’s Tiffany Wilding was equally alarmed: “This is not a good data point for the Federal Reserve, whose committee members had been signaling they were not likely to raise interest rates later this month. After the [CPI] report was published, markets priced in a 50% chance that the central bank would raise rates again in October.” Traders decided they hate tech stocks in the past 24 hours, driving a global wave of selling. The tech-heavy South Korean Kospi index fell 8% and at one point triggered a halt in trading. Nasdaq futures were down 1.03% this morning, signaling that the bloodletting may continue after the markets open in New York. CoreWeave lost 15.46% yesterday and sank a further 5.89% overnight; Oracle was down 8.83%, and lost another 1.57% in post-market trading. Chipmakers suffered declines across the board. Counterintuitively, much of the selling came despite some good news for the sector: Chipmaker TSMC reported Q3 earnings today that beat expectations and guided that its capex would increase over the next two years. In addition, Apple stock closed at a new record high, making it only the second company (after Nvidia) to gain a $5 trillion market cap. And Anthropic reported that it was profitable (on an adjusted basis), although its revenue growth ($17 billion in Q3) appears to be slower than previously estimated The drama extended to the bond market, where investors couldn’t decide whether the benchmark 10-year U.S. Treasury was a safe haven or offering a risk premium. Despite making over $200,000 a year, many NFL referees still hold full-time jobs as attorneys, aerospace engineers, and college professors
2026-10-16

Despite making over $200,000 a year, many NFL referees still hold full-time jobs as attorneys, aerospace engineers, and college professors

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
From August to January, NFL officials travel weekly across the country—and sometimes abroad—to oversee some of the world’s most-watched sporting events. But when the final whistle blows, another job is often waiting for them the next morning. Many of the NFL’s 121 officials hold full-time jobs outside football: Clete Blakeman is a personal injury attorney in Nebraska, Brad Rogers is a senior lecturer at Texas Tech University’s business school, and Shawn Hochuli is a California-based financial advisor. The unusual career combinations recently went viral on social media. But these aren’t just side hustles to supplement their NFL paychecks. Referees earned an average of $205,000 a year in 2025, according to Sports Illustrated, on top of a reported 10% increase in per-game pay in 2026. Instead, many officials built careers in other fields before reaching the NFL—and continue to pursue them alongside football. Shawn Smith joined the NFL in 2015, but has also spent the last three decades in auditing. He’s said there are more parallels between the career paths than people might realize. “Most folks don’t want to see an auditor come and most folks don’t care about refereeing,” Smith, who currently works at Blue Cross Blue Shield of Michigan, told Detroit news affiliate WXYZ earlier this year. “But one of the things we do is we have to be objective, we have to be independent, we have to be disciplined in our craft.” That commitment extends well beyond game day. During the season, officials spend more than 30 hours a week studying video and the rulebook, according to the NFL. They also meet with their crews and review evaluations from supervisors, making officiating feel like a second full-time job. Adrian Hill is another example. A 16-year NFL officiating veteran, he also works as a principal software engineer at NASA’s Goddard Space Flight Center in Maryland. For him, the two jobs require very different approaches to decision-making. “With the NFL, you’re making split decisions on the field, it goes by in a flash,” Hill told CBS Baltimore in 2021. “On the spacecraft side, it’s very different. When we make decisions, it’s very measured, we weigh decisions.” But officiating comes with its own kind of pressure. “I feel more pressure on the NFL field,” Hill said. “When the lights go on, we blow the whistle, and the action is live. If you had a heart rate monitor, my heart rate is at the highest. I know people say it’s just a game, but 20 million people are watching, you make the snap decision.” Reaching that level takes years of work. The NFL requires prospective officials to have at least 10 years of football officiating experience, including at least five years at the varsity college or minor professional level. Individuals also have to be in “excellent physical condition” and belong to an accredited football officials association or have previous experience in football as a player or coach. If those boxes are checked, the application process is relatively simple: candidates are invited to mail off their collegiate officiating schedule for the past three seasons to the NFL’s Manhattan headquarters. For Hill, pursuing two demanding careers is proof that people don’t have to choose just one path. “If someone told a kid they could be a professional football player and medical doctor, you’d think that would never happen,” Hill said. “If you have that drive and determination, you can do it. Don’t listen to them. You can go out and do both.” NFL officials aren’t the only ones balancing two very different careers. For some, a second pursuit starts as a passion project. For others, it’s a way to make ends meet. Comedian and actor Ken Jeong, for example, started his career as a licensed physician when he began pursuing comedy on the side.“During the day, I was a doctor. At night, you know, I was a comic,” he told NPR in 2014. “And it was really just to let off some steam. It became like golf, you know, in many ways. Most doctors have golf as a hobby. Mine was doing comedy.” Eventually, he was able to leave medicine behind and pursue comedy as a full-time career. But for many, maintaining two jobs is a necessity. Several U.S. Olympic athletes went viral earlier this year for balancing full-time jobs with the demands of elite competition. Alpine skier Keely Cashman, who represented Team USA at the 2022 and 2026 Winter Olympics, spends part of the year working as a barista in her small California town. Cory Thiesse and Korey Dropkin, meanwhile, became the first American team to win a medal in Olympic mixed doubles curling, taking silver. Outside competition, Thiesse works as a lab technician at a mercury-testing lab, while Dropkin works as a full-time real estate agent. “I feel grateful that I have a job that pays the bills while I go compete in curling,” Thiesse said earlier this year. That balancing act is growing in familiarity for many young workers navigating a difficult job market and rising underemployment—when people work in jobs that don’t require the degrees they earned. A study from EY found that 39% of Gen Z held both a job and a “side hustle.” Preston Fore is a reporter on Fortune's Success team. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. All Rights Reserved. Exclusive: ‘I’ve seen this movie before’: A Michigan-raised ex-Tesla exec raises $45 million to break China’s grip on batteries
2026-10-15

Exclusive: ‘I’ve seen this movie before’: A Michigan-raised ex-Tesla exec raises $45 million to break China’s grip on batteries

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-15

Visit Original Website
Steven Prast is old enough to remember when a mobile phone came in a bag. It was too heavy to carry around, so most people mounted it in the car. Prast argued in a Zoom interview with Fortune that what eventually shrank it into something you wear on your wrist was more the battery than the transistor, holding up his own phone as proof. “What took us from that to this is the battery,” said Prast, a former Tesla manufacturing leader who is now chief operating officer of the battery-materials startup Holyvolt. By his reckoning, lithium-ion technology is at best 20% of the way through its maturity curve, maybe 10% by conservative estimates. The West won’t win the rest of that curve, he said, by fighting with China over the part that’s already finished. “We will not be able to out-China China on the battlefield that China has defined,” Prast said. “They’re the world champions at being China.” Holyvolt is putting $45 million behind the alternative, all in equity. The Stockholm-based startup told Fortune that it has raised the round from Zone II, CC.VC and several angel investors from the defense and finance industries to hunt for battery chemistries China doesn’t control. The round values Holyvolt at $245 million, according to a person familiar with the company. The gap Holyvolt is trying to jump is enormous. China makes about 85% of the world’s cathode active material, the part of a battery that largely sets how much energy it stores and what it costs. It makes about 80% of the world’s battery cells. Its lead comes from scaling the two chemistries that dominate today, NMC and lithium iron phosphate, or LFP. “If we take LFP and we try to out-LFP China, we will lose,” Prast said. “We’ll lose that every day.” The answer, he said in a statement, is to “invent better materials and processes and make them in the West.” He told Fortune he thinks there must be “another dozen cathode materials that humanity just hasn’t figured out yet.” If batteries are so far from maturity, why haven’t they followed the steady, predictable curve of computer chips? Prast’s answer is that Moore’s Law comes out of physics, a science humans understand well enough to solve on paper, and batteries belong to another tradition. “If you design an airplane and you do that computationally, I will fly on that airplane,” he said. “With chemistry, there are still huge mysteries.” “The problem for battery is that it’s a chemistry problem and not a physics problem,” he said. “If it were a physics problem, you’d have Moore’s Law.” That puts Holyvolt on one side of a split in AI-driven science. Most of the field, Prast said, believes it can solve chemistry computationally. A smaller camp believes AI still needs a physical lab to check its work. Holyvolt is in the second camp. “We have to go to the lab and we have to go test,” Prast said. Holyvolt’s lab is Wildcat Discovery Technologies, a San Diego company it merged with in a deal The Next Web reported at $73 million in March, a figure the firm confirmed as accurate. Founded by Scripps chemist Peter Schultz, Wildcat built a high-throughput system that tests thousands of battery materials in parallel. Over 18 years, the company says, the platform has run more than 225 projects for more than 85 customers, including BMW, and tested more than 500,000 cells. That pile of data is the asset Mathias Ingvarsson, Holyvolt’s Swedish founder and CEO, built the company around. Ingvarsson was one of the core drivers behind Tempur-Pedic’s international expansion and its success before and after its IPO on the New York Stock Exchange, and ran its European business for six years after the listing. He told Fortune that, as one merged company, Holyvolt has trained AI models on Wildcat’s results and wired them back into the lab, so each round of real-world tests sharpens the next prediction. The data and the lab underpin the AI models being built to deliver the predictions the market is seeking, as well as to speed up R&D and develop next-gen breakthrough chemistries. Most AI-for-science startups start with models and then look for data. Ingvarsson told Fortune that Holyvolt came at it from the other direction, with “the reinforcement learning we have done for 20 years, which AI needs to actually learn and build on.” “We’re coming from the other side, which is very, very difficult to build,” he said. “Take years and years and years.” Ingvarsson said the lab already works 10 times faster than conventional R&D, based on a case study completed with a customer, but Holyvolt thinks closing the loop with AI will push that figure upward compared to conventional R&D. “I’d go so far as to say that we have cracked the code on how to do it,” he said. The first thing out of that loop is DRX, short for disordered rocksalt, a family of cathode materials that use neither nickel nor cobalt. Prast described it as delivering “NMC-like” performance, “better than or equal to” nickel-based chemistry, at roughly the landed cost of LFP in North America. A core design requirement, he said, was that DRX’s supply chain be “entirely friendly.” He declined to identify suppliers. DRX has published performance data at scientific conferences, including the International Battery Conference in March 2026. The timing includes a built-in deadline. Starting Oct. 1, 2027, Section 154 of the fiscal 2024 defense authorization act bars the Pentagon from buying batteries from CATL, BYD and four other named Chinese manufacturers, even as the military pushes to expand its use of drones. Holyvolt plans to begin commercializing DRX in 2027, starting with unmanned aerial vehicles before moving into mobility. It is also working on formulations for automakers and one aimed at data centers, which need bursts of very high power as GPUs spin up, Prast said. For Prast, the fight is personal. “I grew up in northern Michigan. Long family history in the auto industry,” he said. “I’ve seen this movie before. I know how it ends.” He framed it as a matter of stability more than rivalry. “It’s unstable when you have too much concentration in any one place,” he said. Ingvarsson took a similar line. “I have nothing against China,” he said. Then he noted that Volvo Cars, his home country’s best-known carmaker, “happen to be Chinese nowadays.” (China’s Geely owns a majority of the company.) Holyvolt doesn’t plan to build gigafactories. Prast said the company is open to licensing, toll manufacturing or joint ventures, and would leave industrial scale to partners. “The transition from there to actual industrial scale is the killer.” This report has been updated to remove Fifth Wall from the list of investors from this funding round, due to a disclosure error from the company before publication. Fifth Wall was a previous investor in Holyvolt. Nick Lichtenberg is business editor and was formerly Fortune's executive editor of global news. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. All Rights Reserved. Indonesia’s Danantara pledges $5 billion to homegrown companies amid market uncertainty
2026-10-16

Indonesia’s Danantara pledges $5 billion to homegrown companies amid market uncertainty

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
Indonesia’s sovereign wealth fund Danantara plans to invest $5 billion in Indonesian businesses over the next six to nine months, its chief investment officer said, a commitment made before MSCI proposed downgrading the country to standalone status, a move that has since sent the Jakarta Composite Index down 11.6%. “We’re encouraging our guys to focus on the fundamentals of Indonesian businesses because they’re growing healthily,” Pandu Sjahrir, Danantara’s chief investment officer, told Fortune on the sidelines of the DBS Asia Future Forward Conference in Singapore on Oct. 12. “Why wouldn’t we be buyers of these types of businesses? It’s our job to look at, support and buy great companies.” This comes as the sovereign wealth fund, launched by Indonesian President Prabowo Subianto in 2025, steps up as a stabilizer for the country’s stock market, currently under scrutiny from global index providers like MSCI. On Oct. 15, days after Fortune’s interview with Pandu, MSCI proposed downgrading the country from emerging-market to standalone status. This would remove Indonesian stocks from MSCI’s emerging and frontier-market indices and prompt investment funds to consider reducing their exposure to the country. The country has until April 2027 to enact reforms. (Indonesia recently preserved its Secondary Emerging status in FTSE Russell’s annual count) That news sparked an 11.6% plunge in the benchmark Jakarta Composite Index. The index is now down just over 40% for the year—showing just how much Indonesia still needs to do to convince global investors to back its companies, and why Danantara is stepping in. “Once there’s a shift in perception, even if the fundamentals are good, the stocks will see a downturn,” Pandu admitted. “Overall, Indonesian consumer stocks are down over 20%, and that isn’t even as bad as those related to mining or energy.” Pandu, who made his name in the private sector, now has to focus on different metrics than a straightforward return on investment. “The idea is to create economic value, and not necessarily commercial value first—that’s an important distinction,” Pandu explained. “If the projects serve as a significant economic multiplier and remain within the cost of capital range, we’ll most likely fund it.” Danantara has four arms: asset management, investment management, the Danantara Development Management Fund (DDMF) and Danantara Resources Indonesia. The fund’s asset management wing supervises and corporatizes more than 1,000 of Indonesia’s state-owned enterprises, while its investment management segment seeks to maximize returns via bonds, public markets, and domestic and foreign equities. The DDMF is Danantara’s arm with the strongest social mandate. It deploys long-term capital toward national infrastructure projects with high social yield but lower immediate commercial returns. Among them is the Giant Seawall Project, a multi-segment complex along the Northern coast of Java set to take up to 20 years to build, and a coal gasification program. A fourth unit—Danantara Resources Indonesia—fully launches next January. In Pandu’s telling, it’ll serve as a single digital platform that centralizes the exports of Indonesia’s natural resources, starting with its top three: coal, palm oil and ferroalloys. (The country is the world’s top exporter of nickel products, contributing over 60% of global supplies, according to Reuters.) “We’ll be using a lot of technology to ensure we do the reporting well,” Pandu said. “We’ll try to do those three [commodities] really well first, and then we’ll go from there.” Yet the snap decision to have Danantara take over exports—announced by Prabowo in May—has unnerved analysts who worry about encroaching state control of the economy, as well as at the sovereign wealth fund. “Under normal conditions, investors might take more time to assess the policy details,” Dedi Dinarto, the associate director at strategic advisory FGS Global, told Nikkei Asia in May, when the proposal was first announced. “But when the rupiah is under pressure, and the equity market is already weak, introducing a new layer of state control mechanism over strategic assets can quickly become a confidence issue.” Pandu wants Danantara to double down on partnerships and human capital next year. On Oct. 6, Danantara announced it was investing $250 million in HighPeak Energy, a Texas-based oil and gas producer. “The collaboration is a way for us to learn about shale technology, which isn’t often seen in Indonesia,” Pandu said. “What we want to do is to learn about new technologies, how to de-risk them, and bring that knowledge back.” “If we are able to have some good downside protection, we get paid to learn,” he added. The sovereign wealth fund is also making strategic hires. In 2025, Danantara hired two executives from Singapore’s GIC, Daniel Lim and Weihan Wong, to lead its global private market coverage. Danantara now has around 500 employees. “The way we look at it—at least during my time—is how we can bring the best human capital [to Indonesia], and encourage the transfer of knowledge for the benefit of Indonesians,” Pandu concluded. And despite the challenges of running a sovereign wealth fund in a country under scruntiny, Pandu said he still gets “butterflies” at work. ”I wouldn’t trade it for the world, man.” Angelica Ang is a Singapore-based journalist who covers the Asia-Pacific region. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. Italy’s air traffic capacity was fully restored on Friday after overnight problems hit the data transmission network supporting air traffic control services nationwide, air navigation company ENAV said.
