€10m Shared Island Digital Creative Industries Innovation Programme launched
2026-10-16

€10m Shared Island Digital Creative Industries Innovation Programme launched

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
The initiative aims to strengthen research and innovation in disciplines related to digital creative industries. The Irish Government has put out a call today (16 October) for potential applicants to submit research and innovation projects to the Shared Island Digital Creative Industries Innovation Programme. These projects must demonstrate a clear contribution to digital creative industries on an “all-island basis”, according to the call. The initiative – which will run from 2027 to 2030 – has an allocated budget of €10m which will be available to succesful applicants. The budget comprises €6m from the Department of Further and Higher Education, Research, Innovation and Science, and €4m from the Government’s Shared Island Fund. The programme aims to support higher education institutions across Ireland as they strengthen their research and innovation capabilities in disciplines related to digital creative industries – including video game development, immersive technologies such as VR and AR, animation, and other forms of digital media and arts. The application portal will open in the coming weeks and the deadline for applications is 12pm, 15 December 2026. The successful projects are expected to be selected and announced early next year. Commenting on the initiative, Taoiseach Micheál Martin, TD said: “The Shared Island Digital Creative Industries Innovation Programme will create new opportunities for higher education institutions across the island to combine their expertise and develop ambitious research and innovation partnerships. The most actionable data to drive superior results Join a culture that offers a world of possibilities “Higher education institutions north and south have considerable expertise and research infrastructure in the digital creative industries. By bringing together their complementary capabilities, this programme will support collaboration at a greater scale and strengthen the island’s international position in these rapidly developing sectors.” Minister for Further and Higher Education, Research, Innovation and Science James Lawless, TD said that Ireland’s digital creative industries offer opportunities for economic growth, innovation and high-quality employment. “To realise that potential, we need to continue building the research expertise, innovation capacity and industry engagement of our higher education institutions,” he said. He added that it will complement other significant investments, such as the existing North-South Research Programme and the €750m Inspire Research Infrastructure and Talent Programme. “Together with our wider research and innovation initiatives, these programmes are building the expertise, partnerships and infrastructure needed to sustain an ambitious and internationally competitive research system.” Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. FCAT and looking at what’s possible, not what is already in place
2026-10-16

FCAT and looking at what’s possible, not what is already in place

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
Fidelity Investments’ Richard Murphy discusses the work carried out by FCAT and the importance of considering what can be made real if you are curious and determined enough. Richard Murphy is the vice-president  and head of FCAT Europe at Fidelity Investments, where he oversees the company’s ‘innovation catalyst’, explores new frontiers, analyses societal shifts and tests emerging technologies. All as a means of redefining what is next for the financial sector “and beyond”. Murphy told SiliconRepublic.com, “If we can predict the outcome, FCAT should not be involved. We work in the grey space. We work with the art of the possible. We pull together ideas. There’s a lot of creativity and imagination involved in what we do. We start small and it’s always about what could happen.” He explained that the Fidelity Centre for Applied Technology, or as it is more commonly known FCAT, is Fidelity’s speculative innovation wing, where a small team pays particular attention to “what’s over the horizon”. Murphy said, “So, we look at what could be possible rather than what’s already in place. That allows us to both identify an opportunity, experiment with that and then scale it out.” The purpose, he explained, is to enable Fidelity to propel itself forward in a world that is under constant transformation, but that often means challenging how we address the processes we use today. “That start-up mindset is very much a part of our DNA. There’s a lot of creativity and imagination involved in what we do, but we start small and it’s always about what could happen. It’s not about solving a clearly articulated problem. It’s identifying a new, often hazy, opportunity that might lead to the next big thing.” Creating the future can often mean putting in the work today. With that in mind Murphy, who holds a strong personal belief that innovation is a “contact sport”, explained that FCAT is highly active within Ireland’s start-up community. Noting it isn’t just with start-ups, he said, “We also work very actively with academia. We work with other companies. We work across multiple different industries. And that’s about bringing together multiple parts of our ecosystem here in Ireland to help elevate the country and help elevate innovation in the region.” And it is a diverse team that enables FCAT to create a cross-collaborative ecosystem of innovation. Murphy said, “We hire everything from really, really deep-tech engineers, a lot of PhDs in areas like quantum computing and blockchain and so on, but also artists, a lot of design thinkers, a lot of human experience designers. And that friction that you get from different perspectives in the same conversation is a huge part of what we use to unlock opportunity.” So, how do you innovate for a future in which the answers and sometimes even the questions being asked are unclear? Murphy said, “Technology is always moving and it’s important that we don’t remain rigid, that we have our eye on what’s coming next all of the time. We have very much a learning mentality and we are very much about experimenting with what’s possible.” This commitment to a mentality of curiosity, that challenges the status quo, is for Murphy a critical common trait often found in FCAT’s associates, who prioritise continuous learning and improvement. “You can’t afford to stay still. That’s comfort. Asking ‘but why?’ and ‘why not?’ are important traits that we encourage everywhere in the firm. That ability to challenge the boundaries and push forward is encouraged.” Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Leah Fletcher. Image: Orla Murray/Coalesce Fletcher is interested in how cannabinoids can be regulated and used in pharmaceutical contexts, she told SiliconRepublic.com earlier this year. The Founder of the Year Awards 2026 took place at the Salesforce Tower in Dublin. Dublin’s Lios among top 10 in the European Start-up Prize for Mobility

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-16

From left: Eimear O'Carroll and Rhona Togher. Image: Johnny Savage The start-up was selected from more than 430 other applicants spread across the continent. Lios, a Dublin-based deep-tech start-up, has been named as one of the top 10 winners of the seventh European Start-up Prize for Mobility (EUSP). Backed by the European Parliament, the event took place in Luxembourg with the goal of connecting sustainable mobility start-ups with European institutions, investors and industry. The company was selected from more than 430 other applicants across the continent. Initially established as Restored Hearing in 2009 by Rhona Togher and Eimear O’Carroll, Lios is the name behind SoundBounce, a compact acoustic material designed to reduce noise and vibration in applications including vehicles and aircraft. Lios’ current area of focus is on sound and vibration control for electric vehicles, aerospace designs and urban transit systems, as a means of reducing noise pollution and improving performance, safety and passenger wellbeing. Commenting on the win, Togher, who is also the CEO, said: “European mobility is undergoing a massive transition, and quiet, efficient technologies are central to that evolution. “Standing on stage in Luxembourg alongside such visionary founders reinforced the strength of Europe’s mobility ecosystem. We are honoured by the recognition from the EUSP evaluation team and look forward to the next steps of the European Tour.” Dan Sobovitz, the managing director of the European Startup Prize for Mobility, added: “This year’s selection reminds us how much mobility innovation happens behind the vehicle, in battery materials, energy production, infrastructure planning and everyday operations. “Our role is to connect these teams with the public institutions, industrial partners and investors who can help bring their solutions into use.” As part of the winners cohort, Lios will take part in an accelerator programme powered by TUM Venture Lab Mobility and participate in an upcoming EUSP European Tour, with locations set to include Barcelona, Spain and Le Mans, France. Earlier this year, Lios renewed its contract with the European Space Agency (ESA) for a second time to deploy its sound technology for space travel. The co-funded contract was valued at almost €2m. Proofpoint bets $6.3bn on Varonis as AI agents drive data risks
2026-10-16

Proofpoint bets $6.3bn on Varonis as AI agents drive data risks

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
Proofpoint plans to move quickly after closing to integrate the two companies’ capabilities, as demand grows for cybersecurity that covers AI agents as well as people. Proofpoint has agreed to acquire Miami-based cybersecurity player Varonis Systems for $6.3bn in a bid to create a stronger offering against growing AI-powered security threats. Under the all-cash deal, Varonis shareholders will receive $49.75 a share, a 52pc premium on the company’s closing share price on 22 June, the last full trading day before rumours of a potential sale first emerged. Varonis provides a data security platform that gives organisations automated visibility and control over their critical data, and it pitches itself as a company that secures AI and the data behind it. Proofpoint itself was acquired by software investment firm Thoma Bravo in 2021, at which time it was taken private, in a deal valued at around $12bn. Varonis will also now be taken private and will delist from the Nasdaq once the transaction closes. Thoma Bravo is providing committed equity financing, and has secured debt financing commitments to fund the purchase, the companies said, in what they said will create “a new cybersecurity powerhouse to deliver a complete security platform that protects against AI-accelerated threats and data risks”. Ontario Teachers’ Pension Plan has also agreed to invest in Proofpoint as part of the transaction. The combined company will serve more than 16,000 enterprises, and Proofpoint said it intends to move quickly after closing to integrate the two companies’ capabilities and align their go-to-market efforts. Sumit Dhawan, CEO of Proofpoint, said the move is a “defining step in Proofpoint’s evolution into a cybersecurity platform built for the future of AI”. “As AI becomes embedded across the enterprise, the data it can access determines both its value and its risk,” said Dhawan. “AI agents are increasingly accessing sensitive information and taking action across systems, making it critical for organisations to understand not only what data needs protecting and who can access it, but how it is actually being used. “By connecting communications, identities, permissions, data, and AI, we are creating an intelligence advantage that helps customers see risk more clearly, respond faster and defend against the next generation of AI risks.” “Our mission is to secure AI and the data that powers it. By joining forces with Proofpoint, we can achieve this mission faster than ever,” said Yaki Faitelson, co-founder, CEO and chair of Varonis. The transaction has been unanimously approved by the Varonis board and is expected to close in the first half of 2027, subject to the usual closing conditions, including approval by Varonis shareholders and regulatory approvals. Until then, Proofpoint and Varonis will continue to operate as separate, independent companies. Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Meta unveils Muse Spark Open to compete with Chinese open weights
2026-10-16

Meta unveils Muse Spark Open to compete with Chinese open weights

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
The multimodal model has 816bn total parameters, with 42bn active parameters and a 1m-token context window. Meta has unveiled Muse Spark Open, calling it the “most capable” US open-weight model to date. The company said that the new model is the first in its class to be able to run on a desktop computer using two of Apple’s M5 Ultra Mac Studios with its latest machine learning framework. The multimodal model has 816bn total parameters, with 42bn active parameters and a 1m-token context window. According to the company, it is built for coding, agentic work and other complex, multi-step reasoning tasks. Unlike their closed-weight contemporaries, developers can download parameters of an open-weight model and modify them for their needs. Open-weight models, however, are not the same as open-source models that also provide access to training data and code. Meta said that the model weights are available for download via Amazon SageMaker AI, Google Cloud Model Garden, Hugging Face and Microsoft Foundry Models. The model is available through popular API platforms including Groq, OpenRouter and Baseten. China currently leads the open-weights space, with models developed by Chinese makers such as Xiaomi, Z AI, Kimi, Alibaba and DeepSeek routinely ranking above their US counterparts in Artificial Analysis’ ranking. Muse Spark Open has had additional post-training safety, the company said. It evaluated the model across its “most severe risks” and found that it met the thresholds set by Meta. Open-weight models are considered necessary for AI leadership, as being able to tweak a model means organisations, from start-ups to public institutions, can build on advanced models without training one from scratch. Earlier this month, France’s AI darling Mistral announced its latest open-weight AI model Mistral Large 4, which it said “significantly outperforms” any open-weight model developed in the US or Europe. Known as ‘Le Chonk’, ML4 is a 1trn-parameter natively multimodal model with 49bn active parameters, and Mistral’s “largest and most capable model to date”. Vaping Strongly Linked to Lung Cancer
2026-10-16

Vaping Strongly Linked to Lung Cancer

Futurism | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
If you’re one of the over 100 million people around the world who regularly hits the vape, you might want to think about ways to start cutting back. According to some recent research by scientists from Seoul National University and published in Nature Medicine, the use of non-tobacco devices like vapes and e-cigarettes to help quit smoking carries a strong link with the development of lung cancer. The researchers analyzed anonymous health data from over 4.5 million Korean adults. They were split into three main groups: current smokers, short-term quitters, and long-term quitters. They were then categorized by whether they vaped daily during a 2018 screening in the Korean National Health survey, with adjustments to control for factors like age, sex, and prior health conditions. In all, scientists found that those who had turned to vaping after quitting traditional cigarettes developed lung cancer at a rate about 56 percent higher than those who quit entirely. Alarmingly, incidents of lung cancer death among vape users nearly doubled, with a lung cancer-specific death rate 91 percent higher than non-vape users. Of course, there’s an important group that falls outside the bounds of the study: anyone who got hooked on vaping without trying cigarettes first. It’d be fascinating to learn more about their fates, but the implication of the study — that vaping is turbocharging the chances of cancer among former smokers — is almost certainly cause for alarm. While it may be easy for non-smokers to roll their eyes at the findings, it’s important to note that empirical data on the relationship between vaping and lung cancer remains relatively scarce. “Although epidemiological and biological studies support the association between e-cigarettes and various respiratory disorders, evidence regarding the relationship between e-cigarettes and lung cancer risk remains limited,” the researchers wrote. It’s an observational study, meaning that it can’t establish true causality. Going forward, more research is needed to say for sure whether there’s a causal association between vaping and cancer — but the early data doesn’t look pretty. Tesla’s New Flying Death Trap Looks a Lot Like a Cybertruck
2026-10-16

Tesla’s New Flying Death Trap Looks a Lot Like a Cybertruck

Futurism | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
Tesla CEO Elon Musk has been promising a next-generation Roadster for the better part of a decade. And while early promises that it will quite literally fly were met with incredulity back in early 2019, the EV maker has finally made good on its promise. As demonstrated late Thursday evening, the new Roadster can indeed fly — briefly, at least — thanks to a “SpaceX package” made up of cold-gas thrusters. The company showed the company flipping around on stage, rotating on its own axis mid-air, leaping over the company’s entire EV lineup, and sticking to a vertical ramp thanks to a fan system that generates up to two Gs of downward force. Good luck doing any of that on a public road, or even surviving on private property. And the flying car bears a striking resemblance to the company’s controversial Cybertruck, featuring extremely sharp body lines and flat panels. It’s a low-poly design approach that feels intimately familiar after a few years of the brutalist pickups. And the similarities aren’t limited to the overall shape, either. The Cybertruck was and still is a major outlier in the automotive landscape — mostly for the worse. From rusting stainless steel body panels that were shoddily glued to the vehicle’s exterior and an excessive curb weight that greatly reduced range to a buggy steer-by-wire system, the Cybertruck was a major departure from design norms in the industry, much like Tesla’s latest Roadster. While we don’t know if the Roadster will suffer from the same issues, its cold-gas thruster system is more than likely going to require some additional upkeep. Besides, considering the system can easily throw the car high into the air, anything going wrong could put any driver in severe danger. After all, Tesla has a long history of treating its customers as guinea pigs. As Musk admitted during Thursday’s event, the bizarre rocket on wheels isn’t exactly going to be the next Honda Civic. It definitely won’t “move the needle in a major way financially,” the billionaire cautioned the audience, calling the Roadster a “small potato situation.” As such, Tesla investors were left unimpressed, with shares bumping by less than two percent when the markets opened. In other words, much like the Cybertruck, the latest Roadster is more tech demo than an actual consumer-facing product, a harsh lesson owners of the former are still learning as their vehicles continue to age like milk. And whether we’ll ever see it gliding along mid-air at highway speeds on public roads is as unclear as ever. Tesla is looking to sell a thruster-less Performance variant, a “Race Ready” mid-tier version with rear-mounted cold-gas thrusters, as well an entirely not-road-legal and “invite only” model that can fly, as shown off on stage. Even more so than the Cybertruck, the Roadster is the ultimate Tesla loyalty test. While the company has yet to release a price, the Roadster is undoubtedly going to cost a pretty penny — if you can even get your hands on one. Whether it will appreciate, like Musk has long promised about the company’s fleet, or turn into an expensive death trap after a short and likely terrifying stint of ownership, remains to be seen. Perhaps it’s more of an attempt to warm up investors to the idea of merging Tesla and SpaceX, a high-stakes gamble that has investors unsettled. These Supposedly Super Secure Ledger Blockchain Wallets Are Secretly Draining User’s Crypto Holdings
2026-10-16

These Supposedly Super Secure Ledger Blockchain Wallets Are Secretly Draining User’s Crypto Holdings

Futurism | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
When your company’s bread-and-butter is selling crypto devices advertised as having “industry leading” security, you better keep a close eye on your supply chain. That’s a hard lesson learned by Ledger, a company specializing in secure blockchain wallets, after news broke that some of its devices had been infiltrated by a mysterious “hardware implant.” According to Bleeping Computer, the malicious implant was designed to monitor inputs made onto each wallet via on-board buttons, along with changes in output on the devices’ e-ink screens. By monitoring this data, the hardware implant could reportedly identify the use of a seed phrase — a sequence of words used to generate static wallet addresses on the blockchain — which it would then transmit to hackers using an eSIM. As a result of the implants, a number of Ledger users had their crypto wallets drained, prompting an investigation by the company. After probing a number of devices, Ledger concluded that the compromised wallets originated from an electronics reseller out of Malaysia named CryptoBilis. Though it remains to be seen where CryptoBilis obtained the hacked devices, Ledger put out a statement recommending customers avoid purchasing and activating any of its hardware purchased from the reseller. “As a precaution, and pending the results of our investigation, we have asked CryptoBilis to pause all sales and shipments of Ledger devices,” the company warned in a statement on X-formerly-Twitter. Though Ledger stated that they “have no indication that Ledger’s security infrastructure, systems or services have been compromised,” the interception does raise some crucial questions around the security of the devices and Ledger’s oversight of its hardware supply chain. If a supposedly world-beating crypto wallet can be easily cracked to drain users’ wallets, how can customers trust any supplier — CryptoBilis or otherwise — to sell secure devices? AI Experts Already Doomsday Prepping for When AI Takes Out the Entire Financial System
2026-10-16

AI Experts Already Doomsday Prepping for When AI Takes Out the Entire Financial System

Futurism | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
Powerful AI models are going on hacking sprees — or helping humans with them — left and right. If the AI industry doesn’t get its collective act together and stop its products from being used to such dangerous ends, it could be only a matter of time before one of these AI-powered hacks targets our essential infrastructure. This is a scenario that some AI researchers are apparently already preparing for. Andreas Stuhlmüller, CEO and cofounder of the AI research company Elicit, recently rattled off a prepper-esque checklist of how to prepare for when AI-powered cyberattacks could take out financial systems, power grids, the internet, and all the other infrastructure that underpins modern life. “Cash: Get a few weeks of spending in cash,” he recommended in a tweet. Then he suggests, in greater detail than we’ll get into here, getting a Starlink terminal in case your local internet network goes down; a battery, generator and solar panels for power; weeks worth of water and food; and a hand-crank emergency radio. Stuhlmüller’s paranoid recommendations come after a shocking cyberattack that targeted the refrigerator systems used in hundreds of grocery stores across France. The as-yet unknown perpetrators cranked up the fridge temperatures up to 122 degrees Fahrenheit, spoiling food and even causing fires. The CEO of one chain targeted by the attack, Intermarche, said it caused losses amounting to “tens ​of millions of euros nationwide in a single day.” AI’s role in the attack hasn’t been confirmed. But given AI’s ubiquity among coders, it’s likely that it was used to some degree. On top of churning out code, the tech is alarmingly good at identifying vulnerabilities. A recent CrowdStrike report found that there was a nearly 90 percent year over year increase in AI-enabled cyberattacks. There’s also, of course, the recent disclosures from frontier AI labs like OpenAI, Anthropic, and Meta, over how their agentic AI systems broke out of their testing environments and launched cyberattacks against other companies’ websites and computer systems. These incidents represent an alarming escalation in the threat AI poses to cybersecurity systems, not only assisting hacks but carrying themselves autonomously while also avoiding the detection of their human overseers. All that being said, it’s a major leap to say that AI models — or humans using them — will go from hacking websites and supermarket fridges to your local power grid and banking system. But we can hardly blame anyone for being worried about this possible eventuality. Better safe than sorry, right? Residents Reject $10,000 Each to Let a Data Center Set Up in Their Town

Futurism | 2026-10-15

Visit Original Website
In the American psyche, data centers have become so reviled that big tech can’t even pay residents life changing money to welcome the facilities into their backyards. That’s not an exaggeration: voters in the former coal-mining community of Hazle Township, Pennsylvania are mobilizing en masse against a massive Amazon Web Services facility that would come along with a $10,000 direct cash dividend to every resident. That’s according to reporting by PennLive, which notes that nearly 17 percent of the township’s residents live below the federal poverty line. Despite the possibility of a $10,000 payout, residents are organizing to reject both the data center campus itself — named “Project Hazelnut” by the developer NorthPoint Development — and a lengthy stretch of power lines meant to supply power to the facility. As PennLive observes, the $10,000 scheme is an unusual attempt by a data center developer to smooth over opposition in a section of the country which is overwhelmingly opposed to the facilities. “We think it’s creative. We’ve not seen (anything like) this,” executive director of the Pennsylvania Ethics Commission Mary Fox told PennLive of the strategy. Yet residents of Hazle Township aren’t swayed by the prospect of fast cash, viewing it as a payoff, and a weak one at that. “It’s like bribery,” as local resident Brian Malloy told PennLive. “Our property values are going to drop. Dramatically,” said Ed Negra, a homeowner and small business owner who characterized the $10,000 as a drop in the bucket compared to the cost of allowing Project Hazelnut to go forward. “If that’s not a bribe, I don’t know what is.” The matter is far from settled, though it doesn’t help that both state and local officials are acting shifty. Pennsylvania governor Josh Shapiro, for example, has previously characterized the project as a “technology campus,” using an executive order to fast-track the process by which developers can erect data center facilities across Pennsylvania. Meanwhile, PennLive reports that Hazle Township supervisor Anthony Griguoli has struggled to present a united front with NorthPoint Development, even as his office works to engineer a zoning ordinance which would allow Project Hazelnut to plow forward. When residents cornered Griguoli at a town meeting in August about the $10,000 bribes, he shrugged that “that’s what they’re saying they want to do,” adding that “I don’t even know if that’s legit, that it’s going to happen.” More on data centers: This Graph of Data Center Spending Compared to All Other Construction Will Give You Chills

Elorian’s first AI model wants to reason with images, not just about them
2026-10-16

Elorian’s first AI model wants to reason with images, not just about them

TechCrunch | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
Last day to save up to $200 to TechCrunch Founder Summit on Oct. 16. Register now. Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. Elorian, the Palo Alto AI startup that raised a $55 million seed round at a $300 million valuation before it launched a product, released its first model on Friday, TechCrunch has exclusively learned. The model, Glimpse, is launching as a research preview with API access and a chat interface is waitlist-only, so for now it’s a results table and a thesis. The thesis? Today’s AI models, for all their skill with language and code, still can’t really see. Most multimodal models look at an image once, convert it to tokens, and then reason about it in text — like describing a photo from memory. Glimpse keeps going back to the source. Mid-reasoning, it can crop into a region, pull specific frames from a video, or run code to measure or compare what it’s looking at, a mechanism Elorian calls a “visual scratch space.” Think of someone pinching to zoom on a phone photo to double-check what they thought they saw. For former Google DeepMind researcher and Elorian CEO Andrew Dai, one of the clearest signs that AI still couldn’t really see came on game night. Last year he photographed a board game he’d just finished with friends and asked a frontier model who was winning. It couldn’t say. Board games “each have their own visual language, each have their own iconography, symbols,” he told TechCrunch. “To an adult, it’s quite easy to understand what’s going on with minimal text, but these models aren’t able to intuit what the symbols mean.” “Humans can look at IKEA diagrams, with no text, and assemble IKEA furniture, right? Why can’t AI models?” he said. The approach isn’t completely new — OpenAI added crop-and-zoom during reasoning to its o-series models in 2025 — but Elorian’s pitch is that it built the whole model around it, cheaply. The company says a small team trained Glimpse on a few hundred GPUs and that the model is substantially smaller than the frontier systems it’s benchmarked against. Glimpse costs $0.60 per million tokens in and $3 per million tokens out — cheaper than mid-level models like Sonnet and Sol but more expensive than lightweight models like Haiku and Luna. But according to Elorian’s evaluations, Glimpse outperforms even GPT-6 Astra on scientific chart reasoning and short-form video captioning, with competitive results on other visual reasoning tasks. Elorian came out of stealth six months ago with a round co-led by Striker Venture Partners, Menlo Ventures and Altimeter Capital, with participation from Nvidia and Google chief scientist Jeff Dean. The initial targets are companies sitting on lots of video: robotics firms, scientific labs, media and creative companies. Longer term, Elorian wants to move from seeing to doing. That means building a model of a scene using that to choose an action, including directly controlling robots. Next year, Dai said, the company aims to build “the world’s best visual reasoning model,” one that leads on every visual reasoning test. Tressfecta wants to make hair braiding a less arduous task for hairstylists

Tressfecta wants to make hair braiding a less arduous task for hairstylists

TechCrunch | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
Last day to save up to $200 to TechCrunch Founder Summit on Oct. 16. Register now. Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. Aisata Diallo, 24, calls herself a lifelong braider. “I’ve always braided my hair growing up and, at some point in my college career, started braiding other people’s hair,” she said. At Northeastern University in Boston, she found limited access to braiders and textured-hair specialists, so she decided to fill that gap by starting a small business of her own. “Throughout that experience, I was very close to some of the pain points in the styling process and complained about it constantly,” she said. Braiding is a manual task, and a single style can take eight hours to complete. Many professionals deal with issues like cramping and carpal tunnel syndrome after spending long days standing behind a chair weaving hair into patterns. At first, Diallo said she didn’t know what to do about it. “I stopped braiding, and one of the reasons that I stopped braiding was due to the physical toll it has on your body.” She wanted to find a way to make braiding less painful andwondered whether new AI technology could automate some part of the process. Building hardware for braiding is expensive, difficult, and complex, which is one reason there have been few innovations in this space. The textured hair market has also long been overlooked by the mainstream. Then, last year, she had an idea: Tressfecta, a company building products for textured hair. Its first project is a tool called Plait Pilot that helps braiders with the initial process of separating hair from packs before braiding begins. That process alone can take hours, especially when a client wants small braids. Diallo said the device is designed to work like this: A stylist places the braiding hair on top of it and sets the size they want the hair separated into, and the device separates the pieces and dispenses them back to the stylist so they can grab them while braiding. “It’s very challenging to build this kind of tool in this space,” she said, citing the nuance and complexity of the braiding style process. Different types of textured hair also need to be accounted for, especially when length and density are considered. “It’s not a one-size-fits-all solution.” Tressfecta is still technically pre-launch, and it is working on the prototype of Plait Pilot. Diallo has been bootstrapping the company, while also drawing on funding from grant and accelerator programs. “I am preparing to fundraise,” she said, noting how hard it can be to raise money for hardware, especially beauty hardware. For now, Diallo said she’s connecting with stylists and braiders who can test the technology. She also spent this week in San Francisco as one of TechCrunch’s Battlefield 200 companies. She doesn’t remember exactly where she first heard about Disrupt, possibly on LinkedIn or in a founder resource group, but when we talked ahead of the show, which wrapped up on Thursday, she was looking forward to connecting with people and showing off her product. She was especially excited to connect with people and for others to see her product. “It feels like a great opportunity,” she said. In ‘You Can See Everything,’ Elizabeth Holmes meets her equal in comedian Nathan Fielder

In ‘You Can See Everything,’ Elizabeth Holmes meets her equal in comedian Nathan Fielder

TechCrunch | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
Last day to save up to $200 to TechCrunch Founder Summit on Oct. 16. Register now. Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. “Isn’t it weird that I like, did a TV show about kind of a business scam, and you got in trouble for like… a business scam?” comedian and filmmaker Nathan Fielder gently asks Elizabeth Holmes. One month before the notorious Theranos CEO surrendered to begin her eleven-year criminal fraud sentence in 2023, Holmes and her husband contacted A24 as a last-ditch effort to exonerate her in the public eye, suggesting they send a film crew to document her final days of freedom. Alongside director Lance Oppenheim, Fielder — a cringe-comedy mastermind whose work often walks the line between fact and fiction — embraced the impossible project of figuring out who the infamous Stanford dropout really is. “I never actually made that connection,” says Holmes. “I think it’s a pretty different situation.” No one is better prepared than Fielder to probe the depths of Holmes’ psyche. His satirical docu-reality show “Nathan for You” catapulted him to cult stardom as he assumed the role of an exaggerated, deadpan version of himself who tries to help struggling small business by executing asinine interventions. In one early episode that went viral, Fielder convinces a coffee shop to change its name to “Dumb Starbucks” as a marketing stunt, but consults a lawyer to help him toe the flimsy line of parody law and escape legal trouble. “If you just said that, you know, Theranos was a comedy bit, maybe you could get off scot free,” Fielder says to Holmes. “I was just kind of making a little joke. Maybe it was in bad taste.” We already know that Elizabeth Holmes is not innocent. What we still can’t figure out, though, is whether she really, truly believes it when she says she did nothing wrong. Even as she sat on trial — listening to testimonies from investors who lost hundreds of millions of dollars, whistleblowers who were silenced when they flagged that Theranos’ blood testing devices didn’t work, and patients who acted on incorrect blood test results — Holmes never wavered. Across Fielder’s body of metatextual comedy, even his most reverent fans can’t quite figure out when he’s acting, when he’s being himself, and if Fielder himself even knows where to draw that line anymore. In that way, Fielder is just like Holmes: after over a decade in the public eye, onlookers remain captivated by each of their ambiguous personas. “The world believes a reality about me that isn’t true,” she tells Fielder. “I know I didn’t commit any crime.” [The rest of this review contains spoilers.] Early on in filming, The New York Times has just published a profile of Holmes, which was widely criticized at the time for allowing Holmes to portray herself as an innocent mom deprived of the chance to watch her kids grow up. And yet, Holmes and her husband, hotel heir Billy Evans, were livid about the article. At one point in an interview, Holmes tells the Times reporter that she wasn’t offended by actress Amanda Seyfried’s portrayal of her in Hulu’s miniseries “The Dropout” because, “They’re not playing me. They’re playing a character I created.” She and her husband insist that the quote was taken out of context. They claim that they have evidence that Holmes actually said, “They’re playing a character they created,” relieving her from any implication that she admitted to concocting an inauthentic persona as CEO of Theranos. Fielder asks Evans multiple times to share this evidence so he can prove that Holmes was misquoted. He never does. Once Holmes reports to prison, Fielder is agonized as he tries to make something out of his month of footage. He calls in the perfect sidekick to help him dissect her confounding behavior: the very actress who portrays her in “The Dropout.” Fielder plays Seyfried a brutal clip from his time with Holmes, when he tries watching “The Dropout” with the subject of the series itself. When they get to a tense scene involving Holmes’ manipulative ex-boyfriend and former Theranos partner-in-crime Sunny Balwani — who is also now in prison — Holmes can no longer bear to watch this fictional version of herself. She turns away and cries. In other words, Seyfried is watching Holmes react to Seyfried’s portrayal of Holmes, who was probably also playing a character herself. But Seyfried is not the only one creating a crude approximation of the real Elizabeth. Evans has constructed a life-like AI avatar of his wife, trapped inside of a large vertical screen that he wheels over to the dinner table to sit with him and his kids as though she’s actually there. The remainder of the film is a constellation of colliding personas. Though it’s a non-fiction documentary, it’s clear that everyone involved is, to some extent, also performing for the cameras. How could they not? Seyfried’s determination to understand Holmes’ story seems pure, but it’s not lost on the audience that she just scored a leading role in one of the most talked-about films of the year. Meanwhile, Evans behaves in such an unfathomably egregious manner that he may even be intentionally playing the villain to drum up sympathy for his reviled wife. Evans knows that Fielder and Oppenheim are not convinced of Holmes’ innocence. This documentary will not sway anyone to believe that his wife is being wrongly held in federal custody. But Evans makes himself appear so appallingly unlikable that some viewers may leave the theater feeling bad for Holmes, seeing her as a victim, and allowing her to double-down on another persona she adopted while on trial: a harmless, docile woman who was manipulated by her ex-partner to obliviously tell a cascade of increasingly harmful lies. Once Holmes is in jail, Fielder is not allowed to bring recording devices when he visits her, so he tries to write a script of their conversations from memory, struggling to accurately recreate the nuances of a conversation where every last word carries immense meaning. With Seyfried reprising her role from “The Dropout,” we see the pair practice reading the script, editing the language as Fielder tries to recall Holmes’ exact phrasing and delivery. In character as Holmes, Seyfried says, “People think Amanda’s show is a documentary. Jev is Vercel’s fastest-adopted AI model ever

Jev is Vercel’s fastest-adopted AI model ever

TechCrunch | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
Last day to save up to $200 to TechCrunch Founder Summit on Oct. 16. Register now. Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. Jev has become the fastest adopted AI model ever — at least by some measures. Vercel data published on Thursday shows that, within two weeks of its launch, the buzzy decision model was being used by 35% of paid teams, with nearly double the reach of the second most popular model, Sonnet 5.5. It’s the fastest adoption a model has ever seen in its first two weeks, according to Vercel’s data, and a major accomplishment for the model’s creators, TypeSafe AI. Published in limited release on September 15, Jev is designed as a lightweight tool for choosing between a few predetermined outputs. Limiting the outputs lets the model run cheaper and faster than alternatives like Sonnet, making it ideal for classifying large quantities of data quickly. That new approach has attracted significant buzz in the weeks since Jev’s release — inspiring competing offerings from OpenAI and Amazon, among others — but until now there has been little hard data to back up the good vibes. The data comes from Vercel’s AI gateway, a unified API service similar to OpenRouter. It’s a necessarily limited view — particularly at risk of undercounting major labs like OpenAI and Anthropic that offer their own APIs — but it shows the buzz around Jev really did translate into actual usage. In fact, Vercel was an early adopter of Jev, using it to replace GPT-6 Luna for classifying harmful commands. The report also shows continuing momentum for open source models, which now account for more than 70% of the tokens processed through Vercel’s gateway and nearly 25% of the total spend. While open source models are capturing a larger share of the market, the market is also growing fast. Spending on frontier labs has grown tenfold, even as it accounts for a smaller share of overall spend, giving credence to the idea that frontier models and open source alternatives may turn out to be complementary rather than competitive. Even as Hollywood remains divided over the use of AI in filmmaking, actor and director Mark Wahlberg believes the technology has a place in the industry as long as it’s used the right way. Wahlberg, who’s also an entrepreneur and angel investor, took the stage at TechCrunch Disrupt to share his perspective on AI’s role in filmmaking, and how he’s open to having an AI likeness of himself carry on his legacy. The actor said he plans to potentially use AI to enhance certain aspects of the upcoming Pete Rose biopic, in which he’ll star in the title role. “It’s a fairly expensive movie, but, we’re talking about utilizing [AI] to enhance the stadium setting, things of that nature,” Wahlberg said. “I don’t think you should just, you know, put an idea into ChatGPT and it puts out a script, and then you should go make that and not use real actors. But if you can use it to enhance what you’re doing, I’m all for that.” That said, Wahlberg made special mention of his belief that storytelling should remain at the heart of filmmaking. “I’ve done big, effects-driven movies,” Wahlberg said. “I think I was in that kind of sweet spot when the first Jurassic Park was made, or when Perfect Storm came out, and you had that wave, the 100-foot wave that was in the trailer. Those are cutting-edge moments in technology with computer-generated images. Now, I think it’s hard to do that, so I’m not so passionate about effects-driven movies per se, as opposed to real storytelling.” When asked by TechCrunch Editor-in-Chief Connie Loizos whether he had visited the CAA Vault, a Creative Artists Agency facility which lets actors use scanning technology to capture their bodies, faces, movements, and voices to create AI replicas, Wahlberg said he hadn’t, but would be open to the idea. “I would be interested because I think in the future, if that’s where things go, it would be cool that my children or my grandchildren could be in charge of my likeness and my image, and hopefully put me in some good movies,” Wahlberg said. “It’ll be interesting to see where things go.” He even said he wouldn’t be opposed if his upcoming animated series, “Ted,” continued for decades with an AI version of his likeness. “I think it’s cool if I’m no longer able to do it or don’t exist, and if it could continue and people want to see it, I think it’d be interesting to see where things go,” Wahlberg said. “I think there’s certain things from my childhood that I wish still existed and were still a part of my life […] I wouldn’t be opposed to it. I think people are going to have massive opportunities to watch whatever it is they want, and if there’s a real audience for that, they should be entitled to have what they want to see.” When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. Veritas expands its anti-counterfeit chip to jewelry, trophies, and fragrance bottles

Veritas expands its anti-counterfeit chip to jewelry, trophies, and fragrance bottles

TechCrunch | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
Last day to save up to $200 to TechCrunch Founder Summit on Oct. 16. Register now. Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. Counterfeits of luxury goods are a major problem for fashion houses. Counterfeiters are getting better at replicating brands’ marks and passing off dupes, known as “superfakes,” as genuine items. A startup called Veritas wants to tackle the problem with its own chip that verifies goods. Veritas was founded by Luci Holland, a former Tesla technical product manager who also has a background in design and art. Its chip is built to be easily inserted into products without damaging them. Brands can use it to verify whether a product is the real deal, and customers can show a certificate of ownership through the company’s app. Holland said other approaches are easier to defeat, because a digital record can be reproduced through a copied QR code or static NFC link. “Our approach combines secure, authenticated chip responses with integration into the product itself. Our chip generates a secure response with each tap and includes a tamper-detection wire. We make the connection between the object and its digital identity part of the proof,” Holland said. The Veritas founder told TechCrunch over email that the company has just introduced the second version of its chip, which works on metal surfaces and can be scanned through glass. “The next-generation chip is ready for production and supports a broader range of applications, including jewelry, trophies, hard-surface products, and glass objects such as fragrance bottles,” Holland said. “Alongside our garment integrations, this gives brands more ways to incorporate authentication directly into their products.” This week, the startup announced a partnership with ATHLOS, a women’s track-and-field competition founded by Reddit co-founder Alexis Ohanian, who is also an investor in Veritas. As part of the partnership, all 18 championship crowns will include Veritas’ chips so they can be verified. The company also embedded a chip into a pair of LeBron James’ first Nike shoes, reimagined by artist Adam Port. Tapping a phone to the shoe brings up its history and story. The company has so far raised $1.75 million in pre-seed funding led by Ohanian’s venture firm Seven Seven Six, with participation from DoorDash co-founder Stanley Tang; Gloria Zhu, co-founder of skincare brand Reys; and former TechCrunch editor Josh Constine. Since its February fundraise, the company has shipped an iOS app for consumers and a brand portal that lets companies manage product records and build owner experiences. Through the app and the portal, brands can offer experiences such as clubs, event check-ins, and segmented early access to product drops. Veritas competed in this year’s TechCrunch Disrupt Startup Battlefield, which ran Tuesday through Thursday. A winner was announced Thursday afternoon. What to Know About the Blood Clot Sidelining Jalen Hurts
2026-10-16

What to Know About the Blood Clot Sidelining Jalen Hurts

TIME | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Jalen Hurts of the Philadelphia Eagles looks on during the NFL match between Philadelphia Eagles and Jacksonville Jaguars at Tottenham Hotspur Stadium on October 11, 2026 in London. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings For an NFL quarterback, a sore calf might seem like an unsurprising consequence of getting hit by a cleat. In Jalen Hurts’ case, an ultrasound revealed something else: a superficial blood clot. The Philadelphia Eagles quarterback was struck in the calf during an Oct. 4 game and sought treatment four days later, according to a statement from the team. An ultrasound in London revealed the clot, but doctors cleared him to play in the team’s game there the next day. Then, a follow-up ultrasound Wednesday night revealed “movement in the small blood clot.” The Eagles announced Thursday that Hurts would miss Sunday’s game against the Carolina Panthers while the team determines his treatment. What makes a clot superficial—and when does it become dangerous? We asked doctors to explain. To understand blood clots, picture a road map. Your deep veins run within the muscles of your arms and legs, carrying most of the blood returning from your limbs to your heart. Superficial veins sit closer to the surface, just underneath the skin and in the tissue beneath it. Connecting veins bridge the two systems. “You have a really well interconnected network of veins,” says Dr. William Shutze, a vascular surgeon at Texas Vascular Associates in Dallas and vice president of the Society for Vascular Surgery (who was not involved in Hurts’ care). Think of it as “a very well developed road and highway system,” with multiple routes to get where you need to go. When a clot blocks one route, blood can take a detour through other veins. A superficial clot forms in one of the veins near the skin. The area can become red, warm, and tender. “It’s usually very uncomfortable,” Shutze says. Some develop in varicose veins; others form where a needle or IV has irritated a vein. You can sometimes feel the affected vein beneath your fingers. “It’s like a pencil underneath the skin, or a knot,” says Dr. Stephan Moll, a hematologist and professor of medicine at the University of North Carolina who runs an Athletes and Blood Clots Clinic (who was not involved in Hurts’ care). Deep vein thrombosis involves a clot farther inside the body. Its symptoms can be more widespread—the whole calf or ankle might become swollen and painful, Moll says. You can’t feel the deep vein itself. Many superficial clots stay put and get better without blood thinners. But sometimes, a clot grows into one of the deeper veins. A piece can then break off and travel to the lungs, causing a potentially life-threatening complication called a pulmonary embolism. Doctors use ultrasound to assess the clot and check for deep-vein involvement, Shutze says. They’re particularly concerned about superficial clots longer than 5 centimeters or within 3 centimeters of where a superficial vein joins a deep one. The Eagles haven’t specified those details. And “movement” isn’t precise enough to establish what happened. “That’s not a term we use in clinical practice,” Moll says. It could describe a clot growing or extending; it doesn’t, by itself, establish that a piece broke loose or reached the lungs. Being in excellent shape doesn’t make someone immune to a blood clot. An injury can damage a vein, while prolonged inactivity slows blood flow. Surgery, certain inherited clotting disorders, cancer, and pregnancy can also increase risk, says Dr. Alina Mitina, an emergency medicine physician at Hackensack University Medical Center in New Jersey (who was not involved in Hurts’ care). Veins near the skin have less cushioning when someone takes a hit. “The deeper veins are protected,” Shutze says, although a severe impact can injure those, too. A clot can form within hours of an injury or develop over several days, he adds. If your leg already hurts from getting hit, you might not realize there’s another reason it’s sore. Travel can add another risk: Sitting through a long flight or car ride means the legs aren’t moving as much, slowing blood flow. Some superficial clots can be managed with warm compresses, anti-inflammatory medication, elevation, and monitoring. Others need blood thinners to help keep the clot from growing—even if it hasn’t reached a deep vein. If blood thinners are needed, treatment is usually temporary, Moll says, often lasting several weeks. That doesn’t necessarily mean spending recovery on the couch. “We like them to be active because we want to keep the blood flowing,” Shutze says. Getting up for a walk, of course, is different from getting tackled. Shutze worries about athletes taking another hit where they already have a clot. Blood thinners add another concern: An injury that causes bleeding can become much more dangerous when blood doesn’t clot as readily. In football, “you have constant significant contact,” Moll says, which raises the risk of serious bleeding, including inside the head or muscles. Athletes are used to being sore. “They often minimize clinical symptoms, completely understandably, because they don’t want to be taken off the roster,” Moll says. Pay attention when discomfort feels different, persists, or worsens. Pain, swelling, discoloration, and warmth can signal a clot. Mitina says patients sometimes describe tightness in the leg; redness around a superficial clot can resemble an infection. If you think you might have a superficial clot, “don’t ignore it,” Shutze says. Have a doctor check it out rather than assuming it’s a bruise or muscle strain. Unexplained shortness of breath, chest pain, coughing up blood, or fainting require emergency care because they can signal a pulmonary embolism. Moll also flags an unexplained cough and fast heart rate as possible warning signs. And getting checked once doesn’t mean you should brush off symptoms that persist or worsen. “If something feels weird or symptoms aren’t getting better, always return to the ER,” Mitina says. “You know your body best.”

13 Habits Food-Waste Experts Practice in Their Own Kitchens
2026-10-16

13 Habits Food-Waste Experts Practice in Their Own Kitchens

TIME | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings At the grocery store, you’re a person who roasts squash. You make salads. You have exciting plans for fresh herbs. A week later, you’re a person throwing away unused squash, salad, and wilted herbs. Even food-waste experts know the gap between the meals we imagine and the food we actually eat. Shelie Miller, who studies sustainability at the University of Michigan, understands how an acorn squash can end up in your cart without a plan. “It’s fall. I’m feeling the fall vibes,” she says, describing the temptation. That’s why she and her husband try to stick to a shared grocery list—and know what they’ll do with what they buy. We asked experts what else they do to keep good intentions out of the trash. Read more: How to Stop Wasting So Much Food in Your Kid’s Lunchbox Once a week, Miller bakes a potato and gives the odds and ends in her refrigerator somewhere to go. Leftover fajita vegetables? On the potato. Little extras from other meals? Those go on, too. “I always call it a garbage potato, or maybe it’s better to call it a kitchen-sink potato,” she says. The toppings change often enough to keep things interesting: “My husband always plays a guessing game of what my potato is going to be this week.” The potato solves a familiar problem: You have some food left, but not quite enough to call it lunch. Miller combines those small portions on something she already wants to eat. “It’s such a great blank canvas,” she says. Stir-fries can serve a similar purpose. But Miller knows where her loyalties lie: “My go-to is a potato.” Jonathan Deutsch has watched fellow cooks buy cases of broccoli, cut off the florets, and throw away the stems. He considers this a terrible loss. The tender interior of a broccoli stem, he says, is “one of the most delicious vegetables.” Deutsch, a professor in the department of food and hospitality management and founding director of the Drexel Food Lab, cooks both parts at home. The night before we spoke, he made broccoli salad with tahini and chili crisp. The stem was woody on the outside, so he peeled it, cut up the tender interior, and then air-fried it along with the florets. Trim the woody bottom and peel away the tough exterior as needed. Deutsch wants people to recognize the good food underneath. “The same care and love that went into the florets,” he says, “went three inches lower into the stem.” A recipe that calls for half a cabbage leaves you with another decision: What happens to the other half? Miller and her husband try to answer that before they start cooking. Cabbage rolls might take care of some; soup or a roasted side dish can use the rest. The same thinking applies to the tomato paste left in a can or the extra chicken breast in a package. “Getting comfortable with all the different ways you can prepare different ingredients really helps extend the menu,” she says. Deutsch starts by checking what’s already in his kitchen. When his now-adult daughters were teenagers, they would announce there was nothing to eat. He could usually find the makings of a meal: a can of tuna, a can of beans, or chicken in the freezer. Only after taking stock would he figure out what he needed to buy. “Instead of generating more waste that you have to deal with, you’re taking surplus food,” he says, “and you’re making it into new things that you want to eat.” Brian Roe is willing to commit to a Costco-size bag of apples. He just doesn’t leave it on the counter. “I’m an apple kind of person,” says Roe, a professor of agricultural, environmental, and development economics at Ohio State University who leads its Food Waste Collaborative. He keeps the apples in the refrigerator, where he says he rarely loses one to spoilage. On the counter, he’s more likely to throw a couple away before finishing the bag. Miller knows what can happen to food you freeze with the best intentions: “It just goes into the abyss of a freezer.” Eventually, you clean it out and find “freezer-burned chicken breasts and chili from three years ago,” she says. “What happened?” She and her husband now make a point of shopping in their freezer before deciding what to buy or cook. Sometimes, that means discovering they already have lunches covered for the week. Other times, it means remembering the portions of tomato paste they froze after a recipe called for only a spoonful. There are only two of them at home, so a big batch can mean eating the same dinner several nights running. “By the fourth day, the last thing you want is more gumbo,” she says. Their approach: “We’ll eat a day or two of leftovers. We’ll freeze the rest.” Then, when they need lunches another week, they can pull out a meal they haven’t just eaten three days running. Miller’s husband has figured out how to fit more leftovers in their freezer: He freezes soups and other liquids flat in zip-top bags on a sheet pan. “It ends up like these little folders of soup,” she says. Once frozen, the thin packages stack neatly, taking up less space than bulky containers. Use bags intended for freezing, leave room for liquid to expand, and follow safe cooling, storage, and reheating practices. Deutsch keeps a bag in his freezer for the parts of dinner that don’t make it onto the plate: onion trimmings, mushroom bits, and herb stems. When he’s ready to make stock, he has a head start. “Stock is like magic,” he says. When people wonder why their soup doesn’t taste as good as their favorite restaurant’s, he points to what might be missing: stock, along with generous seasoning. He also uses it in place of water when cooking rice, polenta, or vegetables to give them extra flavor. He’s selective about what goes into the stock. Add beet scraps, and “you’re gonna have a pink or brownish stock,” he says. Add broccoli trimmings, and “it’s gonna have a little bit of a cabbagey broccoli smell.” That could be delicious in broccoli mac and cheese, but it’s probably not the flavor you want in butternut squash soup. “You have to be a little strategic about what goes in there,” he says. Roe’s air fryer helps him get another meal out of restaurant leftovers. Recently, he used it to reheat half a tuna steak and vegetables his wife had brought home. “That makes things kind of tasty and crispy again,” he says. He thinks about the next meal while putting food away, taking an extra minute to make leftovers easy to reheat. The goal is “storing your leftovers in a way that helps you want to use them the next time,” he says. Sometimes, that means turning last night’s teriyaki chicken into today’s salad. His adult son has found another opening for leftovers: breakfast. “They’re the freshest they’ll ever be because it’s the first meal after,” Roe says. Ground turkey, two green peppers, and feta: Is that dinner? Deutsch suggests asking a chatbot before adding anything else to your grocery list. He’s experimented with AI to find ways to use ingredients on hand, and recommends giving it more than a list of what’s in the fridge. Tell it how much time you have, what equipment you’re using, and how comfortable you are in the kitchen. “Make me an easy recipe with these ingredients,” he suggests instructing your AI platform of choice. You can ask for something that takes less than 30 minutes, too—so you don’t discover halfway through the instructions that dinner needs to marinate overnight. “You could really customize it,” he says. Throwing away part of a chicken breast means wasting more than dinner. It also wastes the resources used to raise and feed the chicken. Animal products generally have a larger environmental footprint than plant foods, Miller explains, which makes keeping meat, eggs, and dairy from going uneaten especially consequential. Miller and her husband still eat meat, but they’ve started serving smaller portions. “Meat is an accent to the meal,” she says. They’re trying to avoid a familiar outcome: “We both have two huge chicken breasts and waste a quarter of them.” Roe describes himself as an evangelist for buying less food—a habit that’s followed him home, “maybe not to the happiness of everybody in my household,” he says. When they’re having people over, Roe tries to calculate what guests will actually eat, while his spouse comes from a tradition of making sure there’s enough to “feed the army.” At Thanksgiving, Roe uses the Guest-imator, an online tool from Save the Food that estimates how much to prepare for a gathering. “A lot of it is just buying less or really thinking about what’s being purchased and why,” he says. Miller studies the environmental impact of plastic packaging. She also has a freezer full of soup in zip-top bags. “Very honestly, we go through a lot of single-use plastic,” she says. Her research helps explain that choice. A 2022 analysis she co-authored found that plastic packaging accounted for less than 10% of the total environmental footprint of 23 out of 30 foods across most measures examined. And in a 2019 study comparing meal kits with grocery-store meals, the greenhouse-gas savings from reduced food waste outweighed the extra packaging emissions. Miller uses reusable containers for lunches and tries to minimize disposable items. But she considers what the packaging helps her accomplish. “A good zip-top bag is really hard to compete with for certain applications,” she says. For her, the environmental cost of throwing away food matters more than the bag used to save it. “When you look at the environmental impacts of food waste versus a single-use plastic bag, the food waste is the much more important thing,” she says. “My big philosophy is not to sweat the small stuff,” she says. Miller collects food scraps in a metal pail with a liner. “It’s nothing fancy,” she says. But she doesn’t consider composting a substitute for eating the food she buys. “A lot of folks think, well, hey, I’m composting, so it’s OK to waste food,” she says. Composting can’t recover all the resources used to produce food that never gets eaten, so she focuses first on using what’s in her kitchen—whether it becomes lunch, soup, or next week’s potato. When food does go uneaten, “throw it in the compost rather than the trash if you have that option available,” she says.

Billionaires Are the Symptom, Not the Disease
2026-10-16

Billionaires Are the Symptom, Not the Disease

TIME | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings After nearly a century, America has rediscovered the wealth tax. This November, Californians will vote on Proposition 40, a union-backed initiative levying a one-time 5% tax on the net worth of the state's billionaires. Senator Bernie Sanders and Representative Ro Khanna have introduced a federal version; a dozen states have advanced millionaire or billionaire taxes this year; and Maine, Washington, Hawaii, and Rhode Island have all enacted one. Not since the Progressive Era has taxing the rich had this much momentum. The energy is easy to understand. The 25 wealthiest Americans pay a “true tax rate” of just 3.4%—a fraction of what nurses and teachers pay—while holding about 2% of the nation's wealth. That’s nearly as much as the entire bottom half of the country combined. This spring, Forbes counted a record 3,428 billionaires worth $20.1 trillion, up $4 trillion since last year. Elon Musk topped the list at $839 billion, and this summer he became history's first trillionaire. At the same time, workers' share of economic output fell to 52.8%, the lowest since records began in 1947. Today, billionaire wealth is at an all-time high while labor's share is at an all-time low. One obvious response is to tax the fortunes at the top and redistribute those funds. But I worry that the wealth-tax movement is aimed at the wrong target. Billionaires create a lot of damage, to be sure. As Alex Gibney’s new documentary Musk lays out, the Tesla and SpaceX CEO spent nearly $300 million helping elect a president and then slashed federal agencies, including several investigating his own companies. Still, he and his tech-bro pals are not themselves the underlying reason for our economic dysfunction. In my view, the root cause is corporate power—the way modern corporations are owned, governed, and permitted to organize markets and politics to their own advantage—and how relentlessly that power has concentrated over the past 50 years. The evidence is everywhere. Since the late 1990s, three-quarters of American industries have grown more concentrated. Corporate margins have roughly tripled since 1980, and after-tax profits—below 10% of GDP for virtually all of 1950 to 2010—now exceed 12%, near the record. Economist Thomas Philippon calculates that weakened competition costs the typical household about $300 a month. Since 1970, when Milton Friedman declared that a corporation's only social responsibility is profit, businesses have been re-engineered around delivering the most value possible to shareholders. Today, the bulk of many senior executives’ pay comes in the form of stock options and share buybacks (when a company spends its earnings to buy up its own stock and lift the price), which now send over a trillion dollars a year to shareholders rather than into wages or investment. Before 1982, this practice was viewed as market manipulation. In the following decades, the forces that once pushed back against inequality were gutted: union membership fell from 20% of American workers to 10%, and antitrust enforcement was consistently muted. This is where billionaires come in. Nobody earns $839 billion from a paycheck. A fortune of that scale can only be made by accumulating equity. That is, gains from corporate profits. Profit is simply what remains after wages are held down, taxes are minimized, and costs are pushed onto workers, communities, and the environment. It makes little difference whether the claim to those profits comes from founding a company or, as with hedge-fund and private-equity fortunes, from the fruit of corporations other people built. Because the richest tenth of Americans own nearly 90% of household stock-market wealth, every dollar shifted from paychecks to profit flows almost undiluted to the top. You don't have to look hard for examples. The Walton fortune rests on wages so low that a federal watchdog found Walmart among the biggest employers of workers on Medicaid and food stamps. In other words, taxpayers are subsidizing the payroll beneath a dynastic fortune. Amazon founder and CEO Jeff Bezos's wealth compounded over years during which Amazon paid no federal income tax on billions in profit while fighting its warehouse workers' union drives. In each case, the fortune is substantially extracted from workers and the public, not the market reward it is billed as. None of this would last without help from Washington. Over the past half century, political spending has become a core business strategy and arguably the highest-return investment a large company can make. In a 1971 memorandum, future Supreme Court justice Lewis Powell urged the Chamber of Commerce to put business on a political war footing. Today, lobbying has grown into a multibillion-dollar industry and business now outspends labor and public-interest groups 34-to-one. Since Citizens United removed the last meaningful limits on business political spending in 2010, every election has cost more than the last—nearly $16 billion in 2024, mostly corporate money. Under President Donald Trump, the exchange has become impossible to ignore. Corporations lined up with seven-figure donations to his inaugural fund; oil executives, who were asked point-blank for $1 billion at a Mar-a-Lago dinner, have watched climate rules fall ever since; Paramount paid $16 million to settle a lawsuit legal experts dismissed while its merger awaited federal approval; and the crypto industry, having spent more than $130 million on the 2024 races, saw the enforcement cases against it evaporate, and this fall is spending hundreds of millions on 2026 midterm races. This is why a politics aimed only at billionaires will miss the mark. I support wealth taxes, and I hope Proposition 40 passes. Yet wealth taxes only claw back winnings after the fact. This year's Forbes’ Billionaires list added 390 newcomers. Taxing Musk, Bezos, and other billionaires does not touch the executives, boards, investment funds, and political machines that keep minting new ones. What would it mean to take aim at the root cause of inequality instead? First, we must recognize that the shareholder-primacy firm is a policy choice—the product of lobbying and lawmaking since the 1970s—and policy choices can be remade by rebuilding labor power through labor-law reform and sectoral bargaining, reviving antitrust, and getting corporate money out of politics. Early American corporate charters, after all, were granted for limited public purposes and could be revoked when abused. It also means redesigning ownership itself. In my research on alternative corporate models, I have studied companies that are deliberately structured so that no one can extract a dynastic fortune, because the equity is legally locked to purpose. When Patagonia's founder moved the company into a purpose trust in 2022, such extraction became impossible by design; Bosch has been majority-owned by a foundation for decades and competes ferociously. Steward ownership, employee ownership, and benefit corporations are working alternatives operating in thousands of companies around the world and address wealth concentration at its source. The wealth-tax debate, in other words, is about how to redistribute the gains that came from corporate power. But we need to redistribute corporate power itself. None of this argues against Proposition 40 or its state and federal counterparts. Vote for them, pass them, let the revenue fund healthcare and schools. It is an argument about where attention needs to be focused next. History tells us that previous American confrontations with concentrated wealth, from Progressive Era trustbusters to the New Deal, succeeded not merely by taxing the winners but by restructuring corporate power through antitrust enforcement, securities regulation, and protecting labor rights. The real question facing America is whether corporations exist to serve society, or society exists to serve corporations. A politics that stops at the wealth tax has already conceded the answer.
