Human Judgement Is the Most Powerful Technology in the AI Age
2026-10-16

Human Judgement Is the Most Powerful Technology in the AI Age

TIME | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings In Ukraine, a Russian drone guided by artificial intelligence killed three civilians at a gas station, including a 19-year-old university student. In Gaza, an AI-assisted intelligence tool helped Israeli forces locate a Hamas commander. The subsequent airstrike killed him and, according to independent estimates, at least 125 other people. Different wars. Different circumstances. But a deeply troubling question: As machines become more involved in decisions of life and death, are we losing sight of the human beings whose lives hang in the balance? That question brought me back this month to a small white house in Reykjavík, Iceland. Forty years ago, Ronald Reagan and Mikhail Gorbachev met at Höfði House, representing rival nuclear superpowers divided by decades of distrust. They left without an agreement. Yet their willingness to talk, listen, and imagine what had seemed impossible helped lay the groundwork for historic nuclear arms reductions. I remember that moment vividly. I was a young Icelandic exchange student living in the United States, watching two world leaders meet in my small home country. I could never have imagined that 40 years later, as President of Iceland, I would return to that same house to ask a new generation of leaders to find the courage to talk. The enduring lesson of Reykjavík is not simply about nuclear weapons or diplomacy. It is about the extraordinary power of human connection and dialogue. And we urgently need to remember it. Never have we possessed more sophisticated tools to communicate, yet our ability to understand one another seems increasingly fragile. We are connected across continents in milliseconds, but too often separated by fear, anger, and mistrust. Our technologies can transform medicine, education, and human prosperity. Yet they can also accelerate conflict, undermine democracy, and challenge human control. Thanks to new technologies like AI, our power has grown exponentially. But has our wisdom kept pace? Over the past week, Iceland has explored that question not only through another conventional summit, but through a journey from remembrance to dialogue, and from dialogue to responsibility. We began with a traveling exhibition from the Hiroshima Peace Memorial Museum and the testimony of a survivor of the atomic bombing. More than 80 years later, she still vividly recalls what she describes as the sudden appearance of a thousand suns, followed by unimaginable destruction, decades of devastating illness, and the stigma endured by survivors. No discussion of nuclear deterrence should ever lose sight of the human lives behind those words. We then confronted the promises and dangers of artificial intelligence with technology experts, including Tristan Harris and Aza Raskin. The central question was not simply what AI can do, but what we should allow it to do—and who gets to decide. Perhaps the most important conversations, however, happened far from the conference halls. Across Iceland, young people invited fellow citizens to put down their phones and sit together to discuss the future they want to create. Students, parents, grandparents, teachers, and neighbors talked about education, belonging, technology, nature, and peace. The young people were not asking adults to arrive with all the answers. They wanted to be heard. To be taken seriously. To have a voice in decisions that will shape their lives long after today's leaders have left office. They understood something we too often forget: decisions made far from the people they affect are rarely wise ones. Because dialogue is more than exchanging opinions. At its best, it is an act of recognition: I see you. Your experience matters. Your future matters. Even when we disagree, we share responsibility for what comes next. The purpose of dialogue is not always consensus. It is connection. And connection is where trust begins. Without trust, even the most brilliant solutions struggle to take root. We carried that spirit back to Höfði House, where leaders from politics, technology, science, business, and civil society gathered to ask what responsible leadership demands of us today. The result was A Call from Reykjavík, signed by three women presidents and open to others who wish to join. It calls for meaningful human control over powerful technologies, insists that decisions about war and nuclear weapons remain firmly in human hands, and places peace and responsibility to future generations at the heart of leadership. The following day, in a public conversation moderated by renowned journalist Christiane Amanpour, President Nataša Pirc Musar of Slovenia, former Irish President Mary Robinson and I reflected on the choices before humanity. Forty years after two powerful men met in Reykjavík, three women—indeed, three world leaders—came together to continue the conversation. Not because women have all the answers. We do not. But because progress requires more voices, more perspectives, and a willingness to listen across differences. And because the future must never belong only to those who currently hold power. I do not believe dialogue is a soft alternative to leadership. I believe it is one of its most demanding forms. It takes courage to listen to someone with whom we profoundly disagree. Humility to acknowledge what we do not know. And strength to resist reducing complex human challenges to slogans, enemies, or simple answers. Dialogue does not mean abandoning our values, excusing aggression, or accepting injustice. Nor is it a substitute for action. It is how we begin to build the trust and understanding that make lasting action possible. The wars in Ukraine and Gaza remind us of the devastating human cost of conflict. AI-assisted warfare adds another dimension: the danger of placing ever greater distance between those making decisions and those living with their consequences. We must not surrender human judgment, accountability, or conscience to machines. But technology alone is not the problem, and technology alone cannot provide the answer. We also need stronger human infrastructure: relationships, institutions, shared values, and habits of cooperation that allow us to respond collectively to danger. We must equip the next generation not only with technical skills, but with the deeply human capacities to shape our future: listening, critical thinking, empathy, courage, and collaboration. In an age of intelligent machines, these may be our most essential skills. Iceland is a small nation. We cannot dictate the course of global affairs. But perhaps our size reminds us of something larger nations sometimes forget: no one can build a peaceful future alone. Forty years ago, Reykjavík showed that even the deepest divisions need not determine our destiny. Today, we need a new Reykjavík moment. Not one confined to a single house, summit, or handful of world leaders. We need it in parliaments and boardrooms, classrooms and communities, across generations and between nations in conflict. Wherever fear has replaced curiosity, wherever people have stopped listening, wherever power has outrun wisdom. The world does not lack intelligence, information, or ambition. What we too often lack is the courage to truly connect. Of course, dialogue alone will not save us. But without it, I struggle to see how we can save ourselves. The future will not be shaped only by the power of our technology or our weapons, but by the quality of our conversations—and the courage of the choices that follow. Fortunately, we still have time. And we still have each other. TIME Ideas hosts the world's leading voices, providing commentary on events in news, society, and culture. We welcome outside contributions. Opinions expressed do not necessarily reflect the views of TIME editors.

Why is France Burning?
2026-10-16

Why is France Burning?

TIME | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Young protesters at Place de la Bastille on October 08, 2026 in Paris, France. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings The French like to take to the streets, and they do it often. On Sept. 17, students at Lycée Saint-Exupéry, a rather underprivileged high school in the Paris suburb of Créteil, erupted in protest over the abysmal conditions of their institution: chronic shortfall of teachers, poor facilities, long declining quality of education. Two weeks later, a wave of protests had swept cities across France. Students from hundreds of schools and scores of universities joined the protests, backed by their parents, teachers, and trade unions. More than 160,000 students and trade unionists protested across France on Tuesday in the third week of demonstrations. France has never seen so many violent incidents in a movement made up largely of high school students. So far, over 200 students, 85 school staff, and 700 police officers have been injured in clashes across the country. Protesters have broken into schools and, in some cases, set fire to occupied or blockaded buildings. On Oct. 5, a 15-year-old boy had his hand blown off by a police grenade during a protest in Lens in northern France, an incident that crystallized the heavy-handed response authorities had adopted from the earliest days of the protest. More than 6,000 protesters have been arrested, most of them minors, and later released without charge. The protests erupted after nearly a decade of Macronisme, the right-of-center, top-down governing style that President Emmanuel Macron of France incarnates. And they express a broader discontent: anger over disparities in school quality, university admissions decisions, and latent racial discrimination, and a sense among young people that those in power were not listening to them, that the country offered them little hope for the future. The protests are also occurring amid a wider crisis of political confidence in the country. France is holding its presidential elections in April 2027, and the far-right and far-left are leading the opinion polls. The frontrunner is Marine Le Pen, who has worked for years to make her far-right party, the National Rally, more electable while campaigning on immigration, crime, and denunciation of an out-of-touch political class. Her party is already the largest opposition party in parliament, and a snap legislative election following the presidential vote could entrench it further. On the other side is Jean-Luc Mélenchon, the leader of France Unbowed, a far-left party, who is  promising a sweeping redistribution of power and resources toward the working class. Both have built much of their appeal on opposition to the political establishment, the promise to wish away budget constraints, and deep hostility toward the European Union. If France today feels like it is at a crossroads, it is because it is. The postwar model of a large welfare and regulatory state, run by a class of civil servants charged with modernizing the economy, tempering market forces and defining the common good, has run its course. France now runs one of Europe’s largest budget deficits: its public debt stands at 119.3 % of GDP and is expected to rise next year. Fears of a crippling financial crisis are growing. Borrowing costs have risen so high that France is drawing comparisons with Greece, whose debt crisis in 2010 threatened the survival of the Eurozone. And with economic growth stagnating, there is no easy way out. If anything, France would need to spend more: on defense, as Russia grows more threatening, the war in Ukraine continues, and the United States retreats from its traditional role as Europe’s security guarantor; on new technologies, as the country that once produced Concorde and high-speed trains now trails in high-tech innovation and AI; on strained public services, from schools to hospitals to neglected neighborhoods; and on infrastructure capable of withstanding the growing effects of climate change. Successive French governments have promised to turn things around and allow for these badly needed investments in the country’s future, but none has succeeded. The French malaise goes deeper. Inequality has been one of the students’ central grievances. They are not wrong. As in many countries, economic inequalities in France have widened over the past two decades under the combined effects of globalization, industrial decline, and the growing concentration of inherited wealth. In Capital in the Twenty-First Century, the French economist Thomas Piketty showed how wealth perpetuates across generations and widens the divide between those who own capital and everyone else. This concentration of wealth and power remains deeply resented: for many, President Macron’s original sin is still his brief stint at the Rothschild investment bank before entering politics. But the most injurious form of inequality lies elsewhere: in the power of France’s national meritocratic myth, which holds that people broadly owe their social position largely to their academic performance. At the top of that system sit the highly competitive grandes écoles, the elite institutions of higher education that have produced leaders in politics, culture, and business. What makes the myth so corrosive is not that academic achievement goes unrewarded but that it remains a pretty reliable path to better jobs, higher status, and positions of power. In such a system, students from underfunded public schools face a profound disadvantage compared with peers who attend better-resourced schools and whose better-educated parents know how to navigate the system. Private schools offer another route, but are still frowned upon as a way to buy an advantage. It is no surprise that the protests broke out in Créteil, a working-class suburb just outside Paris, where schools are chronically under-resourced. Racial and geographic discrimination also contribute to the crisis of France. The collection of ethno-racial statistics is prohibited by French law; it is a laudable restriction in principle but problematic in practice. Visible minorities remain vastly underrepresented among France’s political and business elites. Families with immigrant backgrounds, many with roots in African and Asian countries once colonized by France, are disproportionately concentrated on urban peripheries, where jobs are scarce and residents often face discrimination from employers elsewhere. Islamophobia has also grown over the last decade amid conflicts in the Middle East and a string of Islamist-inspired terrorist attacks. Rhetoric once largely confined to the far right has seeped into the mainstream right, where calls for “order” and “security” often carry an anti-immigrant charge. Macron’s references to the “decivilization” of some young people and former Interior Minister Bruno Retailleau’s description of immigrants’ participation in the protests that followed the police killing of a teenager in 2023 as a “regression towards ethnic origins” are emblematic of that shift. There is another problem with France’s meritocratic ideal: Paris. Political power, economic opportunity, cultural prestige, and elite education remain extraordinarily concentrated in the capital. The result is a deep economic divide between Paris and the rest of the country and an elite that lives largely among its own, surrounded by people with similar educations, careers, and assumptions, and cut off from much of the country. The Yellow Vest revolt of 2018 to 2019 was an earlier social explosion of precisely that resentment. It is hardly surprising that students are denouncing the unequal distribution of resources between Paris and the rest of the country, or that the protests began in Créteil, close enough to the capital for the contrast to be impossible to miss. When students from my own former school, the elite Lycée Henri-IV, where I was a rather poor student, joined the movement in its third week, I noticed how carefully they described themselves as protesting “in solidarity” with less privileged schools. They knew which side of the divide they were on. So much for meritocracy. Where does this leave France? With the presidential elections approaching next April, political parties are scrambling to position themselves in relation to a protest movement that has shown no sign of abating. On Oct. 6, more than a quarter-million people took to the streets as high school students were joined by university students, teachers, parents and trade unions. An even wider “national day of protest” is planned for Oct. 17. Both familiar and new dynamics will shape the election. The familiar one is the runoff dilemma: voters may once again be asked to support a candidate from the opposite political camp simply to prevent Le Pen from reaching the Élysée. Le Pen made the runoff in both 2017 and 2022 and is leading the polls by a large margin. This time, however, the field trailing her is unusually fragmented. Two former prime ministers under Macron, Édouard Philippe and Gabriel Attal, are in the race; Philippe is competing with Mélenchon for second place, while Attal trails both. Whatever the outcome, the presidential vote may resolve little. A president from the far-right National Rally could find herself governing without a stable parliamentary majority or broad support among the public or within the civil service. A compromise candidate from the center or left could face the same fractured parliament that has bedeviled the final years of Macron’s presidency, during which France has had five prime ministers in two years. What is new is the extent to which France is being reshaped by forces now familiar across the globe: a crisis of confidence in democracy, conservative billionaires building media empires and financing ideological movements, polarizing social media, foreign interference in elections, and growing voter distrust of supranational institutions, including the European Union. France, unlike the United States, places strict limits on campaign spending, but money still shapes the broader political ecosystem. Pierre-Édouard Stérin, a self-described “conservative libertarian,” has committed substantial resources to organizations seeking to advance right-wing and far-right agendas. The industrialist Vincent Bolloré has assembled a vast media empire that includes CNews, a Fox News-style channel that has become an important platform for the nationalist right. Whatever the result of the Presidential elections, the outlook is troubling for Europe. A Le Pen or Mélenchon presidency would test European cohesion on everything from Ukraine and relations with Russia to migration, budgets, and the authority of the European Union. But even a more centrist president would likely lack a strong enough coalition to govern effectively, mirroring the current situation in Germany, where Chancellor Friedrich Merz is hobbled by unpopularity and a surge in support for the far-right Alternative for Deutschland. With France and Germany together accounting for roughly 40% of the E.U. economy, political weakness in both capitals would deprive Europe of its traditional engine at a particularly dangerous moment. Europe faces mounting pressure on three fronts: China’s industrial onslaught, reflected in an annual trade deficit of roughly €360 billion; Russia’s war against Ukraine and escalating “gray warfare” operations; and the American turn against the postwar international order on which Europe has long depended. All this comes as Europe must also prepare for the profound disruptions of climate change and AI. The remedy laid out by Mario Draghi, famed for rescuing the euro during his tenure as president of the European Central Bank, calls for greater integration of the single market, more investment and industrial consolidation, and a greater capacity to act collectively. France is indispensable to any such reforms. A politically paralyzed France would leave Europe weaker at precisely the moment when it can least afford it. At the heart of the French Revolution of 1789 was the abolition of the privileges of the aristocracy and clergy. Close to two and a half centuries later, privilege remains deeply embedded in French society, dispersed across an array of almost caste-like social and professional groups, from journalists, lawyers, and intellectuals to civil servants and unionized workers, each with its own advantages, protections, pension arrangements, and claims on state resources. The result is a politics in which almost everyone agrees that reform is urgently needed, provided someone else pays, and their own group’s advantages remain untouched. If this sounds like an overreading of 1789, consider Macron’s own choice of title for the book that launched his presidential bid: Révolution. His presidency may not have proved entirely revolutionary, yet many of his larger prescriptions have aged well: governments around the world have embraced state-led industrial policy, greater strategic autonomy, and reduced dependence on fossil fuels, while distrust of American hegemony and calls for deeper European integration have gained traction as the postwar order fragments. Macron can also claim some credit for preserving, and in places renewing, France’s traditional strengths: an electricity system powered largely by nuclear energy, global leadership in luxury goods, tourism and hospitality, and a deep reservoir of scientific talent, now exemplified by Mistral, Europe’s closest thing to a world-class AI contender. A skilled diplomat and relentless advocate for the multilateral system, Macron has also taken principled stands on Ukraine, Gaza, Lebanon, debt relief for the world’s poorest countries, and climate change, while carefully navigating France’s relationships with America and China. France’s permanent seat on the U.N. Security Council gives it diplomatic weight far beyond the country’s economic size, a status French diplomats ironically describe as “flying first class on an economy ticket.” Yet neither these prescriptions nor these assets have been enough to reverse France’s decline over the past two decades. Perhaps what has been missing is a revolution not so much in policy as in political culture: a new social compact in which the sacrifices required to adjust to France’s reduced means and diminished relative power are accepted because the burdens are seen to be shared fairly. If such a revolution is to come, it is less likely to start in the Élysée or the ministries than among the students in the streets demanding to be heard. What We Know So Far About the Elementary School Shooting in Bend, Oregon

Update your preferences in Account Settings Police tape is placed outside Silver Rail Elementary school in Bend, Ore., on Oct. 15, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings A 16-year-old opened fire at an elementary school in Bend, Oregon, on Thursday, killing three children and a teacher before being shot dead by a community member, police said. Bend Police Chief Mike Krantz said in a press conference that the children killed at Silver Rail Elementary School were aged 5, 8, and 11. Also, a 7-year-old and a 54-year-old teacher were transported to St. Charles Bend hospital with critical injuries. Krantz said the suspect, a male city resident who has not yet been publicly identified, was spotted entering the school by a community member, who eventually pursued him into the school and shot him. The chief said the suspect forced his way into the school by firing at locked doors. Police did not speculate on the shooter’s motive and said they did not know whether the suspect knew anybody in the school or if he had attended. FBI Director Kash Patel posted on social media that the agency’s Portland field office is assisting in the investigation. “We will make every resource they need available,” he added. A vigil was held Thursday evening in the wake of the shooting. Community members grieve during a candlelight vigil at Drake Park in Bend, Ore., on Oct. 15, 2026. Mathieu Lewis-Rolland—Getty Images “A school should be a safe place where children can learn and feel secure,” Bend Mayor Melanie Kebler said at the same press conference the police chief spoke at. “What happened today should never happen.” Oregon Gov. Tina Kotek said at the same conference that her heart was “absolutely broken” over the incident: “No parent should have to have this kind of day, and no community should have to live through this kind of day.” She also thanked the community member who pursued the shooter. CNN’s analysis of incidents reported by the Gun Violence Archive, Education Week and Everytown for Gun Safety shows that the shooting at Silver Rail Elementary marks the 36th one on K-12 school grounds this year. It is the 52nd school shooting in the country this year. Krantz said that at 12:59 p.m., police received a 911 call from a person in a vehicle who reported being shot at multiple times by someone “with a long gun.” Multiple calls followed, reporting that a shooter had entered Silver Rail Elementary School, which is about 120 mi. southeast of Portland. The school enrolls more than 400 students from kindergarten to 5th grade. Krantz said police directed the school to go into lockdown at 1:01 p.m. and first responding officers arrived a minute later. Upon entering the school building, Krantz said, officers confirmed that the suspect was dead and found the four other people who died from their injuries, and their families have been notified. Krantz did not speak on the weapon used in the shooting. But an unidentified law enforcement official briefed on the shooting told the Associated Press that it was a pump-action shotgun purchased by the suspect’s mother. Oregon’s Republican candidate for governor, state senator Christine Drazan, posted on social media that her heart was with the affected families, students, and teaching staff. “We have to commit to protecting our kids,” she added. “We have to put a complete stop to shootings at our schools. This has to end.” Rep. Janelle Bynum, a Democrat who represents the district that includes Bend, said in a statement: “Our babies are supposed to do arts and crafts at school, not use their desks as cover from gunfire. They’re supposed to make friends, not mourn them.” U.S. Senator Ron Wyden (D, Ore.) lamented in a statement that the victims in Silver Rail Elementary School and their loved ones faced the “awful consequences” of another school shooting in the country. He added that the “best way to honor” them is to “at long last pass common-sense gun laws that reduce the risks of more children, parents, teachers and staff forced to live in fear of similar nightmares for their schools.”

This is part five of our series on popular constitutionalism, the idea that the Constitution belongs to the people—and not just to nine justices. Our listeners showed up with stories: a 62-year-old heading to law school, an 8th-grade teacher emailing her senators, an artist drawing the court, and a woman literally knitting the Constitution. Trump’s New Press Secretary Is an Incompetent Liar. She’s Also Perfect for the Job.
2026-10-15

Trump’s New Press Secretary Is an Incompetent Liar. She’s Also Perfect for the Job.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-15

Visit Original Website
This story is adapted from the Slatest newsletter. You can sign up here to get it in your inbox every day. There are two types of press secretaries in a Donald Trump administration: Those who lie slickly and those who lie embarrassingly. So far, Katie Zacharia, who debuted as the White House’s top spokesperson on Tuesday, is looking like the latter. The opening days of Zacharia’s tenure have been defined by questions over claims she has made about her background. She has a law degree from Pepperdine University (go Waves!) and has publicly identified herself as the “general counsel” for Fix California, a right-wing nonprofit. Trump has described Zacharia as an “attorney,” as did chyrons for TV appearances that Zacharia amplified on social media, CNN reports. Asked about her legal background on Tuesday, Zacharia claimed to have passed the bar “when I moved to China,” where she has also said she worked at a large Chinese law firm and as “outside counsel” to the International Finance Corporation and the World Bank. But foreign nationals aren’t allowed to take the bar in China, nor can they practice Chinese law. (She subsequently claimed to have misunderstood the reporter’s question.) The other shoe dropped last night, when the Wall Street Journal reported she has not passed the bar in the U.S., a revelation Zacharia seemingly confirmed on Thursday. But if Zacharia did misleadingly airbrush her credentials, it might make her the ideal spokesperson for an administration that regularly struggles to tell the truth. The president himself is the government’s main fount of misleading-at-best, mendacious-at-worst information, lies that the staffers around him then have to scramble to defend or make real. And if a press secretary willing to shamelessly deceive the public is Trump’s main requirement for the job, Zacharia is a perfect fit. That doesn’t mean she won’t run into trouble, of course. Beyond her apparently zhuzhed-up LinkedIn, Zacharia’s debut appearance as press secretary on Tuesday earned some brutal reviews. Zacharia spoke haltingly, read off notes for long stretches, took just three questions, and at one point said “my president” when she meant “my husband.” She hasn’t held an on-camera briefing since. Still, theater critics zinging Zacharia’s lack of polish are missing the point. Journalists have sometimes let themselves get ensorcelled by Trump’s more capable press secretaries, whose relative poise disguised a shameless willingness to deceive on his behalf. Some reporters praised Karoline Leavitt, whose departure in August cleared the way for Zacharia, for her professionalism while glossing over how she actually spent much of her tenure: misleading the public. It’s true that Zacharia’s job isn’t just to answer on-camera questions but also to work with reporters behind the scenes, credibly speak for the president, and paint the administration in a positive light. By that standard, her days may already be numbered. “Getting behind the podium is tough, but she got off on the wrong foot,” an administration official texted an MS NOW reporter after Zacharia’s debut. Still, the president appears to be sticking by her. “I thought she was very good,” he said Tuesday. Whether that holds is anyone’s guess. But in a perverse way, it could end up being a good thing for the rest of us if the lies coming from the briefing podium are as ham-fistedly delivered as the ones on Zacharia’s résumé. Separating rhetoric from reality has been one of this political era’s defining challenges, both for journalists and for the public. Having someone as clumsy as Zacharia for a mouthpiece makes it easier to tell which is which. I can’t speak for Katie Zacharia, but I’m willing to bet that you’ve earned some relaxation after a long day. For your evening entertainment, my colleagues suggest: Watching a new Oscar contender: La Bola Negra is a sprawling Spanish epic that grapples with the links between fascism and homophobia. It also features sparkling cameos from Penélope Cruz as a sultry cabaret dancer and Glenn Close as a Spanish-speaking American professor. The Odyssey has some competition for the Best Picture Oscar! Learning about gay rights before Stonewall: It’s LGBTQ+ history month, which means it’s a great moment to learn more about our queer forebears. While activists and heroes usually get most of the public’s attention, queer historian and author Timothy Stewart-Winter argues that it’s just as important to focus on the more complicated figures. He tells the story of LBJ’s right-hand man, who got arrested for bathroom cruising in 1964. The incident reveals a lot about what gay life looked like before Stonewall. Appreciating the best part of The Great British Bake Off: Cookbook author Nigella Lawson is a new judge on GBBO, and she is a breath of fresh air, according to ICYMI host Kate Lindsay. While the other judges sometimes treat dessert as something illicit, Lawson’s unabashed enthusiasm for baked goods is a welcome change. Celebrating 20 years of 30 Rock: Join Slate’s culture team in toasting Tina Fey’s iconic sitcom, which premiered 20 years ago this month. Revisit some of the show’s best jingles and parodies, appreciate Alec Baldwin’s singular performance as Jack Donaghy, and test your memory with this quiz. If you’re a 30 Rock fan looking for a similar show to enjoy, check out this ranked list of 14 spiritual successors that my colleagues and I put together. Happy watching, and see you tomorrow! The Worst Tech Bros and the Worst Christian Nationalists United Behind an Extreme Plan. Then It All Fell Apart.
2026-10-15

The Worst Tech Bros and the Worst Christian Nationalists United Behind an Extreme Plan. Then It All Fell Apart.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-15

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. President Donald Trump on Thursday walked back his administration’s plan to livestream the firing squad execution of Nidal Hasan, who killed 13 soldiers and shot dozens more at Fort Hood in 2009. The execution will move forward, and the victims’ immediate families will be able to view it in person, Trump said, but it won’t be broadcast for the public. Trump may be in retreat because, when Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth announced the planned livestream a week ago, the condemnation was swift, brutal, and broad—and not just among the administration’s typical critics. Joe Kent, who served as the director of the National Counterterrorism Center from 2025 to 2026, called it “a stain on our morality.” A daughter of one of the men Hasan killed said livestreaming the execution “stabs us in the back.” Even some conservative personalities shared their distaste. “Public executions are soul-twisting and we should not go down that road,” the conservative radio host Erick Erickson said on social media. Almost all Republican lawmakers who spoke about the idea did so with dismay. “Live-streaming executions is what jihadists do,” wrote Rep. Austin Scott of Georgia. Vice President J.D. Vance was lukewarm to the idea—declining to criticize it but saying he would not watch—and Trump was reportedly having doubts last week before he announced the cancellation Thursday. The opposition to the now-nixed livestream was not, however, universal: A strange coalition of the most ardently Christian nationalist evangelical leaders joined by a group of new-right tech bros made common cause over the idea that it’s not only acceptable, but good, for the federal government to ensure the public can watch agents of the state execute a murderer. These supporters represented a relatively new strain in conservative politics. These elements of the right, both religious and secular, are at the vanguard of anti-woke politics, pushing in online discussions for bolder and bolder ideas to remake American society into an imagined 1950s suburban fantasy. And while members of this coalition weren’t the only ones to support the idea of a livestreamed public execution, they were the ones most gleeful about it: The idea had already found traction in those circles, which act as an incubator for reactionary proposals. But when the rest of the MAGA world didn’t follow, and when Trump reversed course, the unpopularity of the idea became abundantly clear—and these Christian- and techno-nationalist elites were shown to be standing atop a largely illusory movement. It turns out that there are some things that are too far even for MAGA. In a post on Sunday, Palmer Luckey, the co-founder of Anduril Industries, wrote that it was “normal, good, and aligned with our natural and national order” to “revel in the death of those who truly deserve it.” Luckey, a donor to the Republican Party, is one of a number of Silicon Valley figures involved in the “Tech Right.” There are variations within this group’s politics, but Luckey has a background well-aligned with Hegseth’s hypermasculine code. Anduril is a military technology company, and it produces drones and surveillance systems. Musing on the idea of public executions, Luckey argued that “executions should be public and brutal” because “they are the ultimate example of the state’s monopoly on violence, and the only way to keep that from running out of control is to confront voters” with the act. Luckey wasn’t the only one to make this argument. Joe Lonsdale, a co-founder of the military tech firm Palantir, which helped Immigration and Customs Enforcement track down undocumented immigrants, shared Luckey’s mentality. “Our society today is too soft in ways that cause our fellow citizens harm,” he wrote. This wasn’t a new position for him. In December, after the U.S. military hit a boat containing alleged drug traffickers with a missile, Lonsdale chided “leftist schoolmarm leaders” who opposed the extrajudicial killings, accusing them of breeding “violence and evil in our civilization.” To create a peaceful society, he argued, “bold, virtuous men” should “deter evil” by hanging criminals in public. Such “masculine leadership” would “protect our most vulnerable.” These Tech Right figures talk of wanting to correct the ills of what they see as an overly bureaucratic, feminized society. Luckey and Lonsdale, like Vance, claim Palantir’s Peter Thiel as an influential mentor. Thiel has long expressed skepticism about democracy; he and the other tech oligarchs claim to think bigger and more experimentally. These champions of “free thought” landed on boldness and strength as guiding stars. And in this, they share common ground with the New Christian Right. In evangelical America, there is a small network of whiskey-drinking, bearded men with podcasts who have built brands around uncompromising Reformed theology. These men lead small churches but have much larger followings online, and they take provocative stances, such as ones against women’s suffrage or interracial marriage, that are often rewarded by the algorithm. The religion reporter Bob Smietana has dubbed them the “Jim Crow Theobros” because of their penchant for fantasizing about a racially unified—white—country. Like the Tech Right, these men see noble masculinity as the solution to most of society’s ills. They, like Elon Musk, were eager participants in the discourse about the “sin of empathy.” Both groups believe the most important thing is for strong men to protect the weak and vulnerable—sometimes from themselves. Women will be happier once they have their liberties stripped from them, these Christian Nationalists claim: They will find personal and spiritual fulfillment in being submissive wives and mothers. It’s a fulfillment they cannot find with careers or later-in-life pregnancies or the angst of feminism. Just as Thiel looms large in the Tech Right, these Christian Nationalists share a connection with the Idaho pastor Doug Wilson. Wilson, who is older and slightly less caustic than the more podcast-bro types but no less radical, leads a network of churches that includes Hegseth’s. Vance has his Silicon Valley friends. Hegseth has his Theobros. They all flirt with violence in pursuit of a more traditionally ordered society. “If we’re televising executions, let’s start the trials in DC,” the pastor and content creator Joel Webbon wrote on social media over the weekend, before suggesting that Mike Huckabee, the U.S. ambassador to Israel, should be executed for treason. The Arizona pastor Dale Partridge, another member of the New Christian Right, has proposed fixes for our society that include banning women from elected office and putting only white Christian men in leadership positions. When it came to the idea of public executions, he saw another promising idea. “If we executed criminals swiftly and publicly on a weekly basis,” he wrote, “America would be a safer and more beautiful place.” Hegseth believes in his own righteousness. He saw no problem with bragging about a public execution—something that conjures images of Jim Crow–era lynchings or Taliban sports stadium killings—because to him, righteousness is firmness and discipline. Hegseth has spoken of a wrathful and warlike God, as when he prayed for “overwhelming violence of action against those who deserve no mercy.” So he may have been caught by surprise when the news of the plan was met in the evangelical world with ambivalence, wariness, and, occasionally, alarm. Unlike Roman Catholics, who uniformly condemned the announcement, evangelicals have no theological qualms with the death penalty itself, and a solid majority of Americans still see capital punishment as justified for murder. But Walter Kim, the president of the National Association of Evangelicals, argued that the livestream “risks turning one of the state’s most solemn responsibilities into a public spectacle, promoting a culture of death, rather than a culture that upholds the dignity of every human being.” Bart Barber, who led the Southern Baptist Convention, the nation’s largest Protestant denomination, from 2022 to 2024, warned that “this culture isn’t serious enough to watch something like this,” predicting that “people will hoot, howl, and thump their chests instead, as though they just delivered the kill shot in [a first-person shooter] game.” Even House Speaker Mike Johnson, a die-hard Southern Baptist, seemed to indicate he thought it went too far, arguing that it would serve as a propaganda tool for terrorists. There were, of course, the people who have wedged themselves so firmly into the Trump machinery that they cannot break from it. A few edgy right-wing Christian political influencers bragged about the administration’s strength. As did a few more standard Christian Nationalist leaders, including the controversial charismatic preacher Mark Driscoll; the dean of the School of Theology at the Southern Baptist Theological Seminary, Andrew Walker; and Robert Jeffress, the pastor of one of the nation’s most influential Baptist megachurches. Jeffress, in particular, offered a theological defense of livestreamed executions: “Capital punishment is commanded by God in Genesis 9:6 to remind people of the sanctity of human life,” Jeffress said in a social media post. “The more people that can see that truth illustrated through a public execution, the better.” But these figures, members of the traditional evangelical elites, do not speak for the entire community of evangelical elites. They instead stood out against the other evangelicals who made pleas for a sense of decorum and human dignity. It seemed clear, in their jumping to defend such a cruel decision, that even as Trump’s political power wanes, they see their role as performing loyalty to Trump. They were different from the dissenting evangelical leaders, who, it seems, still hope to impose their own religious values onto the conservative public, rather than the other way around. And they were different from the Theobros, who hope to lead by pulling the entire MAGA movement to the right. So the true champions of public executions remain the Theobros and Silicon Valley oligarchs, the truest match for Hegseth’s wrathful, hypermasculine theology. For a brief moment, they scored a kind of symbolic victory. But in the end, the episode backfired on them. Gleefully supporting public executions didn’t make these men look strong. It made them instead seem too grotesquely bloodthirsty for even today’s conservative movement. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. The People Who Asked Zohran Mamdani Why He’s Killing Kids Explain Themselves
2026-10-15

The People Who Asked Zohran Mamdani Why He’s Killing Kids Explain Themselves

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-15

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. Libby Lenkinski was walking toward Union Square in New York City last week on the evening of Oct. 7, when she first encountered a sea of Palestinian flags. Lenkinski is an Israeli American who has spent more than two decades working against Israel’s oppression of Palestinians and helping lead organizations that pushed for democracy and equality there. She also lost friends in Hamas’ Oct. 7, 2023, attack. She had come that night to read a eulogy for an Israeli killed in Gaza at a vigil that would also remember Palestinians killed, an event organized by Israelis for Peace, a group of left-wing Israeli expatriates. She wasn’t expecting to find herself on the opposite side of a barricade from people she had spent much of her adult life marching alongside. “I couldn’t quite hear what was happening. I couldn’t quite decipher what I was seeing. I was like, is this part of it or is this something else?” she recalled. Then she noticed someone wearing what appeared to be a Hamas headband. When Mayor Zohran Mamdani appeared at the vigil, the protesters began to shout at him: “Zohran, you can’t hide, you support genocide.” “Traitor, traitor, come on out, you sold all your people out.” “Mamdani, what do you say? How many kids did you kill today?” What exactly was this about? For Lenkinski, the disruption was painful on several levels. “What happened that night at the vigil was brutal,” she said. “It was megaphones from before we started until after we left. You couldn’t get through a single testimony—including from families in Gaza—without being disrupted by these chants.” When video of the incident began to spread, it was also confounding far beyond New York. Conservatives reacted with glee; many liberals scratched their heads. Had the left flank already turned on Mamdani, not even a year in? It’s clear that the majority of the New York City voters who brought Mamdani into office have not, in fact, turned on him. In a Suffolk University poll in September, some 56 percent of voters said he was doing an “excellent” or “good” job, and another 17 percent rated him “fair.” More than 6 in 10 voters said he was performing better than they expected. But those numbers may not reflect some discontent in the left-most movement where the future mayor earned the early enthusiasm that helped vault him into office. It wasn’t just one rally, either. There had been rumblings of frustration in criminal justice circles over policing, for which Mamdani has largely retained the status quo in New York. After Immigration and Customs Enforcement agents shot a man several times in a vehicle while a 5-year-old child sat in the back seat, Mamdani’s self-described “political home,” the Democratic Socialists of America, demanded the removal of NYPD Commissioner Jessica Tisch if she did not “end NYPD complicity with ICE.” Even one of Mamdani’s handpicked congressional candidates joined the call. When a reporter asked Mamdani about that this week, he replied flatly, “Jessica Tisch is the police commissioner. I have no plans to change that.” (Mamdani has demanded ICE leave New York City.) If Mamdani has so far shown a pragmatic streak in how he governs that can feel at odds with the loftier promises of his campaign, some friction with his most hard-line voters might be inevitable. But I wondered specifically what motived the protestors on Oct. 7, who managed to make international headlines. A City University of New York law student who helped organize the march agreed to speak with me on the condition I not publish his name. He said the demonstration began as a student walkout, with protesters converging on City Hall to demand action on Palestinian rights. They marched to Union Square after learning Mamdani would be attending the vigil. The law student objected in particular to Mamdani’s response to protests against West Bank property sales he said were promoted at New York synagogues, arguing that the mayor had defended policing those demonstrations without doing enough to stop the sales, along with similar protests against CUNY’s investments in Israel. He was also unhappy with the mayor’s Strategy to Combat Antisemitism, announced in September, which he said conflated anti-Zionism with antisemitism in some pro-Palestinian groups. “Why are we targeting Mamdani? Because Mamdani won on Palestine. The only reason he won this election is because of Palestine. If it were not for Palestine, he would not have won. And now that he is mayor, it’s our job to pressure him,” the organizer told me. “He’s going to make mistakes. He’s going to fall for a lot of pressure where he’s going to accept Zionist demands. And when he does do that, which he has done repeatedly, it’s up to us, the people, to pressure him to choose his principles over his position.” He had no regrets about pursuing Mamdani to the vigil. “I think it was a victory,” he said. I asked whether he knew the vigil had included testimonies from families in Gaza, which Lenkinski said the protesters had drowned out. “I—I did not hear that,” he replied. I noted Mamdani’s explanation that the vigil had mourned both Israeli and Palestinian deaths. “That just shows that he’s appealing to both sides and he’s not taking a principled stance,” he said. The law student told me Mamdani had never gone far enough to earn his vote, but protesters with him that day included an ex-Mamdani intern. Nerdeen Kiswani, the Palestinian American co-founder of Within Our Lifetime, one of New York’s most confrontational pro-Palestinian organizations, applauded the protest and said more should be on the way. “I was critical of him from when he first announced his campaign, and I don’t think I shifted,” she told me. “I have been consistent.” Many supporters, she said, voted for Mamdani expecting to hold him accountable once he took office. “He has executive power, so rhetoric is not enough. He has to take action to make material change.” “I’m glad that he’s in this position,” she said. “It’s precisely why we need to keep protesting him and people like him.” Kiswani said unease over Mamdani’s handling of the NYPD was also fomenting some of the pushback, at least in her slice of the political spectrum. “No mayor has retained the same police commissioner from the administration prior to theirs since 1964,” she said, referring to Tisch, who was elevated under the previous mayor, Eric Adams. “All of these frustrations that people have had for a while exploded.” It is fair to say not everyone saw Oct. 7’s events in New York the same way. Rania Batrice, a Palestinian American political strategist who was a deputy campaign manager for Bernie Sanders’ 2016 presidential campaign, was disturbed. She knew some of the Jewish activists at the vigil and considered them among the most committed opponents of Israel’s occupation. “My first reaction was: This is absolutely ridiculous,” she told me. She described the attacks on Mamdani as “wildly unhelpful.” “This whole idea that people aren’t allowed to mourn or remember is really troubling to me,” she said. Mourning Israeli civilians, she argued, did not negate the suffering of Palestinians or diminish the struggle against Israel’s government. For Batrice, the danger wasn’t simply that the confrontation looked terrible. It was that years of political marginalization could make activists so sensitive to perceived betrayal that they might fail to recognize the gains they had achieved. “The reality is, you have to look at the totality here, not a single moment,” she said. “You can have feelings about a single moment, but that doesn’t negate everything else he’s done and said. He’s shown up in the toughest circumstances. I think it fractures the movement, honestly. And that’s not something any of us need right now.” Debbie Almontaser, a longtime Muslim community organizer who helped elect Mamdani, wasn’t surprised by the confrontation. She has spent decades fighting for the kind of political influence New York’s Muslim communities now have. (In 2007, she was pushed out as the founding principal of the city’s first Arabic-language public school amid a campaign of anti-Muslim attacks; a federal agency later found that the city had discriminated against her.) But even while she understood it, she didn’t necessarily see its wisdom. “This happens with every elected official who ran on specific promises that, in some shape or form, did not quickly materialize,” Almontaser said. “He is the mayor of over 8 million people in New York City, and he is accountable for their safety, security, and well-being. And there needs to be room for him to be able to be true to his role and his position.” Almontaser had a measured perspective on criticism the mayor has received, not just from pro-Palestinian activists but from pro-Israel critics as well: “For decades, people who’ve been in political positions—whether it’s in the Republican Party or the Democratic Party—have always had one-sided position to support Israel in a way that has alienated, isolated, and marginalized the Palestinian people, the Palestinian cause.” She remembered a time when Arab and Muslim New Yorkers struggled to find elected officials willing to acknowledge their experiences at all. Now one of their own had become mayor, and some Jewish New Yorkers were experiencing a sense of alienation that she recognized. “Nobody should feel that way,” she said. Just eight days before the vigil, Almontaser had publicly endorsed Mamdani’s new strategy to combat antisemitism—the same initiative that had helped provoke the student protest. Lenkinski had welcomed it, too. Both women had spent years advocating for Palestinian rights while maintaining relationships with Jewish organizations. What some activists saw as a concession to Zionist political pressure they considered part of the difficult work of building a city in which different communities could feel safe. Mamdani, for his part, has been steadfast in his response to the criticism. Asked about being called a traitor and worse, he told reporters, “I am the mayor of New York City, and this is what I signed up for.” Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. Networking problems after the October 2026 Windows update? NTLM might be to blame
2026-10-16

Networking problems after the October 2026 Windows update? NTLM might be to blame

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
Legacy protocol is going to be axed, and enforcement by default will highlight how migration is going The October 2026 Windows security update may break networking for some customers who are still clinging to long-deprecated NTLMv1 security. A seemingly minor change in the update could have major implications for administrators and enterprises that haven't fully moved away from the legacy technology. Before the October 2026 update, Windows permitted NTLMv1-derived credentials for Single Sign-On and generated warning events. After the update, requests to use NTLMv1-derived credentials are blocked. It comes down to the BlockNtlmSSO registry value. If it hasn't been set, Windows will now use Enforce mode (1) instead of Audit mode (0), thus blocking requests and potentially impacting Wi-Fi, Ethernet, and VPN authentication, and therefore connections. The update does not set the value of BlockNtlmSSO, and administrators can set it to 0 to temporarily allow NTLMv1-derived Single Sign-On credentials. However, Microsoft noted, "Using Audit mode reduces the protection provided by Enforce mode and should only be considered as a scoped, temporary security exception." To be clear, this is not a bug and is by design. The addition of NTLM to the deprecation list occurred in 2024, and Microsoft added audit logs for NTLMv1 usage in late August 2025. The Windows giant has made no secret of its desire to ditch the legacy technology, stating in 2023, "Our end goal is eliminating the need to use NTLM at all." The problem is NTLM's longevity. It appeared in 1993 with Windows NT 3.1, and removing it from enterprise environments has proven tricky, even though it is somewhat insecure by modern standards. Microsoft removed it from Windows 11 24H2 and Windows Server 2025, but some remnants can still be found, notably when using MS-CHAPv2-based connections, including PEAP-MSCHAPv2, and EAP-MSCHAPv2, in a domain-joined environment. Hence the introduction of the audit mode in 2025, to help administrators spot where the technology was still being used. It is now 2026, and Microsoft has taken the next step. If Credential Guard is not enabled and BlockNtlmSSO is not set to Audit mode (0), the use of NTLMv1-derived credentials will be blocked as Microsoft works "to finalize the disablement of NTLMv1." There is no indication of how long the BlockNtlmSSO workaround (setting the value to Audit mode) will work - most likely until the next major Windows release. In September, Microsoft said, "In-market versions of Windows client and Windows Server will continue to support NTLM." However, it also stated, "Our long-term objective is a Windows platform that doesn't need NTLM at all." As such, if the message wasn't already clear, the move to enforce it by default makes it impossible to miss. The technology still exists in the operating system, but it is on its way out and will be removed in a future Windows release. Problems with networking after the October 2026 Windows security update could therefore signal that migration plans are not as far along, or as complete, as administrators might have thought. ® Legacy protocol is going to be axed, and enforcement by default will highlight how migration is going SPONSORED POST: As AI hunger soars, NTT Global Data Centers finds it's permits, not parts, that could cap the datacenter boom PARTNER CONTENT: A million reasons why advanced computing matters for genomics The inevitable election-themed cyberattacks are whirring again, as the feds issue PSA about the ones to watch out for New models arrive so fast, we already have more new tech than it's possible to understand in a career Legal eagles still plodding along with some operations, but paper and e-notices delayed until further notice Browser maker using trusted hardware to protect conversations with GLM 5.3 Flash Microsoft's Go rewrite gets some competition, with a developer claiming his port runs twice as fast Silicon giant budgets up to $64B for expansion while keeping an eye on construction progress and power supplies Torvalds says contributors must understand their code, whatever tools they use What are they puffing? NTT Global Data Centers weighs the price tag of not building fast enough
2026-10-16

NTT Global Data Centers weighs the price tag of not building fast enough

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
SPONSORED POST: As AI hunger soars, NTT Global Data Centers finds it's permits, not parts, that could cap the datacenter boom The next 200-megawatt data center permitted in your county probably won't look like the buildings you remember from a decade ago. It will occupy the footprint of two or three football fields and draw more power than most nearby industrial parks combined. It will also likely trigger a town hall meeting before the first pad is poured. That collision between AI-driven demand and physical reality is what NTT Global Data Centers set out to quantify in its new AI Stress Test Report. The report models three growth trajectories through 2030 and stress-tests each against the constraints operators hit, such as land availability, water consumption, specialized labor and more. The middle (baseline) scenario projects annual demand growth of about 23 percent, which pencils out to roughly $3.8 trillion of additional investment in digital infrastructure. Ramp to the accelerated case and the figure climbs to $5.7 trillion. Bruno Berti, CTO at NTT Global Data Centers, notes that he has watched the size envelope stretch beyond recognition. "We used to build ten-megawatt buildings, smaller buildings, to support some of these applications," says Berti. "But now we're building buildings that are hundreds of megawatts, double the size, sizes of two, maybe sometimes three football fields." Ask most operators what will bottleneck AI infrastructure and you'll hear GPUs, transformers, switchgear. NTT Global Data Centers’ stress test suggests something more mundane: paperwork, planning and process. Power availability and deployment timelines, not fiber capacity, are the gating factors in the scenarios modeled. Berti puts it plainly: it doesn't matter how quickly you can get GPUs if you can't get a permit to put them anywhere. That reframes the strategy question. Berti argues against a "build, baby, build" reflex, preferring a portfolio approach that treats capacity expansion as a system-wide coordination problem rather than a land race. Two objections come up in almost every community consultation, according to Berti: data centers waste water, and they steal power from the grid. Both are misconceptions and come from an older generation of facilities. Modern designs, he says, use liquid cooling technology, which removes heat more effectively, eliminating excess water waste. Additionally, operators plan the new class of hundred-megawatt-plus sites in partnership with utilities years in advance, often with on-site solar, wind or other renewable generation that can help stabilize the grid rather than drain it. His conclusion is blunt: transparency builds trust. So, the industry has to stop treating what it does as a secret and start to build that trust through education and partnership if it wants the permits it will need through the decade to turn capacity into reliable AI infrastructure that can benefit communities, policymakers and businesses alike. Uncle Scam Wants You … to cough up your cash and personal data, FBI warns ahead of midterms
2026-10-16

Uncle Scam Wants You … to cough up your cash and personal data, FBI warns ahead of midterms

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
The inevitable election-themed cyberattacks are whirring again, as the feds issue PSA about the ones to watch out for The FBI is once again warning US citizens to be on the lookout for the scams that are so often rife around election season. With November’s midterms fast approaching, the FBI said scammers are known to use the names, imagery, and slogans of high-profile candidates for a range of frauds. These are often related to making donations and selling merchandise, but can also be geared toward stealing personal data for use in follow-on scams. And of course, more recently, fraudsters’ efforts can be all the more convincing given the rise in AI use and deepfake technology, which can also be used to more accurately imitate the likenesses of candidates and other officials. Campaign investment pool schemes are among the four big frauds the FBI wants the public to watch out for over the next few weeks. These involve scammers convincing their marks to pay into a campaign pool, which ostensibly pays out to all of those who donated after the candidate wins. More commonly, these types of pooled investment scams are based on collective investments into stocks and other appreciating assets. The FBI did not provide details about these campaign pools’ investment incentives or how victims are supposedly able to increase their stake by donating to the cause, but did say that no genuine campaign donations appreciate over time. Another common donation-based scam to be wary of is the one that convinces victims to fund a fake political action committee (PAC), one that collects supporters’ cash and promises to hand it to their favored party or candidate. Instead of forwarding money to a specific political campaign, however, the scammers pocket it all for themselves. Fraudsters are also known for claiming to sell merchandise related to political campaigns – orders that ultimately go unfulfilled. These constitute non-delivery scams, which rank in the top five genres of internet cons, according to the Internet Crime Complaint Center’s (IC3) most recent annual report. The same report put phishing at the number one spot in that same list, and the classic cyberattack features on the FBI’s list of midterm-related scams, too. Creating a sense of urgency, scammers will text victims claiming they have not yet registered to vote, and asking them to follow a link to a webpage where they can enter their details and complete the process, which of course leads to identity theft. While the FBI doesn’t include this detail in its advisory, Texans should be especially wary of these communication attempts given the recent computer issues that led to a massive backlog in registration applications. As of September 22, 226, around 600,000 applications were allegedly in limbo after a website flaw failed to record voters’ intentions to vote, and therefore did not receive registration forms from the Texas Secretary of State (SOS), according to a lawsuit [PDF] filed by voter registration organizations last week. However, spokesperson for the SOS’ office Alicia Pierce told USA Today that the backlog was only in the tens of thousands and that as of this week it was all-clear. Scammers may nevertheless continue to capitalize on the public awareness of the backlog when devising the lures for their phishing campaigns. The feds advised the public to be wary of these kinds of communications, and verify all political organizations, such as PACs, via the government’s election commission website. Although some scam PACs can appear on the website as registered, you can usually discern the suspicious ones when they report high expenses for matters such as consulting, fundraising, and other admin costs. The FBI's advisory follows separate action taken by X, which on Wednesday evening warned that influence campaigns originating from Iran, Russia, and China were targeting US voters, amplifying content across the political spectrum. The social media platform responded by nuking more than 13,000 accounts linked to the foreign interference attempts. ® Legacy protocol is going to be axed, and enforcement by default will highlight how migration is going SPONSORED POST: As AI hunger soars, NTT Global Data Centers finds it's permits, not parts, that could cap the datacenter boom PARTNER CONTENT: A million reasons why advanced computing matters for genomics The inevitable election-themed cyberattacks are whirring again, as the feds issue PSA about the ones to watch out for New models arrive so fast, we already have more new tech than it's possible to understand in a career Legal eagles still plodding along with some operations, but paper and e-notices delayed until further notice Browser maker using trusted hardware to protect conversations with GLM 5.3 Flash Microsoft's Go rewrite gets some competition, with a developer claiming his port runs twice as fast Silicon giant budgets up to $64B for expansion while keeping an eye on construction progress and power supplies Torvalds says contributors must understand their code, whatever tools they use What are they puffing? Attack on BAE-managed system disrupts US bankruptcy courts
2026-10-16

Attack on BAE-managed system disrupts US bankruptcy courts

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
Legal eagles still plodding along with some operations, but paper and e-notices delayed until further notice The US' bankruptcy courts are currently down owing to what officials are calling a “cybersecurity incident.” The Administrative Office of the US Courts said the websites and systems that support the Bankruptcy Noticing Center (BNC), which manages electronic comms related to bankruptcy cases, were pulled offline while the vendor investigates. BAE Systems runs the affected systems and websites on behalf of the Federal Judiciary, and is “working to restore operations,” according to an announcement by the US Courts on Thursday. “As a result of the incident, all access to the BNC systems and websites that BAE operates on behalf of the judiciary and the related bankruptcy noticing process are unavailable at this time, including bnc.uscourts.gov, bankruptcynotices.uscourts.gov, notices.uscourts.gov, and the National Creditor Registration Service," it said. “The incident has also halted the BNC’s production and distribution of bankruptcy notices for bankruptcy courts.” While the public notice was published on Thursday, the issues affecting the BNC systems began a few days earlier, on October 9, according to the Bankruptcy Court of the Eastern District of Texas. Alongside its own announcement, the same court published a general order indicating that for any case filed on or after September 14 with an active motion to stay, that delay would be extended by an additional 30 days. The court said it will continue to accept filings, open cases, and process documents, but said there may be a delay in the entry and service of notices and orders until the issues are resolved. The courts' ability to process electronic and paper bankruptcy notices is affected, and it applies to federal bankruptcy courts across the country. A spokesperson at BAE told The Register: "BAE Systems, Inc. operates the Bankruptcy Noticing Center (BNC) on behalf of the Administrative Office of the U.S. Courts (AOUSC). We are aware of a security incident that is isolated to the BNC system and have taken immediate steps to address it. We are coordinating closely with AOUSC on the response." The disruption comes at a difficult time for the US public sector, which has struggled with multiple recent cyberattacks. ShinyHunters' attack on the FBI in September is doubtless the most high-profile of these, although the Arizona Judicial Branch confirmed an attack on the state’s court systems at around the same time. Officials said the “criminal hackers or their bots” were responsible for stealing personal data and backup court files. The US Courts also said in August 2025 that it was stepping up its cybersecurity measures in response to increased targeting of its case management system. ® Legacy protocol is going to be axed, and enforcement by default will highlight how migration is going SPONSORED POST: As AI hunger soars, NTT Global Data Centers finds it's permits, not parts, that could cap the datacenter boom PARTNER CONTENT: A million reasons why advanced computing matters for genomics The inevitable election-themed cyberattacks are whirring again, as the feds issue PSA about the ones to watch out for New models arrive so fast, we already have more new tech than it's possible to understand in a career Legal eagles still plodding along with some operations, but paper and e-notices delayed until further notice Browser maker using trusted hardware to protect conversations with GLM 5.3 Flash Microsoft's Go rewrite gets some competition, with a developer claiming his port runs twice as fast Silicon giant budgets up to $64B for expansion while keeping an eye on construction progress and power supplies Torvalds says contributors must understand their code, whatever tools they use What are they puffing? www.theregister.com - Articles
British watchdog seeks tougher terms around Microsoft's dominance of business software
2026-10-16

British watchdog seeks tougher terms around Microsoft's dominance of business software

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
Another reason for Donald Trump to hate British regulation The UK's competition watchdog wants to designate Microsoft with strategic market status (SMS) in business software, allowing the regulator it to intervene to level the playing field, and says it is considering other areas for investigation. Regulators around the world are watching to see what the UK decides in this case, with bodies such as the US Federal Trade Commission (FTC) and the European Commission mulling similar actions. The Competition and Markets Authority (CMA) is opening a consultation on whether to tag the Redmond software giant with strategic market status in respect of its portfolio of business software applications. This follows an investigation the regulator started back in May into Microsoft’s software portfolio including Word, Excel, Outlook, PowerPoint and Teams, plus its security, server and operating systems. The watchdog is also probing whether Microsoft enjoys a position of “substantial and entrenched market power” through the suite of software that could be seen as stifling competition and harming buyer choice. It lays out its proposed decision in a document [PDF] and is inviting comments that must be submitted by November 13. The CMA will make its final decision by February 13, 2027. The CMA makes it clear the company holds a strong position in each of the five areas it investigated (personal computer operating systems, productivity software suites, security software, server operating systems and relational database management systems), and that customers face barriers when switching providers – limiting their choice and weakening the ability of other software developers to offer rival products. Its proposal document notes Microsoft has been by far the largest provider of business software in the UK for decades. It finds that many customers view Microsoft's portfolio as a unified software environment, forming an end-to-end, integrated stack which makes for easier technical integration between products, with discounted pricing for buying whole bundles of applications together. This makes customers less likely to adopt products from alternative providers that may offer similar or superior functionality, as doing so would duplicate licensing costs for products already in their Microsoft bundle, the CMA says. “Organizations should be able to choose the products that work best for them, switch provider when they want to and combine services from different suppliers. We want to ensure businesses have the freedom to choose how they use Microsoft’s products alongside alternatives,” said CMA chief exec Sarah Cardell. Finding that the criteria for SMS designation are met would not imply or assume wrongdoing on Microsoft’s part, the regulator stresses. A separate document [PDF] spells out the potential interventions that the CMA could make. These include ensuring that Microsoft’s technical design choices do not restrict the ability to use other providers; that commercial arrangements (like bundling) do not limit effective competition; and preventing Microsoft from using its market power in business software to distort competition in adjacent activities, such as cloud services. Cloud providers previously complained that Microsoft's licensing terms make it more costly for companies to deploy Windows Server instances and services such as SQL Server on non-Microsoft clouds. The proposed SMS designation grew out of the CMA’s investigations into whether the big global players were having a negative impact on the cloud services market in the UK. This largely fizzled out with the watchdog securing undertakings from Microsoft and Amazon Web Services (AWS) that they would work to reduce barriers to switching between clouds and operating multi-cloud deployments. In an update, the CMA says that more can be done to address barriers to switching between cloud providers and using multiple cloud services, and it will continue to work with AWS and Microsoft to address them. It also says it its looking into whether customers can freely choose the best AI tools, and whether alternative AI providers have a level playing field to compete against Copilot. In a statement sent to The Register, Microsoft said it does not share the CMA’s views in its proposed decision on business software solutions. “All our products - including Copilot - are open and interoperable, giving customers the ability to use them alongside competing services and choose solutions that work best for them at attractive prices. We will continue engaging constructively to ensure UK customers and businesses carry on benefiting from robust competition,” a spokesperson told us. However, some pressure groups were quick to signal their approval. The Browser Choice Alliance said it welcomes the CMA’s proposed decision, adding that: “BCA members have long experienced how Microsoft leverages its market power from Windows and productivity software to push users towards its Edge browser, even where they’re trying to use an alternative.” The Open Cloud Coalition, which counts Google as a member, said: “Microsoft’s restrictive software licensing practices distort competition in the cloud, harm choice, and increase costs. This once-in-a-generation opportunity to end these practices can strengthen competition and lower customer costs, if the CMA acts quickly and decisively.” ® Legacy protocol is going to be axed, and enforcement by default will highlight how migration is going SPONSORED POST: As AI hunger soars, NTT Global Data Centers finds it's permits, not parts, that could cap the datacenter boom PARTNER CONTENT: A million reasons why advanced computing matters for genomics The inevitable election-themed cyberattacks are whirring again, as the feds issue PSA about the ones to watch out for New models arrive so fast, we already have more new tech than it's possible to understand in a career Legal eagles still plodding along with some operations, but paper and e-notices delayed until further notice Browser maker using trusted hardware to protect conversations with GLM 5.3 Flash Microsoft's Go rewrite gets some competition, with a developer claiming his port runs twice as fast Silicon giant budgets up to $64B for expansion while keeping an eye on construction progress and power supplies Torvalds says contributors must understand their code, whatever tools they use What are they puffing?
