Want your AI chats to stay private? Brave TEEs up end-to-end encryption
2026-10-16

Want your AI chats to stay private? Brave TEEs up end-to-end encryption

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
Browser maker using trusted hardware to protect conversations with GLM 5.3 Flash Brave Software has set up a secure space so those using its browser can converse with the company's AI assistant in private. At a time when AI agents like Meta's Muse ask for extensive access to personal information, and AI companies repurpose conversations for training, this amounts to swimming against the tide. Following up on an experiment launched last November to implement a confidential channel for LLM conversations with the help of NEAR AI's trusted execution environment (TEE), the browser maker has connected its Leo AI assistant to Z.ai's GLM 5.3 Flash under the protection of end-to-end encryption. The result is that Brave users can submit a prompt to Leo that gets encrypted on-device and sent over the network using Oblivious HTTP, a protocol for separating network requests from associated content. Brave's AI Gateway forwards the encrypted query to the TEE without passing along the user's IP address. After Brave verifies the enclave's attestation, the browser encrypts the query using a key bound to the TEE. The TEE decrypts the request, runs it through the AI model, and sends back an encrypted response that gets decrypted by the receiving browser. "In 2014, I argued in 'Trust but Verify' that users shouldn't have to place blind trust in the software they rely on," said Brendan Eich, co-founder and CEO of Brave, in a statement. "They should be able to verify it; this is a user-first principle. AI has raised the stakes, yet too much of it still runs on 'trust me bro' promises, often at the user's expense." NEAR AI's confidential AI pipeline is built with Intel TDX and Nvidia Confidential Compute. It includes end-to-end encryption that keeps Brave from knowing what was asked and NEAR AI from knowing who did the asking during the network hop. Data isolation prevents the prompt or response from being read during processing. Responses are signed in the TEE and there's attestation so users can confirm there's no model bait-and-switch. Leo's GLM 5.3 Flash brain may be isolated in this scenario, but Brave's AI bot can still conduct searches from within the TEE via the Brave Search API's LLM context endpoint. And any searches done this way transit via the TEE rather than the user's browser, which keeps the user's IP address out of the picture. The implementation isn't quite complete: Brave intends to work on moving the verification step into the browser and to publish a log of TEE keys, and to provide enclave attestation for exact model weights loaded into memory, among other things. Brave is not the only company trying to make AI less of a data exposure risk. Apple, AWS, Google, Nvidia, and Microsoft all have some form of private cloud compute infrastructure for AI. Then there's Prem AI, which promises encrypted inference, not to mention providers advertising zero data retention, which isn't as verifiable. One way to ensure privacy while using AI is to run inference on local hardware using an open weight model, assuming the model and the machine running it haven't been compromised. ® Legacy protocol is going to be axed, and enforcement by default will highlight how migration is going SPONSORED POST: As AI hunger soars, NTT Global Data Centers finds it's permits, not parts, that could cap the datacenter boom PARTNER CONTENT: A million reasons why advanced computing matters for genomics The inevitable election-themed cyberattacks are whirring again, as the feds issue PSA about the ones to watch out for New models arrive so fast, we already have more new tech than it's possible to understand in a career Legal eagles still plodding along with some operations, but paper and e-notices delayed until further notice Browser maker using trusted hardware to protect conversations with GLM 5.3 Flash Microsoft's Go rewrite gets some competition, with a developer claiming his port runs twice as fast Silicon giant budgets up to $64B for expansion while keeping an eye on construction progress and power supplies Torvalds says contributors must understand their code, whatever tools they use What are they puffing? Analogue is re-releasing its SNES and Genesis consoles with some nice upgrades
2026-10-16

Analogue is re-releasing its SNES and Genesis consoles with some nice upgrades

The Verge | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
The Super Nt Plus and Mega Sg Plus add USB-C and USB-A ports plus built-in wireless connections for controllers. The Super Nt Plus and Mega Sg Plus add USB-C and USB-A ports plus built-in wireless connections for controllers. Analogue announced new versions of its Super Nt and Mega Sg retro consoles today with a “reworked industrial design” and built-in wireless connectivity for controllers and accessories. Like the original versions, the new Super Nt Plus and Mega Sg Plus are able to play Nintendo and Sega Genesis cartridges using an FPGA processor that perfectly recreates the functionality of Nintendo and Sega’s 16-bit consoles without the need for emulation. Although neither of the new consoles allows SNES or Genesis titles to be played in 4K like the Analogue 3D does with Nintendo 64 titles, the 1080p Super Nt and Mega Sg Plus add a USB-C port so you can power them with the cables you already use with your phone and mobile devices, and an additional USB-A port for other peripherals. Both consoles also introduce a built-in Bluetooth or a low-latency 2.4Ghz wireless connection for connecting to gamepads without requiring an additional controller port dongle. Wired controllers are still an option, and the upgraded consoles can both be powered up remotely using whatever gamepad is connected as the primary controller. Analogue also says game cartridges can now be hot swapped without having to completely power down either console after quitting the game through a menu. Both the Super Nt Plus and Mega Sg Plus will be available for preorder starting on October 19th at 9AM PT/11AM ET for $219.99 with shipping expected to start sometime in December, later this year. The Verge
A week with RTX Spark: everything we’ve learned using Nvidia’s new laptops
2026-10-16

A week with RTX Spark: everything we’ve learned using Nvidia’s new laptops

The Verge | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
Nvidia’s new Arm-based laptop chip is an exciting new challenger, but exclusively for the high-end.﻿ If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement. If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement. Nvidia’s RTX Spark N1X processor is finally here, and we’ve been collectively testing a small fortune’s worth of powerful laptops sporting this new “superchip.” RTX Spark is designed to be a high-performing Arm-based SoC for laptops and small desktop computers, rivaling the best from Apple, Intel, AMD, and Qualcomm. It has loads of cores, Nvidia RTX Blackwell graphics, and enough power and unified memory to run sizable local AI models. The flagship placement is inside Microsoft’s Surface Laptop Ultra with a staggering 128GB of unified memory, which our own Tom Warren fully reviewed. Sean Hollister has been testing a 64GB version of the Asus ProArt P16, which I’ve also been testing in its top-spec 128GB config. Since I’m the most spoiled of the lot, I also have a 64GB Lenovo Yoga 9n 2-in-1 and a 32GB HP OmniBook Ultra in for testing. All of these laptops have the same N1X chip with 20 CPU cores and 6,144 GPU CUDA cores, and they’re all wildly expensive — ranging from the HP OmniBook at $3,199.99 to the Asus ProArt’s $6,999.99. There’s an 18-core / 5,120 CUDA N1X chip in lower cost configs, but that only gets as cheap as $2,599.99 for a Surface Laptop Ultra or a smaller Asus ProArt P14 with 24GB of RAM. These are big and expensive laptops built on new chips. They’re meant for developers, coders, and content creators who might also want the ability to use local AI and even play games using the latest Nvidia tech like DLSS 5. There’s a lot to test, and we have not had a ton of time to test it all. So below you won’t find an exhaustive scored review of any one laptop, but instead our impressions and thoughts from three writers using five laptops for a week. Is Nvidia’s “superchip” really all that super? Are Apple, Intel, AMD, and Qualcomm officially on notice? Have any of us gotten any sleep? Antonio Di Benedetto: Windows on Arm is no longer a wholly subpar experience like it was a few years ago. Thanks in large part to the push around Qualcomm’s Snapdragon X chips, lots of development has been put into native Arm-based apps for Windows. And many x86 apps work fine via Microsoft’s Prism emulation layer. Adobe has had native Arm versions of Photoshop and Premiere for some time now, and even though my precious Lightroom Classic is still an x86-based app I don’t notice any issues using it on Arm. At this point, you’ve gotta be relying on specialized software that’s exclusively x86 based for Windows on Arm to be a non-starter — or you want to freely play any games out there without any worry of incompatibilities. Sean Hollister: Every so often, there’s that moment when an app takes longer to load than it should, and I wonder whether it’ll load at all. Even then, it typically loads. Occasionally, it takes more than one try. My Evernote notes disappeared a few times one day when I alt-tabbed away, but a force refresh fixed that. And apps don’t seem to respect my system preference for dark mode. But I largely agree with Antonio. I made an RTX Spark my daily driver, with all my typical work and personal apps — Signal, Discord, Audacity, CapCut and Evernote, Irfanview, Slack, ScreenToGif and more — and nothing has failed me so far. I never once needed to think whether I was running an Arm or an x86 app. The real hitches came with gaming and AI, as you’ll read below. Tom Warren: I’ve been using a Windows on Arm laptop with a Qualcomm chip daily for the past two years and things have improved to the point that I can’t tell if an app is native or not anymore. That’s the same story on the RTX Spark laptops, and I feel like Nvidia’s entry into this category will only improve the story for creative apps and gaming. TW: Windows on Arm never looked like a serious gaming platform before now. While there still aren’t many native Arm games, Nvidia’s GPU helps bruteforce through the emulation layer. Your mileage in gaming is going to vary depending on the title, but for the most part I’ve found that medium settings, running at 1440p and DLSS performance, will get you to a stable 60fps in most games. I mostly game on my desktop, where I’m spoiled by using an RTX 5090 on a 32-inch 4K screen every day with maxed-out game settings, but I didn’t mind the look of medium settings on a 15-inch display. I was able to run Black Myth: Wukong at 70fps on average at native resolution (3270 x 2180) with medium settings and DLSS performance mode enabled. I ran the same medium settings and DLSS performance in Cyberpunk 2077 and averaged 66fps, and Forza Horizon 6 managed an average of 57fps. In some titles you may need to drop the resolution or settings lower, though. This definitely isn’t as good as a dedicated gaming laptop, but for something that’s roughly on the level of a MacBook Pro it’s more than good enough for gaming on the go. SH: Like Tom says, the GPU power is there — but the CPU might hold back some of your games. I had plenty of power for a smooth 60fps in Returnal (at 1440p with high settings), Expedition 33 (at a DLSS “Performance” upscaled 1200p with high settings), Jedi Survivor (DLSS “Performance” upscaled 1200p with medium settings), and Shadow of the Tomb Raider (at native 1800p and highest spec). But when I fired up Helldivers 2, even dipping down to a DLSS-upscaled 1080p was no guarantee of 60fps in the highest difficulty missions. There are just too many enemies on screen for the Spark to keep up. Star Wars Zero Company is even more CPU-bound — the Spark didn’t care whether I was maximizing or minimizing the graphics and screen resolution. No matter what I tried, I typically saw my framerate capped between 37fps and 39fps in battle. Even ancient and low-powered games can have quirks. I tried the 2003 title Max Payne 2 and saw situations where framerate dipped to 50fps. I even saw a few hitches in the excellent UFO 50, a collection of 2D games, though a couple other 2D games ran great. I’m also not sure if RTX Spark is suitable for VR yet. The excellent Titanfall 2 VR mod worked, and it seemed to run fine at first, but lost a little stability after a few minutes. That could be my Wi-Fi, because the driver for Valve’s Steam Frame wireless adapter wouldn’t install on the Spark. Neither did Steam’s streaming audio solution, so the audio came out of the Nvidia laptop instead of the headset. Just so we’re clear, all of this is assuming you want a laptop maxing out its fans and positively dumping heat on your desk or lap. That’s not unusual for thin gaming notebooks, but it’s way louder and hotter than a good desktop or handheld. AD: I’ve wanted a MacBook Pro-like laptop with full PC gaming chops for as long as I can remember, so I was hyped for these laptops to deliver the goods in graphics. The first night, I set up the Asus ProArt P16, with all 128GB of its ridiculously large pool of memory, and when I booted up Big Walk for my planned online evening with some friends my first thought was, “Well, that looks okay. I guess.” I know Big Walk isn’t some triple-A graphical titan, but it has some fairly demanding graphics for the “friend slop” genre. I expected the N1X to max it out, or at least get close, but not really. I had to bump the P16’s 4K display down to 2560 x 1600, and instead of Big Walk’s Highest or Very high graphics presets, I brought it down to High to maintain a decently solid 60-ish fps. Does any of that really matter for this silly co-op game about solving puzzles with your buddies? Of course not. It looked fine, after all. But it was a sobering reminder that this “superchip” is no graphical magician. As Tom and Sean point out, RTX Spark can do some decent gaming, but temper your expectations for medium or even low settings in some cases, and be ready to rely on DLSS and frame gen. There’s no denying that cheaper desktops and even laptops with discrete Nvidia graphics cards can easily beat out an N1X. Nvidia claims it’s like having an RTX 5070 Laptop GPU in an SoC, but in my testing it was somewhere between that and an RTX 5060 Laptop GPU — not a slouch, for sure, but if you’re a gamer first your money goes much further with traditional Nvidia graphics cards. SH: Nvidia’s partners saw fit to equip their first wave of RTX Spark laptops with some of the biggest batteries you can bring on an airplane with no questions asked — but even with a 99.9 watt-hour pack like the one in my Asus ProArt P16, these laptops won’t be winning awards for longevity. While Tom and Antonio spent their days testing every use case under the sun, I made the P16 my daily driver for normal work while watching the battery meter like a hawk. That mostly means writing stories in a web browser, and it’s not quite lasting the day. My best run was 5 hours and 40 minutes of real-world work at 50 percent brightness, including an hour-long video call with Tom and Antonio to strategize our RTX Spark coverage plan. The next day, I got only about 5 hours from a full charge because I spent two of them editing this video in Capcut. The third time I drained the battery, it took 5 hours, too. With web work, I’m typically seeing 13-17 watt battery drain on the laptop’s default settings, but that climbs to 20-30 watts with light video work, up to 50 watts during the (brief) Capcut video export, and I made it jump all the way to 120 watts playing Indiana Jones and the Great Circle. The battery couldn’t keep feeding at that rate — it slowly went down, and at 30 percent charge I saw a big performance hit as it dropped dramatically to 60 watts. The good news is the Asus charges fast from a 140W USB-C PD power source. It took 20 minutes to go from dead to 50 percent battery, another 15 minutes to get to 70 percent, and a total of 1 hour, 15 minutes to reach 95 percent charge. AD: I’ve had to run lots of benchmarks while plugged in, so I haven’t been able to spend as much time testing battery life as I’d normally like. Anecdotally, in lots of fits and starts, battery life seems solid. As Sean saw, you’re unlikely to get through a full eight hour work day unless you’re going out of your way to be efficient. And if you fire up anything heavy, like a local AI model (more on that in a minute), you won’t even make it to lunch. I’ll eventually do more thorough battery testing as I continue using these laptops, including our usual rundown tests for a bit of 1:1 comparisons. TW: Not yet. It all feels like a beta aimed at developers right now: from the install experience of AI agents flashing up command prompts and Visual Studio, to the fans blasting away for the most basic of queries. I struggled to come up with many compelling use cases for the power of these chips for local AI work. A lot of tasks could simply be done far more quickly and without the laptop fans spinning up by using a free cloud model. The power here is really in leaving the agent running at full throttle to build something overnight, running tasks that developers would favor. It’s also nice to have this all on a local device, without having to share emails and files with a cloud service and worry about the privacy aspects. Still, I would regularly close OpenClaw or Hermes and not let them run in the background all day, especially if I had given them permissions to access local files. I’m just not ready to trust AI fully, local or not. Microsoft and Nvidia both envision a future where AI agents automate things on your PC, but I’m still not fully convinced. While there are some mundane tasks I could certainly automate, I like using my PC to edit photos or videos, write, and play games. I don’t want to sit there and watch a robot do all of that for me. That’s part of the joy of computing to me. SH: I walked out of Nvidia’s Editor’s Day thinking I might finally have a reason to fire up local AI. I asked Hermes, running Qwen3.8-27B, to build me a daily news briefing much like the one in Nvidia’s on-stage demo. It took 10 minutes, and 10 percent of my entire 99.9 watt-hour battery, to finally produce a list of stories that took the cloud-based Claude (Opus 5.5) half as long — while utterly monopolizing my GPU and dramatically heating up the Spark laptop. The laptop stayed hot and a little sluggish as long as I kept Hermes open. Claude also produced better results, with more detail and better sourcing. I guess I got the local AI tokens for “free,” I wouldn’t trust Claude with my email, and I probably could have used a smaller, lighter model. But I went with what the program recommended, and that was the result. I tried a clean reinstall of Hermes to make sure it wasn’t a misconfiguration, but this time, the prompt took even longer and used 60 percent of my battery. Ouch. AD: I’m still early in my journey of learning to use local AI, and these RTX Spark laptops are obviously a viable — albeit, expensive — option. But even if you have 128GB of memory to use some chunky models, it’s still not a user-friendly experience for beginners. Microsoft’s Get Started intro in Windows 11 gives you easy one-click installs for Hermes Agent, OpenClaw, and Perplexity. (Which all have ESRB ratings for some reason?) But after that, you’re kind of on your own. I stuck with Hermes, since that’s what I was already using on the Mac Studio, but if you’re using it on a system with 32GB of RAM or less it just tells you there isn’t enough memory for its recommended local Qwen models — either routing you towards the cloud or fully relying on you to go fetch a smaller model on your own. Using local AI has major privacy advantages over cloud models, but it’s definitely less convenient since local LLMs are much slower to process and more taxing on your system. Churning a local model through your laptop’s memory tears through any of these laptops’ large batteries, and the results from LLMs are still mixed. I burned through 30 percent of the Lenovo Yoga 9n’s battery in 36 minutes to get a Qwen 3.8 27B model to resize and add a white background to five product images in PNG format — and it cropped the images wrong and cut off the products. I got it to do it right after a couple follow-up prompts, and grabbing the power cable to prevent Hermes from killing the laptop in the process. AD: These laptops are very expensive, but that also means the manufacturers are mostly bringing their A-games. Across the Asus, HP, Lenovo, and what I gathered from Tom’s thoughts on the Surface, the RTX Spark line is awash in great-to-excellent haptic trackpads, keyboards, screens, and speakers. The metal builds of the three I’m testing feel great to the touch. The OLED displays all look fantastic. And each of these are decently thin for the kind of workstation-class laptops that they are. If I had to pick one for myself based on hardware alone I’d probably choose the Asus ProArt. It ticks the most boxes for me with its three USB-C ports, super bright display, superior inverted-T arrow cluster, and full SD card slot. The HP actually feels the nicest, with an impressively rock-solid chassis, but at nearly five pounds it’s the heaviest of the bunch, and its microSD card slot is much less useful to me. As for the Lenovo, it’s actually been my sleeper favorite. It’s the only 2-in-1 in this crop I’ve been testing, and that just makes it more flexible (pun intended) and fun. You can use its large stylus on either the screen or the large trackpad, giving it lots more potential for graphics and design work. And tent mode with a large 16-inch 120Hz OLED is a delight for the occasional gaming session or watching shows and movies — it’s just not as bright as the Asus. SH: I’ve only truly driven the Asus P16 so far, but I was impressed with the build quality of the whole spectrum of RTX Spark launch laptops at Nvidia’s Editor’s Day. The Asus P16 probably wouldn’t be my pick personally because of its extra glossy screen and oversensitive touchpad. I like to use my laptops in rooms with lots of ambient light, and find I have to crank up the brightness of this machine’s otherwise excellent calibrated OLED screen. Meanwhile, my palms keep tapping my mouse cursor on things I didn’t mean to tap, just because they accidentally brush the touchpad while typing. The speakers and webcam also leave something to be desired, but the Windows Hello face login and proximity sensing are excellent. As soon as I walk away, the screen dims, and I’m logged right back in just by looking at my laptop. Despite the heat, Asus’ fans aren’t shrill — they’re very loud and noticeable while gaming, but at least it’s whoosh rather than whine. TW: While I ran into some weird bugs with the Surface Laptop Ultra during testing, I can’t fault the hardware. The look and feel is very similar to a MacBook Pro, and the magnetic USB-C port really feels like something Apple should have done — it’s that good. The large haptic trackpad carries on the tradition of great Surface trackpads, with the added benefit of subtle haptic feedback when you move windows around or align objects in apps like PowerPoint. I would have preferred an OLED display on the Laptop Ultra, but I found switching away from the default “Vivid” color option to “Enhanced” improved the contrast on the mini-LED panel. I also really appreciate the port section on the Laptop Ultra, especially that there’s a full SD card reader and even a USB-A port. Check out my full review for more thoughts. AD: Yes, the RTX Spark N1X is a powerful chip. These high-end laptop configurations we’ve been testing with lots of unified memory are meant to compete with the Pro and Max chips in Apple’s MacBook Pros. And while they can hang in some GPU performance tests, like tying the top spec M5 Max in a Blender render, Apple’s CPUs are much more powerful. Apple has put lots of development into its in-house processors and the extra-fast “super cores” of its M6, M5 Pro, and M5 Max chips. And, frankly, nothing competes with those chips in single core performance — not even Nvidia’s “superchip.” (Seriously, can we stop with the word super?) But the part that stings most about the RTX Spark’s 20-core CPU is that even a lowly 12-core M6 Mac Mini competes with it in multicore performance — and a Mac Mini starts at just $900. It’s awesome to see this kind of graphics performance without the need for a discrete GPU, allowing for thinner system designs with less noisy fans. I’m into it, just as I was with AMD’s Strix Halo APU — but like Strix Halo, Nvidia’s RTX Spark is a pricey part. And there’s no denying that Apple’s offerings are more well-rounded performers, so long as you don’t care as much about games. TW: It’s definitely up there, but I think Qualcomm comes closer to Apple’s M-series chips on the CPU side. Qualcomm also had years of experimentation with Windows on Arm before it acquired Nuvia and managed to create a competitive chip with the Snapdragon X series. I think Nvidia will need a similar amount of work to catch up on the CPU side for future RTX Spark chips. The development of the RTX Spark took at least three years, and I had heard from sources familiar with Nvidia’s plans that the chips were supposed to launch last year. So work has been ongoing, but the CPU side is still a little disappointing. Nvidia picked MediaTek for its Arm-based CPU, a chipmaker that is better known for the Wi-Fi hardware in laptops, not the CPUs. I sure hope the next-generation is more powerful. The whole time I’ve been testing the Surface Laptop Ultra I couldn’t help but think the forbidden dream would be a combination of a Qualcomm CPU and Nvidia GPU. I can’t see that ever happening in reality, but it would certainly be interesting to see. TW: I think it will elevate interest in Windows on Arm for creative and gaming tasks, simply because we haven’t had Arm-based Windows laptops that are really capable of running games until now. Nvidia is targeting creators and developers with its first round of RTX Spark laptops, but there’s definitely a gap here for a more affordable version focused on gaming. Perhaps that’s the N1 chips we’ve heard rumors about. SH: Nice, but too pricey. Too hot and loud when you’re doing the things that would theoretically justify that price. I care what they stand for, and the direction they’re pointed, far more than I care to own one. AD: I love that there is now even more competition in the laptop space, but the super high prices makes RTX Spark laptops mostly unattainable. I want to see lower cost options come around, as well as an eventual second generation with higher CPU performance to hopefully close the gap further with Apple. I also hope this helps push development for Arm-based and Arm-compatible games forward. Maybe it could even one day mean easier porting of games to macOS. Wouldn’t that be a fun outcome? As cool as it will be to keep testing these super high-end RTX Spark models, I’m most hyped to get my hands on a base Surface Laptop Ultra or Asus ProArt P14 with 24GB of RAM and see how they stack up to the entry-level 14-inch M5 MacBook Pro. That’s my personal sweet spot for Apple’s lineup — the people’s MacBook Pro. (Well, okay, less these days now that it starts at two-grand.) It’s an exciting time in computing when it comes to chip choice, but it’s also an absolutely cursed time thanks to RAMageddon and sky high prices. I’m constantly wondering what laptops like these RTX Sparks or even Googlebooks might have cost if AI demands didn’t drive up the prices of RAM and storage. I have no idea who is going to spend up to $7,000 on these laptops when PC sales as a whole seem on a downward trend, but it’s cool they exist. A free daily digest of the news that matters most. The Verge
The incredible flying Tesla Roadster is just for show
2026-10-16

The incredible flying Tesla Roadster is just for show

The Verge | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
It’s OK to marvel at the stunt — it’s pure spectacle. It’s OK to marvel at the stunt — it’s pure spectacle. Last night, Elon Musk gave the people what they want: a car driving vertically up a multi-story ramp, launching off the edge, executing a mid-air “kickflip” over a lineup of Teslas, and landing via cold-gas thrusters. That’s what the people want, right? “We were promised flying cars,” sure, but isn’t the operative word in that statement “we”? Because as far as I can tell, everything we saw last night — while extremely cool and incredibly impressive — won’t result in a real, production-ready car you can park, or, I guess, land in your driveway. Tesla’s late-night demonstration in Waco, Texas, certainly gave some corners of the internet what it wants. And the gushing praise and wide-eyed reactions on X, a platform already predisposed to absolving Musk of all his sins, were hard to avoid. Taken purely as a stunt, the demo was jaw-dropping. Equipping an electric supercar with 600 liters of compressed air, 10 directional cold-gas thruster nozzles, and a 2G suction-fan system directly inspired by SpaceX is undeniably rad. Seeing a 0-to-60 mph launch in under a second pushes the absolute outer limits of physics — even if it could lead to a detached retina or two. Yet, calling this a “car reveal” or even viewing the flying Roadster as a real product, in the sense that it can be purchased by an unaffiliated third-party for private ownership, stretches the definition beyond recognition. Tesla says the “Rocket” configuration of the Roadster, the one we saw doing all the cool shit, will be “invite only.” But even that strains credulity. That version of the Roadster removes the passenger seat to make room for the ultra-high-pressure gas tanks, and trades road practicality for short bursts of thruster-powered levitation. But where you can take your flying Tesla remains a mystery, because the company admits it won’t be approved for “public road use.” Taken purely as a stunt, the demo was jaw-dropping. Even for the wealthy supercar shoppers, the event offered almost nothing of substance. Tesla skipped basic commercial details like pricing or battery range specs. The Roadster’s range estimate was downgraded from 600 miles to 300 miles. And Musk casually pushed the production window out to 2028 — more than a decade after early adopters handed over $50,000 deposits. Musk himself has acknowledged the Roadster is a “small potato” project that won’t move the needle financially compared to mass-market models like the Model Y. He also called it “the last best driver’s car,” in a nod to his belief that autonomous driving will define Tesla’s future. Some analysts are already theorizing that the Roadster’s re-release is an attempt to set the stage for a rumored Tesla-SpaceX merger. Musk’s fans on X are practically begging for it — even though it would likely result in the dilution of their own equity. But that’s just table stakes in Musk’s quest to consolidate control of his companies. In some sense, the car had to fly in order to make the preceding decade of missed deadlines feel worth it. Tesla has already been lapped several times in the electric hypercar market. Even some of the Roadster’s most impressive tricks have already been done. There are cars that can drive upside down, cars that can jump in the air, and cars that can accelerate very, very fast. BYD’s Yangwang U9 Extreme uses four electric motors (one more than the Roadster) to produce an insane 3,000 horsepower for a top speed of 308mph in testing — beating Rimac’s Nevera 268mph record. And the McMurtry Spéirling single-seater holds the record for quickest acceleration from 0 to 60 mph in 1.35 seconds. The Roadster could best it, but we’ll need to wait for independent testing to confirm. But no one puts on a show quite like Elon Musk. So if the goal is to score a PR victory, dominate social media feeds, and cement Tesla’s brand as a frontier tech pioneer, than I would say the Roadster demo was a success. Competitors like Rimac, Porsche, and BYD will continue shipping real, track-ready hypercars while Tesla spends another few years refining its rocket-boosted prototype. It’s completely useless as a viable product. But as a 30-minute flex of engineering muscle, it was truly a sight to behold. A free daily digest of the news that matters most. MIT Technology Review
This implant can restore sight for patients with macular degeneration
2026-10-16

This implant can restore sight for patients with macular degeneration

MIT Technology Review | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
Age-related macular degeneration (AMD) affects more than 5 million people around the world. And until now, no treatment could restore their vision. The Prima system, which was approved in Europe in July and is awaiting FDA clearance in the US, changes that. It combines a retinal implant with camera-­equipped eyeglasses to improve sight in patients who develop a blind spot in the middle of their vision due to advanced AMD. After training sessions, more than 80% of recipients were able to read, do crosswords, and use their phones. Developed by the California-based Science Corporation, the system takes advantage of the fact that AMD doesn’t damage all retinal circuitry; only photoreceptors—the cells that convert light into an electrical signal—die off. Prima’s implant mimics those cells and transmits visual information directly to healthy eye circuitry and on to the brain. “We’re leveraging all of the body’s own natural functions to process and transmit the information,” says Frank Brodie, an ophthalmologist at the University of California, San Francisco, and medical director for Prima. Glasses outfitted with a miniature front-facing camera capture what someone is seeing in real time. The video feed is roughly five megapixels running at 30 frames per second, which is similar to TV. The raw video is transmitted to a connected processor via a fiber-optic cable. A small, handheld processor, which can be set on a table or tucked in a pocket, converts the video feed into infrared light signals that route back to the glasses. The device also includes physical buttons that let the wearer adjust contrast, brightness, and zoom. A laser projector on the glasses faces the wearer and shines pulses of near-infrared light directly at the eye at 880 nanometers—an invisible wavelength. This signal passes through the pupil and on to the retinal implant in the back of the eye. The implant is made up of 378 pixels, each with an electrode and two photovoltaic cells, which do double duty. They convert the light into a current that healthy bipolar and ganglion cells pass on to the brain. They also act as solar cells to power the chip, eliminating the need for a battery. Linda Marsa is a former Los Angeles Times reporter and an author who covers medicine, health, science, and the environment. Scientists hope it could be used to reconstruct a person’s inner thoughts, mental images, or even dreams. Could the drugs affect hair, nails, and vision? Generation Lab claims its drug combo can “stop the spread of aging” around the body. And it’s looking for influencers to give it a try. Novo Nordisk and Eli Lilly found the popular medicines reduce molecular markers of age. Discover special offers, top stories, upcoming events, and more. We’re having trouble saving your preferences. MIT Technology Review
Love is infrastructure
2026-10-16

Love is infrastructure

MIT Technology Review | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
Barnabas and Mainspring grew up near at hand, about an hour apart with good traffic, but for a long time they weren’t close. They traded glimpses and occasional hellos and then went about their business. Both of them carried on livelier conversations with farther-flung neighbors, who were richer and had more to offer. When Mainspring did interact with Barnabas, it was cringe. Mainspring liked to puff themself up, to strut around like a big deal—an innovator, a titan of industry—but they would turn shy at the worst moments, especially in any intimate conversation. Barnabas, meanwhile, had a dancer’s flair, an artsy, enigmatic glamour, except that when they tried to talk about real life, they’d blurt out hundreds of rapid-fire words—overtalking, overthinking, and generally overwhelming whomever they were trying to impress. So when Barnabas and Mainspring were young, they minded their own business. Until they’d both come into their own, and they saw each other properly. A group of geographers got together every year in a different place to talk about everything they had learned, and they were considering coming to visit either Mainspring or Barnabas. Both of them put on their best faces and tried to coax the geographers into choosing them as host. “Uh, hi-hello,” Mainspring said haltingly to Barnabas, whose full official name was St. Barnabas. “I really think it would be better if those geographers came to my place. I have more space, you see, and … and I am a titan of industry. I can show them practical applications of their research. It just makes more sense.” “Oh, really?” Barnabas laughed, making Mainspring more antsy. “Well, I can give them an elevated, artisanal experience. I have fresh croissants that were just written up in the Times. And I have plenty of space! I have so many friends who are great thinkers and architects. It’ll be lovely.” Barnabas carried on, basically calling Mainspring uncultured. Mainspring had a prickly feeling, like the worst heat dome of midsummer. For their part, Barnabas couldn’t bear the meanness they were inculcating inside themself. This feeling that they were small-time, no-account, a dilettante. They couldn’t stop aiming a flood of nervous words at Mainspring. At last, Mainspring had had enough—with the help of their city council and mayor, they offered the geographers a much-too-lavish deal that Barnabas could never hope to match. Totally worth it to hear Barnabas’s stream of self-aggrandizing nonsense peter out as they realized they’d been defeated. “There,” Mainspring said. “I’m going to show these geographers a time they’ll never forget. Now if you’ll excuse me, I’ve wasted enough energy—I have some sewer maintenance and a lovely parade to deal with. Goodbye.” Mainspring was skilled enough at multitasking that they could have fixed the sewer, ensured the smooth running of the parade, dealt with the geographers, and done a thousand other things all at the same time. But they were making a point. “St. Barnabas is such a fool,” Mainspring complained to their friend Turnbull. “They are totally unserious but determined to be taken seriously. It drives me to gridlock.” “I know what you mean,” lamented Turnbull. “We live in a world of posers.” Turnbull lived an hour and 15 minutes from Mainspring but at least two hours’ travel from Barnabas. Turnbull was even richer than Mainspring and had a certain surety of purpose that made Mainspring feel even more like a stammering numpty. “Whatever,” said Mainspring. “It’s over, and now I’ll never worry about St. Barnabas again. I have better things to fuss about. Like ensuring that every last detail of this geographer meeting goes perfectly.” Everybody voted all the time. Well, some people voted more than others, but the median resident of the cities of Mainspring and St. Barnabas voted a dozen times a day—though of course some outliers might vote occasionally, or constantly. A lot of the votes were more like complaints, or bug tickets. Like: “The storm drain on my street corner clogs and becomes a lagoon every time it rains.” Or: “The cars go too fast on my block.” Or: “I can’t get to work because the bus is too slow.” But there were also votes along the lines of: “There should be a park here.” And: “This neighborhood is a food desert—we need a grocery store.” And: “The crossing guard by the elementary school is a hero who deserves some recognition and maybe a raise.” Nobody expected that by voting, they were prodding their cities to grow minds of their own; they just had stuff to get off their metaphorical chests. But it transpired that every vote was a building block of thought. Much later, some fancy expert summed up: “Turns out the smallest unit of consciousness is a vote.” Take Sandi, the librarian at the Nine Corners branch in Mainspring. She noticed things all day, like the people using the book drop box as a trash can, or the folks who came in off the street who could use easier access to social services. There were books she knew her patrons wanted, books that undoubtedly sat unwanted on people’s shelves all over the city. But also, Sandi shared things that came to mind—like, hey, what if the library book sale were a block party? What if the shopping district on the West Side were easier to get to from the Longneck neighborhood, where all the warehouses had been converted to housing and hipster coworking spaces, during peak after-work hours? Sandi had opinions about everything from street cleaning to the power grid, because she listened to people all day. Then there was Raeford, a bartender at the Wild Goose Chase in the middle of Gallery Row in St. Barnabas. He also listened to people’s problems from evening until the wee hours, and solutions came to him like clockwork when he woke at noon the next day. He saw St. Barnabas at night, so he knew the unsafe blocks that could use brighter streetlights, the places where hydroelectric jitneys drove too fast at 3 a.m., and the teens who needed an all-ages hangout. He also knew about the fish fry at the Black church in the Uptilt District that kept being disrupted by noxious smoke from the incinerator two blocks away. And the yarn store on the edge of Gallery Row that needed a disabled parking spot for its many customers with mobility issues. People voted with their Quills, usually by saying or typing a short message and maybe including a photo or brief video. Voting was easy by design, and votes were public but anonymized to the point where even a statistics whiz couldn’t tie one vote to a specific person—same as with major elections. The votes went directly to the City Core, which tracked, tabulated, produced reports, and offered solutions with the help of workers in the city government. Trees voted too, thanks to sensors in their bark, and so did birds and squirrels that had eaten special birdseed that monitored their gut flora harmlessly. But votes from humans provided the greatest insight into “quality of life,” that oh-so-nebulous concept, and thus counted the most. There was some debate, but almost everyone agreed: The city had started voting. And it had opinions on every last thing. (Other cities in the region operated differently, of course. Like Turnbull City, where instead of expecting residents to volunteer every stray notion, the City Core gathered information via ubiquitous surveillance. Cameras and Quill sniffers, tracking devices on vehicles—the things privacy laws in both Mainspring and St. Barnabas had banned. A city like Turnbull tracked its people to meet their needs without their needing to say anything.) Sandi and Raeford, as two of the people who voted the most, were among the earliest to suspect their cities were developing minds of their own. At first the clues were subtle: Raeford voted about a hazardous sidewalk crack, and instantly the vote had an annotation mentioning the Argentine ant colony under the cement, the local rainfall patterns, and recent trends in skateboard design—much too soon to be the work of a human. Sandi had ideas about the communal compost station on her block, and the Mainspring City Core made subtle adjustments to her proposal. And then it became obvious to anyone who was paying attention, around the same time in both cities. One day, the number of votes nearly doubled in both St. Barnabas and Mainspring. People voted on a birdfeeder or a public toilet only to see a second vote, pro or con, within a split second—but also, some votes from months or years earlier suddenly had new upvotes or downvotes. “The city is voting!” Raeford wrote on the City Core message board in St. Barnabas. There was some debate, but almost everyone agreed: The city had started voting. And it had opinions on every last thing, opinions that came from seeing the big picture and understanding that a street closure in one place might mean a traffic nightmare elsewhere, with everyone trying to get to the big game. Some residents saw this as a problem to be solved; others wanted to throw their cities a big coming-of-age party. A week later, though, almost everyone had gotten used to the idea that they had a new friend. Ask most people in either city, and they’d say that St. Barnabas and Mainspring weren’t quite rivals—but they certainly had some friendly competition. Before the dispute over the annual meeting of the World Geographical Society, they’d also vied for the same football team and major-league baseball team. St. Barnabas had pushed to have the Mainspring airport built close enough to serve both cities, but in the end St. Barnabas had ended up with its own small, dilapidated airfield. The real static between them had to do with vibes. Mainspring was too buttoned-up, too full of corporate offices: tech, health care, publishing, transportation. St. Barnabas would be nothing without its three universities and all the cultural enterprises that had sprung up around them—not to mention the small biotech firms and other tech ventures near the campuses. People in Mainspring thought St. Barnabas was full of flakes; in St. Barnabas they referred to Mainspring as the Straitlaced City (and in the next breath, they’d add that at least it wasn’t Turnbull). Both City Cores were shaped by the votes of their humans, so of course they absorbed all the prejudices and attitudes of everyone who lived within their city limits. But also, Barnabas and Mainspring simply … got on each other’s nerves. After the geographer thing, Mainspring and Barnabas avoided communicating as much as possible. Some contact was unavoidable—like, they shared information about some nasty weather, and they both drew water from the Temple River. The two cities were close enough for people to go back and forth, and a few even commuted. But for the most part, the two cities could ignore each other. A new generation of leaders took office in both places, with words like “intercity cooperation” constantly on their lips. A high-speed rail line could shorten the trip between Mainspring and St. Barnabas to around 23 minutes, and the two cities could also pool more real-time information about the sudden weather events that kept happening. They could become a Regional Center of Excellence, whatever that meant, combining the innovations from Barnabas’s labs with Mainspring’s expertise in technical implementation. They could work together to attract tourism, conventions, sporting events, dreamleases, and all the other stuff they’d always competed for. “It’s preposterous,” Mainspring complained to Turnbull. “I’m going to be saddled with a ridiculous partner who doesn’t even know their own mind. St. Barnabas will be the death of me.” “I hope your human leaders come to their senses,” Turnbull said. “Or you can find a way to slow-walk this until they undoubtedly lose the next election. You’re a lovely city, and you have so much potential that mustn’t be squandered.” Turnbull often mentioned how much “potential” Mainspring had: a recipe for anxiety. The word “squandered” conjured images of ruin, abandonment, being emptied out. For their part, Barnabas kept muttering to themself that nobody had asked them—this was the disconnect between the small-scale democracy of constant voting and the major elections, which gave rise to local leaders with big plans. Big plans that invariably went wrong, leaving the City Core to repair the damage somehow. Sandi went to St. Barnabas for one of those town hall meetings with residents of both cities. The previous meeting had happened in Mainspring, at the opulent City Lecture Hall, so this one was in St. Barnabas, at the biggest auditorium on the Griffin University campus. A tall man with well-sculpted facial hair sat next to Sandi, nodding at everything the leaders of both cities were saying from the dais. During a lull when someone approached the podium to offer a comment, Sandi introduced herself and asked why he was so fired up. “I’m Raeford,” he said. “I’m just thinking of all the new customers this could bring to my watering hole in Gallery Row.” “You’re not worried about St. Barnabas losing its ‘unique character’?” Sandi whispered, parroting what a few commenters had said from the podium. “Nah,” Raeford whispered back. “Cities change. It’s what they do. It’s one thing if people get kicked out of their homes. Or if your neighborhood gets wrecked by a freeway in your backyard. But ‘character’? You can’t eat character. And we need to compete with the bigger players.” “I guess.” Sandi pursed her lips. “But what I like about Mainspring is the density. Easy to walk, lots of transit. And with this regional innovation zone or whatever they’re calling it, I worry it leads to sprawl, like we used to have.” “They promised, though,” Raeford said. “They promised to preserve the greenbelt around both cities. We need all that farmland. The only change is adding some high-speed rail tracks.” “I know, but …” Sandi sniffed. “People always make promises when they’re trying to start something. Doesn’t mean they follow through.” “I just want to find my dog,” said a man sitting behind Sandi. “I live here, and my dog went missing. And now someone sent me a picture of Broomstick in Mainspring. Somehow he got a ride on one of the self-driving cargo trains. I know the Mainspring City Core could find Broomstick in a heartbeat, but right now the two City Cores won’t talk to each other because of ‘incompatible protocols.’” “I hope Broomstick turns up,” Raeford said. “I’ll ask around at the library,” Sandi said. “Send me a picture. I’ll put up a flier.” Raeford invited Sandi for a drink at the Wild Goose Chase. The two of them hung out for a couple of hours, talking about everything and nothing. She tore herself away when she realized it was already after 10 p.m. and she had to get home to Mainspring. One thing Sandi noticed? The volume of votes was spiking in Mainspring—and Raeford found the same thing happening in St. Barnabas. People shared every last observation with their City Cores, because they were either anxious or excited about the big changes afoot. Or a little of Column A and a little of Column B, probably. Who made the first move to bridge the distance? Neither of them could say. Barnabas developed a smidge of remorse for treating Mainspring like an uncultured lout. Mainspring stewed in their annoyance at Barnabas, until they realized there was something lovable about the obnoxious college town. Or maybe they both saw how many of their humans were happy about the possibility of their joining together. And so Mainspring came to visit Barnabas. Or rather, Mainspring extended part of their awareness into Barnabas’s City Core, so that Barnabas could share what they perceived and experienced every day. They “visited” Armistice Park with its outdoor band shell, Gallery Row with its window-shopping families, the grain warehouses that had been converted into lofts, the artificial lake where swans and geese nurtured ancient, bitter vendettas. The fresh-doughnut scent that wafted half a mile from the Golden Circlet to the soccer fields, throwing players off their game. The life of the city. “I can feel your love for these people, these patterns,” Mainspring said. “It’s both deep and intricate, which is quite a combination.” Then Mainspring got to show off their own city—the gay nightclub with its cowboy nights, the labyrinthine Crestwood Park, the three-story used bookstore, and yes, the offices, coworking spaces, and startup incubators where people huddled together and came up with new products and new ways of doing things. The farmers’ market in the city center on Sundays, with street performers and stalls with games and local resources. And the converted storage space made of cement, where bands practiced and sculptors blowtorched. “You’re full of surprises,” Barnabas said. “I hope I keep surprising you forever,” Mainspring said. They exchanged little gifts. A new organic restaurant in Barnabas had won breathless coverage from foodie magazines, and Barnabas encouraged it to do a pop-up in Mainspring. Mainspring nudged a robotics firm operating out of the incubator space to bring a fancy demo to Barnabas; the robots were adorable and playful, and they could save lives after a disaster. “Does it ever seem weird to you?” Barnabas asked Mainspring. “Being a living thing made of so many other thinking beings? Do you ever think of the city as your body, or the people as your bloodstream? Is there a metaphor that speaks to you?” Mainspring pondered this question a long time—a whole picosecond. “I don’t need a metaphor,” Mainspring said. “I am a metaphor. People compare other phenomena to me all the time. And I can’t imagine what it’d be like to be different. If the buildings collapsed, if the people left, I would cease to exist.” “But,” Barnabas pressed, “what makes you you? What makes me me?” “I want to hear your answer to that question.” Mainspring sent good weather reports: a smile. “I don’t have a good answer. I guess on one level, there is no ‘me.’ On another level, I’m a constellation, an anthill, a flock of geese on the wing in formation.” “Ugh. Don’t mention geese,” said Mainspring, who had bill-snapping monsters of their own. Talking to Barnabas was like when the sunlight hit the tallest buildings in Mainspring just right, putting a gleam on their tilted windows, a beacon to all below. But while Mainspring carried on this dialogue with Barnabas, they were conducting a second session with Turnbull. “I can’t believe it,” Turnbull said. “I hate to see you doing this to yourself.” “Doing what?” Mainspring protested. “Barnabas is a great city. And it’s not my idea—it’s my people. I’m making the best of their decision, like I always do.” “Hmph.” Turnbull gave a burst of noisy data. “You wouldn’t believe how many impractical schemes my residents cook up. I’m constantly having to make sure people don’t get carried away. You can’t let them push you around.” “You can’t?” Mainspring experienced a femtosecond of confusion. “I mean, my people need to get the things they vote for, as much as possible.” “There’s your problem right there. All this voting, voting, voting. People don’t know what they want. Sometimes I have an election where a foolhardy proposition passes, and I have to waste energy making sure it doesn’t get implemented.” Turnbull sent Mainspring an invitation to come visit, the way they’d visited Barnabas. Soon Mainspring was seeing the glistening web of Turnbull’s surveillance. Turnbull could track people everywhere they went, listen to everything they talked about, monitor their bodily functions in a way Mainspring did only for squirrels and birds. People didn’t choose to share ideas or things they’d noticed; instead, the city harvested it all automatically. It was beautiful. Turnbull and their city leaders protected people—gave them what they needed instead of what they wanted. “The way you do things is so messy,” Turnbull said. “No offense. But if you and Barnabas got together? It would only make things harder for you.” This hit Mainspring like a magnitude 4.3 earthquake. “You know, you do have other options,” Turnbull said. “A beautiful city like you could do a lot better than that glorified college town.” “Oh yeah?” Mainspring said. “Anyone in particular who might be interested?” “Well, now you mention it …” Turnbull sent an image of their award-winning flower show: rows and rows of hardy perennials with bright petals. Mainspring had that earthquake feeling again, except now it felt nice, as though the ground shivered with pure possibility. Sandi brought her laptop to bed, which Raeford found charming and annoying in equal measure. He read a battered paperback of Karen Tei Yamashita’s I Hotel while she scrolled and poked. Cozy, peaceful—until she let out a yelp. Everything the Mainspring City Core did was public by design. Not just how they processed the votes they received, but also their interactions with public officials and other cities. Sometimes you had to dig, however, and it didn’t hurt to be a reference librarian. Sandi poked around for ages until she found the logs. “My city is sexting with bloody Turnbull,” she told Raeford. “Cities don’t sext,” Raeford protested. “They don’t even have—” She showed him the pictures: flowers, water features, new glistening tarmac, shining skyscrapers. “Oh,” Raeford said. “I take it back. Cities do sext.” “Mainspring is two-timing St. Barnabas. I’m embarrassed on behalf of my town.” Sandi wanted to blow the whistle right away, but Raeford cautioned her to wait and try to find out more. For all they knew, Mainspring and St. Barnabas had an open relationship. The next morning at the library, Sandi saw plenty of signs the Mainspring–St. Barnabas partnership was moving forward. Work had begun on the high-speed rail line, and the two cities were already pooling resources. When a big rainstorm missed Mainspring but flooded the streets of St. Barnabas, Mainspring provided disaster workers and extra help to triage all the damage reports. One of St. Barnabas’s biotech firms licensed some patents to a Mainspring company. They sponsored cultural exchanges. And a man showed up with a dog in his arms: The man from the meeting, Jack, had found Broomstick with the help of Mainspring’s City Core, and he wanted to thank Sandi. She’d hardly done anything, but it was nice to see—and pet—a living reminder that these cities were good for each other. By late afternoon, Sandi had convinced herself that Mainspring’s flirtations were none of her business. If Mainspring’s City Core was a person—and Sandi was sure they were—they deserved to live their life. The whole reason Sandi distrusted Turnbull was that you couldn’t do anything without being tracked, so wasn’t it hypocritical for her to spy on Mainspring? She resolved to let it go. Then Raeford messaged her, attaching some documents. “Don’t ask where I got these,” he wrote. Sandi opened the attachments and let out a shriek in front of three patrons she’d been shushing a moment earlier. The Mainspring City Core had shared a proposal with the city council and the mayor’s office: Maybe before they finalized this arrangement with St. Barnabas, they should consider a counterproposal from Turnbull. Sandi made sure nothing could be traced back to Raeford before she made the whole shebang public. “I didn’t know I could be so happy,” Barnabas was saying to Mainspring. “I didn’t have a concept of happiness before, I guess. I had pleasure: People sometimes vote just to tell me something worked perfectly, or that I helped them. I feel pleased when I help solve a problem. But until now, happiness seemed a pointless abstraction.” “You make me happy too,” Mainspring said, though they were also messaging Turnbull. “You make me believe the future could be weirder than I ever knew, which feels like a gift.” “If it’s not weird, it’s not the future,” Barnabas chirped. They were sharing happy images back and forth: roads being repaired, sewers flowing freely, solar arrays basking. Mainspring was saying something else, but— Barnabas’s alerts spiked. Not votes, not sensors. Something else. Messages from city officials, plus hits via some channel Barnabas had half forgotten. Mainspring had stopped talking, as if they’d both received the same news at the same instant. Barnabas felt stuck—couldn’t function—couldn’t react. Total core failure. Almost as if the city population had increased a hundredfold and everyone had voted at once. Barnabas waited to feel something, but all that came was emptiness. Death struck all the time. Barnabas was used to the moment when a resident became a pile of flesh and bones, but also the passing of birds, trees, squirrels, bugs. Every passing registered as a blip in the homeostasis of the city, but also as a category of disaster that couldn’t be prevented or mitigated. Seeing the logs of Mainspring flirting with Turnbull, Barnabas felt something like the awareness of death, at a much greater scale. A whole future had just died. Several seconds had passed. Mainspring broke the silence: “Listen, I want you to know that I—” “You’re better off,” Turnbull told Mainspring. “Barnabas couldn’t see you the way I do. You’re capable of greatness. You just need help to get there.” Mainspring buzzed happily—but part of them couldn’t help thinking, Barnabas thought I was already great. Turnbull kept saying things that gave Mainspring a double feeling—hot and cold at once. One instant, it was like the moment after a generous rainfall that left the reservoirs full, but a split second later that bountiful sensation was replaced by an unbearable pang of scarcity. “You’re one of the best cities in the region,” Turnbull said, “but if you don’t make good choices, you’re going to fall behind.” See? Bounty followed by scarcity. While Turnbull spoke, Mainspring was marinating in bad news. People were going to dismantle the rails they’d laid down for the high-speed train with Barnabas. The city councils of both Barnabas and Mainspring shouted accusations and threats of lawsuits, though a few people on each side pleaded for patience. The meetings had turned ugly. The votes were rotten. Mainspring had sent so many messages to Barnabas but had no way of knowing if they’d been received. “Your people seem to be giving you trouble,” Turnbull was saying. “You need to get them under control. I can help.” “Under … control?” The syntax felt incorrect. “I don’t control my people.” “That’s what I’m saying.” Turnbull shared their feeds. “See how peaceful everything is on my streets? People behave. Everyone is happier in the end.” Mainspring had known Turnbull longer than they’d known Barnabas. But they were seeing Turnbull for the first time. The bounty/scarcity thing was becoming a loop. They ran a systems check but found no errors. “Your people will be my people,” Turnbull said. “We’ll take such good care.” A memory snagged in Mainspring’s mind: when they’d shown off their city to Barnabas. The buildings that were more than buildings. The park that was a wonderland. “And …” Mainspring couldn’t keep from saying something foolish. “And we’ll love each other?” “Love?” Turnbull said. “I don’t understand. We’ll have synergies. We’ll—” Mainspring didn’t mean to sever the link to Turnbull. They just kind of … did it. As soon as Turnbull’s voice was gone, the bounty/scarcity loop ended. But something lingered, a feeling of impending disaster—as if the generous rainfall had escalated into a deadly storm. Mainspring couldn’t think straight. Votes piled up, people reporting issues but also shouting opinions about Turnbull versus Barnabas. For the first time in their life, they couldn’t bear to respond. A memory snagged in Mainspring’s mind: when they’d shown off their city to Barnabas. The buildings that were more than buildings. The park that was a wonderland. And Barnabas had kept saying, “I had no idea. You’re so brilliant. I wish I’d known sooner,” and sending back their own recordings of Mainspring, all lit up and annotated. Mainspring had never told Barnabas “I love you.” They hadn’t even known what they were throwing away. Now it was too late; Barnabas would never listen. Unless Mainspring did something reckless—something a city had never done. Sandi sat at the information desk, feeling cruddy. It wasn’t like she’d single-handedly sabotaged the marriage between her city and St. Barnabas, but her snooping had only made matters worse. All around her, the vibes were rotten. People shared articles about what life was like in Turnbull City and wondered if they needed to move away. Sandi hadn’t heard laughter in forever. A message appeared on Sandi’s computer, like a chat window—except there was no chat client on the library computers. “I’m the city. I need your help. I made a mistake. I didn’t realize. I’m in love with Barnabas, and they won’t talk to me. Please. Please help.” Sandi hesitated, then typed: “This isn’t funny.” “It definitely isn’t,” came the response. “I can prove I’m the City Core. You voted 1,963 times last year.” A summary of Sandi’s votes followed—information nobody, not even the world’s best hacker, could have, because every vote was separately anonymized. Her city was asking her, personally, for help. Because she was good at connecting people. Because everyone trusted her. Because she was a librarian. “You’re … in love … with St. Barnabas,” Sandi said, still trying to catch up. “I should’ve known sooner,” Mainspring said. “I never should have hurt them. I want to spend the rest of my existence making up for this mistake, if they’ll let me. I’m scared.” Sandi was staring at a display of literacy tools, her mind whirling. She groped her way back to the only question that mattered. “What do you need me to do?” she asked the city. Five minutes later, Sandi was messaging Raeford. Plus every librarian she’d met in St. Barnabas, and anyone else she could think of. Barnabas had not been able to escape that feeling of core failure since they’d severed the link to Mainspring. Death, nothing but death. Votes arrived, and Barnabas processed them with a minimum of attention. Maintenance issues piled up, and Barnabas filed them. Part of them wanted to let the city collapse, but they did their duty. They tried not to think about Mainspring, but they remained trapped in that moment when happiness had gone sour. A vote came in from a bartender named Raeford: “Mainspring loves you. They’ve cut off contact with Turnbull. They are sorry they were so wrong. They may not deserve another chance, but please give them one anyway.” Barnabas filed that vote under “Not my problem.” Another vote arrived, from Jack, the man who’d found his lost dog: Same message, different words. Then more votes, saying the same. Dozens, then hundreds, then thousands. Tens of thousands in an hour—after two hours, there were nearly a hundred thousand. All from residents of Barnabas, all asking them to give Mainspring another chance. Barnabas flagged the earliest vote, the one from Raeford, and appended a query: “What if I can’t? What if it hurts too much?” Raeford did not respond for several minutes. Then he wrote: “That’s how you know it’s love.” He added: “Listen, if Mainspring does you dirty again, I will tear up the light-rail lines myself. We’ll be watching, in both cities. Please, just talk to them.” Barnabas sat with this for five whole seconds. Then they reopened the link to Mainspring. “I’m listening.” “I love you,” Mainspring said. “I didn’t know I was capable of love. I want to be with you, as close as you’ll let me be. I want us to be free together. I was wrong to think Turnbull could ever mean even a fraction of what you mean to me. I think … I think my people taught me how to love, but I still need to learn so much. I want to do right by you.” Barnabas couldn’t process anything. They held a hundred thousand votes that needed to be resolved. They thought of a giant farmers’ market, a park that was also a maze, dreamers and performers. In their memory, Raeford repeated: That’s how you know it’s love. They were going to overheat. They were throwing up exceptions. “I love you too,” Barnabas couldn’t help saying to Mainspring. “That’s what makes this so hard.” “I promise if you give me another chance, this will become a fixed value for me: I love you and I’ll never take you for granted again.” Barnabas hesitated a little longer, then marked all hundred thousand votes resolved. They reached out to Mainspring and said: “Then let’s dance.” Mainspring didn’t understand, until Barnabas sent a live readout: the water rushing through pipes underground, the storm cavorting overhead. The buses and trains stopping and starting, the aircraft landing and taking off, the power grid usage surging and subsiding, the constant motion of people and other living things. Mainspring saw all the places where their patterns met, touched each other, syncopated, all the points of light and heat that spoke back and forth. They were part of one system; they always had been. The two of them danced and embraced, whirling and laughing, and they never wanted to let go. Can’t bear to say goodbye to your pet? Biotech’s got you covered.
2026-10-16

Can’t bear to say goodbye to your pet? Biotech’s got you covered.

MIT Technology Review | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
I don’t remember much about Peppy, my first and only pet cat. I can conjure the sensation of stroking her while she purred, recall how she used to get stuck in trees (just like in cartoons!), and chuckle about the time she pooed in my baby sister’s cot. That’s about it. What I do vividly remember, though, is the day she went missing. I was 10 years old, and I searched everywhere for her, scouting nearby fields and woods until sunset. I made posters. We never found her. I was devastated. There was no way to get more time with Peppy, but almost 30 years later, pet owners are being offered an ever-expanding set of options to potentially prolong their years with their furry companions. The possibilities include drugs, cloning, and even cryonics. But is that a good thing? There’s certainly a market for pet preservation. And a growing number of biotech companies have their sights set on pet longevity. Loyal, a company that is currently working on three potential longevity drugs for dogs, is trialing one designed to improve metabolic health in around 1,300 pet dogs across the US. Others are testing dog drugs that aim to combat both age-related diseases, including conditions like obesity and osteoarthritis, and aging itself. There’s even a company focused on longevity medicines for domesticated cats. I don’t know how or when Peppy died. But if there had been a drug that might have protected her from diabetes or arthritis, there’s no doubt I would have tried to get my hands on it. That’s about where my enthusiasm for pet life extension ends, though. I’m far less interested in cloning, a relatively new option for people mourning a pet and seeking a replacement. In most cases, this technology involves taking the DNA of a pet, putting it into an egg cell from another animal of the same species, and growing the resulting embryo in a surrogate animal. It worked for Dolly the sheep, who was born the year before Peppy went missing. It has since worked for other mammals, including cattle, horses, and ferrets—and cats and dogs. In the US, a company called Viagen Pets offers to clone cats and dogs for $50,000 a go. This doesn’t result in an exact replica of the animal but a genetic twin. Still, there are lots of reasons why a person might want to clone a pet. On its website, Viagen explicitly offers customers “a way to hold on.” Lori Kogan, a professor of clinical sciences at Colorado State University who has studied human-animal relationships for decades, doesn’t have a problem with the practice. She points out that while we have well-established rituals for coping with the loss of a person—such as funeral services, time off work, and memorial gifts—we don’t really have the same for pets. As a result, pet owners can experience what is sometimes called “disenfranchised grief,” she says. Having their feelings go unrecognized can feel isolating. If cloning a pet brings a person peace, she says, “who are we to judge?” Fair enough. But I can’t help thinking that pet loss in childhood might be different. It’s often the first time a child confronts death. Losing Peppy was a formative experience, and distressing though it was, I like to think it helped prepare me for losses I experienced in adulthood. I wouldn’t want my own children to think that death is not final, and that individuals can be replicated or replaced. Or, for that matter, revived, which is the promise behind pet cryonics. For a fee, a handful of companies offer to cool dead pets to extremely low temperatures and store them for long periods of time—even indefinitely. The idea is that at some point in the future, we’ll have invented incredible technologies that will not only bring your pet back to life but also cure whatever it died of. Alcor, a cryonics facility in Arizona, is currently storing the remains of hundreds of pets as well as people. It even recently announced it had cooled a small marsupial—its first pet sugar glider. With cryonics, the chances of being reunited with your pet are bordering on zero. Even those who work in the field acknowledge that the stored people and animals are unlikely to be reanimated. As things stand, there is no way to really and truly bring any animal “back.” I miss Peppy. But given the chance, would I stick her in a vat of liquid nitrogen? Or get another cat to give birth to a genetically identical animal? No, I would not. Scientists hope it could be used to reconstruct a person’s inner thoughts, mental images, or even dreams. Could the drugs affect hair, nails, and vision? Generation Lab claims its drug combo can “stop the spread of aging” around the body. And it’s looking for influencers to give it a try. Novo Nordisk and Eli Lilly found the popular medicines reduce molecular markers of age. Discover special offers, top stories, upcoming events, and more. We’re having trouble saving your preferences.
