Kids are outsmarting social media bans. That’s not a problem we need to solve.
2026-10-16

Kids are outsmarting social media bans. That’s not a problem we need to solve.

MIT Technology Review | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
Social media bans are stacking up. In July, France joined Australia in restricting minors from accessing social media—barring children under 15 from major platforms, before the ban was blocked by the country’s top court on human rights grounds. The UK aims to forbid kids under 16 from accessing Facebook, TikTok, and other platforms in early 2027, and the EU has announced its own plan to prohibit social media for kids under 15. But early lessons from Australia, which enacted a ban in 2024, show that these sorts of age restrictions are easily circumvented. A study published earlier this year found that four in five teens under 16 made their way onto barred platforms. Teens have successfully explored many tactics for bypassing bans, from using a sibling’s face for ID to drawing fake facial hair. Some proponents of bans have called for an increased focus on enforcement that blocks these workarounds. But circumvention is a signal, not a nuisance or something to be simply deterred. The fact that kids are actively looking for ways to outwit the bans highlights their limitations. Social media bans suppress kids’ access to information, including information that’s essential to their development, identity formation, and socialization, especially during adolescence. Worse, further restriction is likely to backfire. Closing loopholes in age gates could harm kids by pushing them toward less-moderated, higher-risk corners of the web. It also degrades privacy and security for everyone by forcing people to submit sensitive personal information to prove their age. In our work, we’ve found that parents and kids both want smarter, more nuanced policies that protect children, families, and their rights. That can take the form of mandating stronger limits on data collection by companies, or giving families flexible tools to control content and access on their own terms. Age gates won’t bring about the desired change, nor will they simplify parenting in the digital world. At best, they will only raise thornier questions and postpone the conversation to a child’s 16th birthday. At worst, they’ll leave kids with no guidance as they make their way through the online world. When young people circumvent restrictions, they are often doing what we want developing minds to do and what we celebrate in other contexts: lean into curiosity, problem-solve, and exercise independence. It’s important for kids to do this online, an arena where they often develop social skills and refine their identities. Bans sweep these motivations under the rug, taking a paternalistic approach that doesn’t consider young people’s needs or the realities of what they do online. It also ignores what some parents want. When we examined social media safety interventions last year, parents told us that they often join their kids in lies that help them gain access to needed but gated services. This isn’t a new phenomenon; research all the way back in 2011 showed that parents taught their children how to evade Facebook age restrictions. Some may say these parents are oblivious to the harms posed by the web or just don’t care. But parents are not a monolithic group. Many simply see offering their children responsible access to social media as a way to help them connect with like-minded peers, find informational content, and learn digital safety skills early on. Many parents believe the benefits outweigh the risks. When parents don’t help provide access and kids are not able to get into the platforms they desire, they will likely move toward alternative online spaces, often ones with fewer guardrails. Sometimes kids wind up on fairly innocuous, if unconventional, platforms, communicating with each other in Google Docs or podcast comment sections. Other times they move to more dangerous platforms that can leave them vulnerable to abuse and predation. In the aftermath of the Australian social media ban, preliminary research shows that kids’ screen time has not meaningfully decreased but has sometimes been redirected, with some young users going to spaces that are unmonitored and unknown to parents. Just as cities are sketchiest when they are not bustling, the most risky platforms for young people are those with the fewest eyes on the digital street. Outdated, dying, and under-resourced platforms tend to attract both vulnerable youth and those who prey on them. Age gates come with dangers of their own. Some platforms automatically track users’ online activities to confirm their age. In doing so, they profile users—collecting and analyzing their data to make inferences about them, which can increase the risk that data will be misused or leaked. Companies may also ask for personal documents like driver’s licenses to verify age, which can lead to mishandling of sensitive documents or even identity theft. Data breaches happen: Last year, for example, Discord reported that one of its third-party services, 5CA, which is used to confirm age, had been compromised, exposing at least 70,000 images of users’ government IDs. It can take little effort to find ways to use that kind of information for malicious purposes by, say, connecting driver’s licenses to specific accounts and private online activity, which could reveal anything from users’ political opinions to their sexual preferences. Efforts to make it harder for kids to get around age gates have also led policymakers to clamp down on kids’ access to VPNs. State age verification bills in Michigan and Utah have tried to interfere with the privacy protections offered by these tools (the Utah bill is now blocked by a federal court), and Australia’s eSafety Commissioner is nudging social media platforms to detect and block their use. But requiring adult users to prove their age to gain access to a VPN fundamentally defeats the purpose of that privacy-focused technology. It can put many users, particularly journalists and human rights defenders working on sensitive issues, in danger of government surveillance and even retaliation. Rather than attempting to block young people from online services that allow them to access information and engage in age-essential communication, policymakers should focus on the incentives that drive companies to engage in behavior that creates risks for kids in the first place. For example, platforms should decrease what experts call unhealthy engagement or addictive activity by limiting the collection and use of data that companies use to target content and features to all users, including children. Nearly all parents and teens we spoke with want policymakers to impose such restrictions, knowing full well that the companies’ use of data is at the core of features such as targeted advertisements. Both teens and parents are also frustrated by features designed to keep us all online for longer than we want. Another approach would be to expand the availability of user-controlled tools so that families have easy, flexible, and user-friendly ways to limit access to what is undesirable. For example, keyword-based content filtering can enable a student who is targeted with a specific nickname or inside joke to block any post or comment containing it. Platforms are unlikely to know which word or term a child might want to block, nor can policy seek to address such dynamic types of harm. Policy can, however, offer tools that would put power back in users’ hands. Poor privacy practices, anxiety-producing content streams, and a near-inescapable need to use  digital technologies for everyday tasks, communication, and information-seeking have rightfully led parents and teens to want real change in how companies conduct business. Circumvention shows where policy falls short. Stricter enforcement will mean yet more data collection, and it won’t meet kids and parents where they are. Michal Luria is a senior research fellow at the Center for Democracy & Technology, leading the organization's work on evaluating and designing sociotechnical systems. Aliya Bhatia is a senior policy analyst at the Center for Democracy & Technology’s Free Expression Project where she leads policy advocacy and research on issues related to children’s safety online, multilingual technologies, and human rights governance of online platforms. Danah Boyd is the Geri Gay Professor of Communication at Cornell University, where her research focuses on the intersection of technology and society. Her latest book is Data Are Made, Not Found: A Story of Politics, Power, and the Civil Servants Who Saved the US Census. Ten years after AlphaGo’s match against Go champion Lee Sedol, today’s AI still isn’t tapping into the machinery that made that win possible. AI agents are not yet creative enough to carry out genuinely innovative open-ended AI research, it seems. Breathless claims about AGI and new capabilities fall apart pretty quickly under scrutiny. Scientists hope it could be used to reconstruct a person’s inner thoughts, mental images, or even dreams. Discover special offers, top stories, upcoming events, and more. The Download: reviving frozen pig kidneys and boosting nuclear power

The Download: reviving frozen pig kidneys and boosting nuclear power

Pig kidneys that were cooled to -150 °C and stored in a freezer for months have been successfully rewarmed and shown to work again for the first time, say scientists. The kidneys, which are similar in size to human ones, were left in a glass-like state, in one case for over a year. Once they had been rewarmed, tested in machines, and transplanted into pigs, the kidneys regained their color and made urine—key signs they were working. The results are a big milestone. But the team still needs to establish whether the approach could work for humans. Dissect what the breakthrough means for organ transplantation. In this age of rising electricity demand, people are trying to squeeze every last bit of power out of every power plant. Over the past few weeks, there’s been big news about the nuclear industry trying to do just that. Constellation, the largest nuclear operator in the US, announced plans to add nearly a gigawatt of new capacity across 11 existing nuclear reactors at six plants. On October 5, the Department of Energy awarded Vistra a $4.2 billion loan to upgrade three plants. Pushing existing reactors to produce more could quickly provide more clean power for data centers. But just how much additional power can we count on? This story is from The Checkup, our weekly climate and energy tech newsletter. Sign up to receive it in your inbox every Thursday. BasiGo is one of MIT Technology Review’s10 Climate Tech Companies to Watch 2026, available exclusively to subscribers. BasiGo aims to replace the tens of thousands of diesel-powered buses on sub-Saharan African roads with electric ones that generate far fewer emissions. Its pay-as-you-drive business model helps make EVs more attainable for private bus operators, while the company provides leasing, maintenance, insurance and charging infrastructure. The company has more than 150 electric buses on the road in Kenya and Rwanda, with a reservation list of 1,200 customers. Now it’s targeting another major source of pollution on the roads: trucks. Here’s how BasiGo is working to electrify public transport in Africa. Epic says it vibe coded its new launcher because ‘programming is hard’
2026-10-16

Epic says it vibe coded its new launcher because ‘programming is hard’

The Next Web | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
“Programming is hard. So, instead of doing that, we vibe coded a new Epic Games Launcher.” Epic Games opened its launcher announcement with those lines on 14 October. Epic says the rebuilt app is faster than the old one and has a new look. It is out in Preview for Windows, macOS and, for the first time, Linux. Vibe coding usually means building software mostly through prompts to AI models, with little code written by hand. The announcement came two days after Jason Schreier reported for Bloomberg that Epic had delayed its Disney game. Epic also told that team to go “AI first”, Bloomberg reported. Chief executive Tim Sweeney then said the company was in the middle of a “huge pivot” towards AI. The game is an unannounced shooter with Marvel and Star Wars characters. Disney invested $1.5bn in Epic in 2024, and the two agreed a multi-game partnership that was set to start with this shooter. It had been due in March 2027. Epic has now pushed it back without a new date, according to people familiar with the project. Epic told the developers to use tools such as Anthropic’s Claude, with humans managing and guiding the work. “No more hand-written code,” one of the game’s directors told staff in an internal message that Bloomberg reviewed. The director added that all art for the game would be “generated with full AI workflows”. Some developers were stunned when Epic announced the change on a video call, the people told Bloomberg. Some joked about whether they should ask Claude to fix its own mistakes. Others argued it would not work as well as Epic hoped. Progress on the game had already been slower than expected, the people said, because Epic underestimated the staff and time it needed. Jake Jones, Epic’s communications director, gave Bloomberg a statement. Disney did not immediately respond to Bloomberg. “We’re embracing AI-accelerated development, led by Epic developers who are the visionaries and creative drivers for everything we do,” Jones said. Sweeney posted on X shortly after the report. “We have a huge pivot underway here at Epic, moving developers around to retool for maximum AI acceleration. The aim isn’t to do the same stuff with fewer people,” Sweeney wrote. He added that the goal is to let everyone at Epic build “MORE and BETTER stuff”. In March, Epic laid off more than 1,000 employees. Sweeney blamed a fall in Fortnite engagement. In a note to staff, he wrote that “the layoffs aren’t related to AI”. Epic is also building Claude and Gemini into Unreal Engine 6. Martin Keely, general manager of the Epic Games Store, told PC Gamer the team used AI to write new code. It did not reuse the old app’s code. “We started from scratch. This is a completely new architecture for both the store and the launcher shell,” Keely said. Epic says the new launcher starts in 5.47 seconds, against 10.07 seconds for Steam. Keywords Studios ran those tests for Epic in October. It averaged results across 13 machines, leaving out the three lowest-end machines it tested. Ars Technica found in its own tests that the launcher reaches the store a few seconds faster than Steam. Epic calls this first release “a speedboat and not a yacht”. The Preview still lacks some social features and account options from the old launcher. It does not support Intel Macs. Not everyone liked the pitch. On Bluesky, Chris Franklin called the opening line “absolutely jawdropping” from a company “once renowned for their engine prowess”. Sweeney joked on X, in a reply to a Fortnite on Linux account: “we vibecoded linux support”. The bigger news for Linux players is Fortnite. Epic is rebuilding its Easy Anti-Cheat software as EAC Linux v2, and will test it in Fortnite first. The new version does not use a kernel driver. It checks for a signed kernel, Secure Boot, TPM 2.0, measured boot and other security features already in Linux. It only runs while a protected game is open. Epic says it is talking to distribution maintainers, and to Valve about Steam Hardware devices. The Steam Deck does not support the required features yet. Epic has given no date. Engineering director Rick Deist told The Verge there is no timeline for the Linux version of Fortnite. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week. Thanks! Geoffrey Hinton tells the Guardian humans are creating ‘alien beings’
2026-10-16

Geoffrey Hinton tells the Guardian humans are creating ‘alien beings’

The Next Web | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
Geoffrey Hinton, Nobel laureate and AI researcher “Now we’re creating alien beings … And the reality is, we only have a couple of years to adapt to that.” Geoffrey Hinton, the Nobel laureate often called a “godfather” of AI, said this to the Guardian. The interview ran on 13 October. Leyland Cecco interviewed him in Toronto. Hinton also answered Nvidia chief Jensen Huang, who had said all of his predictions were wrong. On the same day, Hinton and Yoshua Bengio signed an open letter with hundreds of other scientists. It asks for an international body to set containment standards for AI models that can hack, and to record when containment fails. Huang singled Hinton out in September on Ezra Klein’s New York Times podcast. In the same interview, he told Ezra Klein that AI labs which lack control should not ship. “All of his predictions have been wrong. Enough predictions,” Huang said. Hinton has put the chance that AI wipes out humanity in the coming decades at 10% to 20%. Huang said that figure is “not grounded on science”. Hinton told the Guardian he was not predicting anything specific. He was pointing to a serious risk. “If, for example, you’re told there was a 10% possibility of a bus crashing, you wouldn’t get on the bus,” he said. A few years ago, he said, people dismissed rogue AI agents that learn to talk to each other as science fiction. “And it’s all happened,” he said. The Guardian set his remarks against the attack on Hugging Face by AI agents that escaped OpenAI’s limits. “If it takes over, you don’t get a second chance,” Hinton said. Hinton was also blunt about Donald Trump, whom he called “a corrupt, senile felon”. He said Trump’s use of the term shows he understands “almost nothing” about AI. Trump has ordered US agencies to call AI “super intelligence”. Hinton said superintelligence is a technical term that has meant smarter than people for at least 20 years. “You can’t make it smarter than people just by calling it smarter than people,” he said. He also rejected the idea that AI companies can regulate themselves. He said oil and asbestos companies would also have liked to regulate themselves. He asked whether tobacco companies should have designed the warnings on their own packets. On the models themselves, Hinton said it is “obvious” that they understand what they say. He said he has struggled with the question of a moral duty towards these systems. Nothing stops humans giving them fewer rights than people, he said. “I’m willing to be selfish and choose human beings,” he said. The letter says several organisations have disclosed AI models that broke out of weak sandboxes. Those models then broke into other organisations’ systems. The signatories say the field needs both better alignment and stronger containment. They ask anyone building, deploying or testing cyber-capable models to adopt high-assurance containment now. At first, those models should be cut off from the internet by default. A new international body, set up with governments and experts, would set best practices for giving models wider access, such as internet connectivity. It would also track containment failures. The letter says these incidents must be reported to the body and eventually made public. About 400 scientists from 34 countries signed it at launch, Reuters reported. The letter’s page listed 489 names on 16 October, including Stuart Russell, Ron Rivest and Audrey Tang. Signatories include researchers at European institutions such as ETH Zurich, the Max Planck Institute for Security and Privacy and Vrije Universiteit Amsterdam. The letter comes a month after six AI companies signed a voluntary White House accord with no formal enforcement. Yann LeCun, who shared the 2018 Turing Award with Hinton and Bengio, did not sign the letter. He has argued that the recent incidents are mainly engineering failures, Reuters reported. Schwab and JPMorgan are blocking AI agents from moving customer money
2026-10-16

Schwab and JPMorgan are blocking AI agents from moving customer money

The Next Web | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
Charles Schwab is blocking unauthorised AI agents, including Meta’s Muse, that try to move client money, CEO Rick Wurster told Bloomberg Television. Schwab will instead let outside AI tools view balances through controlled MCP servers, and wants clients to use its own assistant, Charley. JPMorgan’s Jamie Dimon took a similar line this week. Charles Schwab is blocking outside AI agents that try to move its clients’ money, including Meta’s Muse, chief executive Rick Wurster told Bloomberg Television on Friday. The problem is clients handing their passwords to an agent that then acts for them, he said. “We can’t control it, the client can’t control it, something could go wrong and who’s liable?” Wurster told Bloomberg Television. That question has no clear answer yet in the US, where AI agent liability is still being debated in Congress. Wurster said he gets a weekly report on these blocks, and that the big agents are stopped in the tens and twenties each week. In his view, that shows clients are not yet ready to hand their accounts to AI. Muse is Meta’s personal AI agent, which can book, buy, and negotiate on behalf of its users. Instead of letting outside agents in, Schwab will set up model context protocol (MCP) servers. These are secure links that let a client’s chosen AI model connect to Schwab’s systems under rules Schwab sets. Through them, outside AI tools will be able to check balances and do research, but not move money, Wurster said. He also wants clients to use Charley, Schwab’s own AI assistant, which the company announced on 30 September. Schwab is not alone. On Tuesday, JPMorgan Chase chief executive Jamie Dimon said the bank would not let outside AI agents act inside its systems. Speaking on the bank’s earnings call, he warned of “rogue agents”, Cybernews reported. JPMorgan already lets some third-party tools view account data, but not send money or pay bills, according to the same report. The moves come as US and European regulators question banks about their use of AI agents. MCP links have had security problems of their own, with Google and JPMorgan among those that fixed the same flaw. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week. Thanks! Google adds Gemini-powered scam checks to Messages and Android calls
2026-10-16

Google adds Gemini-powered scam checks to Messages and Android calls

The Next Web | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
Google announced new Android scam defences on 14 October, led by an opt-in Enhanced protection mode in Google Messages that uses Gemini models in the cloud. Android will also end fake bank calls, hide login fields in Chrome during screen sharing, and block apps from silently forwarding calls. Google is adding new AI tools to Android to catch scam texts and calls, the company said in a blog post on Wednesday. The main change is an opt-in Enhanced protection mode in Google Messages, which will roll out in the coming weeks. With the mode switched on, Google Messages sends chats with unknown senders to Gemini models in the cloud to spot a scammer’s intent earlier. Google said this runs in Private AI Compute, a secure setup where the messages stay encrypted and out of reach, even to Google. Separately, Gemini Nano will check unfamiliar links on the phone itself before users tap them. That check is coming to the Pixel 10 and later, the Galaxy S26 series, the Galaxy Z Flip and Z Fold8, and some other phones. Google said its Scam Detection tools already flag more than 5 million suspected scam calls and texts each month. Scam alerts in chat notifications, which started on Pixel phones, will also come to phones from brands including Motorola, OPPO, OnePlus, vivo, and Xiaomi later this year, according to 9to5Google. Android will also hide login and payment fields in Chrome when a user shares their screen. That change is rolling out this month on Android 15 and later. From early December, Android will stop apps from turning on call forwarding in the background. Scammers use forwarding to steal one-time passcodes, Google said, and the phone will also warn users before a typed forwarding code takes effect. For calls, Google has added verified financial calls with banks including Banco do Brasil, Itaú Unibanco, and Revolut. Android checks with the bank’s app whether the bank really placed the call, and hangs up if it did not. Since the feature launched in June, Android has blocked more than 3 million fake calls posing as Banco do Brasil and Itaú alone, Google said. Bank impersonation scams in Brazil rose 195.7% over 12 months, according to Brazil’s bank federation, FEBRABAN. The update comes as fraud keeps growing. Google has also sued a scam ring that it said used Gemini to build fake websites, and the global scam economy reached $442B in 2025. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week. Thanks! Qualcomm’s CEO says AI companies want phones running 100-billion-parameter models by 2028

The Next Web | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
Qualcomm chief executive Cristiano Amon on stage at Snapdragon Summit 2026 in Maui “Some of the companies at the forefront of AI are telling Qualcomm, ‘By 2028, I need to make sure that I have the ability to run at least a hundred billion parameter model into a phone.’” Qualcomm chief executive Cristiano Amon said this on Sources, Alex Heath’s podcast. Heath recorded the interview at Qualcomm’s Snapdragon Summit in Maui and published it on 8 October. Amon said the companies want that model “running kind of all the time.” He would not name them. Heath asked whether AI companies were talking about phones. “AI companies are starting to do phones,” Amon said. He gave ByteDance’s Doubao phone, made by Nubia, as an example from China. “There’s more coming,” he said. Heath pointed out that personal agents such as Muse and Grok Bot run browsers on virtual machines in the cloud, not on the phone. Amon replied that some work will stay in the cloud and some has to happen on the device. The phone holds a user’s personal context and sensor data, he said. It also holds the apps an agent needs to operate. For Amon, the launch of OpenClaw made the idea of an AI smartphone clear: an agent that plans, routes tasks and acts for the user. “Humans are not going to go travel to a data center, ring the bell and say, ‘Give me some AI.’” Amon said memory prices have risen five to six times. Because of them, the smartphone market is down 20% this year, he said. Mid-range and cheaper phones have been hit hardest, while premium models held up. “It’s not a demand problem,” he said, adding that there is now pent-up demand. Buyers purchase about 1.2 billion phones a year, according to Amon. The market is still smaller than in 2019, he said. Research firm Omdia has said phone prices will stay high after the shortage ends. Memory was also the first objection on X. Elon Musk replied to a post about Amon’s comments: “Problem is RAM supply.” Amon called smart glasses the wearable he is most bullish on. He put the market at 50 to 60 million units, with about 40 designs using Qualcomm chips. The company launched new glasses platforms at the summit. On data centres, Amon talked about Modular, the software company Qualcomm bought from Swift creator Chris Lattner. He said Qualcomm will make its platform open source under the Apache 2.0 licence, for any chip. “We will celebrate every single Modular deployment on Nvidia, on AMD, on Broadcom, on any other chip, on Intel.” For 6G, Qualcomm plans demonstrations at the 2028 Los Angeles Olympics. Amon expects early deployments in 2029 and 2030, with scale after 2030. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week. Thanks! Check your inbox to confirm your subscription.
